# The Jingyou Era Scripture of the Great One's Propitious Responses

<image-desc>A vertical white title label is pasted on the left side of a dark blue, textured book cover. The label is enclosed in a double-line border and features seven large Traditional Chinese characters written in a calligraphic style. On the right edge of the page, the white stitching of a traditional Chinese thread binding is visible.</image-desc>

<term>Jingyou</term> <note>original: 景祐. The regnal era name for Emperor Renzong of the Northern Song Dynasty, specifically the years 1034–1038 CE. This indicates the text was likely compiled or officially sanctioned during this period.</note>, <term>Taiyi</term> <note>original: 太乙. Literally the "Great One." This refers to the Taiyi Shushu, one of the three highest forms of traditional Chinese divination (alongside Qimen Dunjia and Da Liuren), used primarily by the imperial court to predict major events like war, weather, and dynastic transitions.</note>, <term>Fuying</term> <note>original: 福應. "Propitious responses" or "auspicious resonances," referring to the favorable signs and blessings granted by the heavens in response to human actions.</note>, <term>Jing</term> <note>original: 經. A classic text or scripture, denoting a work of foundational importance or sacred authority.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of an 11th-century Chinese manual on the Taiyi system of divination, a sophisticated art used for state-level predictions. The title identifies it as an imperial-era scripture from the Song Dynasty focused on auspicious cosmic signs.</summary>
<keywords>Song Dynasty, Taiyi divination, Jingyou era, imperial astrology, Chinese classics, statecraft, cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This is the first page of the preface to the text. It establishes the historical and theological foundations for the divination system described in the following chapters.</meta>

# Preface to the Jingyou Era Scripture of the Great One’s Propitious Responses

The star of the <term>Supreme Ultimate</term> <note>original: 太極, Taiji. In Chinese cosmology, the ultimate source of all existence.</note> that shines with a bright red hue is called the <term>Great One</term> <note>original: 太乙, Taiyi. A supreme celestial deity and the name of a sophisticated system of imperial divination used to predict grand-scale events like wars and natural disasters.</note>. Its assistants are the <term>Five Emperors</term> <note>original: 五帝, Wudi. Deities governing the five directions and five elements.</note>, and its spirit truly presides over the <term>Nine Palaces</term> <note>original: 九宮, Jiugong. A cosmic grid or magic square used to track the movement of celestial energies.</note>. The Son of Heaven <note>The Emperor.</note> reveres it with solemnity; as recorded in the history of the *Spring and Autumn* period, one must offer sacrifices to it. 

It was the <term>Yellow Emperor</term> <note>original: 黃帝, Huangdi. The legendary ancestor of the Chinese people and purported inventor of many arts, including the calendar.</note> who first established the methods of the Divine Calendar. Later, Emperor Wu of the Han Dynasty established the <term>Shrine of Ganquan</term> <note>original: 甘泉之祠. An important imperial ritual site during the Han Dynasty.</note>. Drawing comparisons to the <term>Big Dipper</term> <note>original: 北斗, Beidou.</note> at Dujing, and with the ritual officials dressed in embroidered robes, they raised the <term>Spiritual Banner</term> <note>original: 靈旗, Lingqi. A ritual flag used to signal to the divine or lead an army.</note> to point toward the State of Dai, and offered ritual jade at Yunyang. From that time on, the deity of the Great One became the focus of prayers for military success.

According to the theories of the <term>Practitioners</term> <note>original: 方士, Fangshi. Specialists in occult arts, alchemy, and divination.</note>, the books of this system are based on the signs suspended in the heavens. They are not to be compared to mere agricultural stars used for farming. When blessings arise for the state, they are passed down through the generations. The lands where this star’s influence descends are called auspicious regions; the responses to one's prayers are as reliable as the <term>Great Tortoise</term> <note>original: 元龜, Yuangui. Refers to the ancient practice of tortoise-shell divination, known for its extreme accuracy.</note>. To the virtuous, its resonance is like an echo. Among all systems, this is the most significant, honored by a hundred kings.

I <note>The Emperor, likely Renzong of the Song Dynasty.</note> have succeeded to the great lineage and have heard of this perfect way of governance. In conducting the affairs of state, one must truly serve Heaven. In ruling the country and pacifying the people, I have never departed from the <term>Way</term> <note>original: 道, Dao.</note>. Moreover, as the Great One acts as an emissary for the Primordial Sovereign, bringing abundance from its residence in the <term>Purple Palace</term> <note>original: 紫宮, Zigong. The celestial center of the universe where the supreme deity resides.</note>, there are many voluminous writings and clear records of its lessons.

However, when searching through these categories, one finds them scattered among surviving volumes. In investigating their ultimate meaning, I found much that was cluttered and confusing. Attempting to grasp the truth was like "chasing the wind and catching shadows"—no easy task. Furthermore, despite the importance of the work, many previous efforts show signs of neglect. 

I intended to examine these texts personally, but found them difficult to use in their current state. Therefore, I commanded <term>Yang Weide</term> <note>A famous Song Dynasty astronomer and diviner.</note>, the <term>Washman of the Crown Prince</term> <note>original: 太子洗馬, Taizi Xima. A prestigious official title originally meaning "one who precedes the prince's horse," but later referring to high-ranking secretarial and library roles.</note> and Acting Director of the Bureau of Astronomy; Wang Li, the Spring Official; and Hanlin Astronomers Li Zili and He Zhan, to compile and edit these works at the <term>Hall of Cultivating Goodness</term> <note>original: 資善堂, Zishan Tang.</note> and submit them to the Palace Domestic Service.

<summary>This preface by the Emperor explains the cosmic importance of the Taiyi (Great One) divination system, tracing its history from the Yellow Emperor to the Han Dynasty, and announces a new imperial commission to reorganize the scattered and confusing texts into a definitive scripture.</summary>
<keywords>Taiyi, Great One, Emperor Renzong, Yang Weide, Song Dynasty, imperial divination, celestial bureaucracy, astronomy, Jingyou era</keywords>

--- Page 3 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface to the Jingyou Era Scripture of the Great One’s Propitious Responses. The previous page introduced the cosmic significance of the Great One (Taiyi) and its historical veneration by the Yellow Emperor and Han Dynasty rulers. This section details the compilation of the current text by imperial officials and its comprehensive scope.</meta>

Ren Chengliang, Deng Baoxin, and Huangfu Jihe—attendants of the Eastern Palace and supervisors of the <term>Imperial Pharmacy</term> <note>original: 御藥院, Yuyaoyuan. Despite the name, this office often handled confidential imperial matters and communications beyond medicine.</note>—together with **Yang Weide** <note>A famous astronomer and diviner of the Northern Song Dynasty who served as a high official in the Bureau of Astronomy.</note> and others, oversaw the progress of this labor. After several months of work, the book was completed. They requested that the various discourses on the <term>Great One</term> <note>original: 太乙, Taiyi. Referencing the celestial deity and the divination system associated with it.</note> be organized into a separate collection of ninety volumes. Consequently, it was titled the ***Jingyou Era Essential Summary of the Great One’s Propitious Responses***.

Looking back at the "Treatise on Literature" recorded by the ancient historian Ban Gu, there were once many schools of thought regarding the Great One: the *Great One’s Military Strategy*, the *Great One’s Miscellaneous Divination*, the *Great One’s Clouds and Rain*, the *Great One’s Yin and Yang*, and the *Great One’s Nine Palaces*. While many of these titles exist, the original chapters have mostly been lost to time. Of the records available today, only a few remain from the period following the *Yellow Emperor’s Great One Formula*.

However, this newly compiled collection begins its records from the <term>Great Beginning</term> <note>original: 上元, Shangyuan. The theoretical starting point of the cosmic calendar where all cycles align.</note> and categorizes the <term>Four Calculations</term> <note>original: 四計, Siji. These are the four primary mathematical cycles in Taiyi divination used to determine the state of the world.</note>. Within its pages, it clarifies the positions of the **Lord Base**, the **Minister Base**, and the **People Base** <note>These represent the three tiers of society—the Emperor, his officials, and the common people—within the celestial grid.</note>, and identifies the movements known as the **Great Wanderer** and the **Small Wanderer**. It describes the omens and auspicious signs across the **Nine Provinces** and the **Twelve Zodiacal Divisions**, and examines the methods of governance over cycles of three and twelve years.

The text is meticulously organized by category. Toward the end, it addresses the occurrence of unseasonable wind, frost, rain, and snow, as well as matters concerning war and famine. It includes the ritual methods practiced at the ancestral temples throughout the seasons, the protocols for offering sacrifices to avert calamity, and the techniques for directing armies and distinguishing favorable directions in the field. Emerging from these diverse subjects is a system that encompasses everything.

As for the teachings regarding the <term>Yang Nine and Hundred-Six</term> <note>original: 陽九百六. These refer to specific years of catastrophic drought and flood in ancient Chinese numerology.</note>, they exhaustively reveal the profound and subtle mysteries of the divine. These have been handed down as celestial laws for the observation of future generations. In ancient times, the sage kings **Yellow Emperor**, **Yao**, and **Shun** understood these cosmic transformations; they ensured the people did not grow weary, and by following the divine changes, they made life suitable for the populace. Thus, they received protection from Heaven, and their path was auspicious and without disadvantage. 

This current collection investigates the profound and reaches the distant; it reveals the subtle and clarifies the obscure. It can resolve the doubts of the world and bring the affairs of the empire to completion. Those who rule the myriad nations can...

<summary>This page describes the compilation of the 90-volume "Jingyou Era Essential Summary of the Great One’s Propitious Responses" by Yang Weide and other officials, detailing its comprehensive coverage of cosmic cycles, social structures, and military applications.</summary>
<keywords>Yang Weide, Taiyi, Great One Divination, Nine Provinces, Lord Base, Minister Base, People Base, cosmic cycles, military strategy, Jingyou Era, Northern Song Dynasty</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page concludes the preface of the Jingyou Era Essential Summary of the Great One’s Propitious Responses. It transitions from the historical and theoretical justification of the work to the formal attribution of the author, Yang Weide, and his numerous official titles at the Song court.</meta>

...Is it not truly to be esteemed? In the spare moments of my official duties, I have thus written this preface at the beginning of these scrolls.

->**Yang Weide**, Your servant, Grandee for Assisting Righteousness, Librarian of the Heir Apparent <note>original: 太子洗馬, Taizi Xima. A prestigious title for an official in the service of the Crown Prince; despite the literal characters for "washing horses," the role involved managing the prince's library and records.</note>, Concurrently Director of the Spring Office <note>The Spring Office (Chunguan) was the department within the Bureau of Astronomy responsible for celestial observations and calendar management.</note>, Acting Supervisor of the Directorate [of Astronomy], Concurrently Supervisor of Calendar <unclear>Books</unclear>, Pillar of State, and Recipient of the Purple and Gold Fish Pouch <note>original: 賜紫金魚袋, ci zijin yudai. A high-ranking honorific indicating the right to wear imperial purple and carry a decorative fish-shaped pouch as an insignia of rank.</note>, having received the<-

->**Imperial Decree** to compile and collect [this work].<-

<summary>This page concludes the preface to the Jingyou Era Essential Summary, concluding with the author’s formal signature. It identifies Yang Weide, a prominent Song dynasty astronomer, as the compiler acting under direct imperial command.</summary>
<keywords>Yang Weide, Northern Song Dynasty, Imperial Decree, Bureau of Astronomy, Taiyi, official titles, preface</keywords>

# Volume 1 of the Scripture of Blessed Responses of the Supreme One from the Jingyou Era

## Section 1: Calculating the Accumulated Years of the Supreme One

<note>The "Supreme One" (Taiyi) refers to a high-level form of divination used by the imperial court to predict large-scale events like wars, famines, or the fate of dynasties based on cosmic cycles.</note>

Since the <term>Era-Establishing Superior Epoch</term><note>The "Superior Epoch" (Shangyuan) is a theoretical starting point in Chinese astronomy where all celestial cycles—the sun, moon, and planets—reset to zero or perfectly align.</note> in the year of the *Jiazi* <note>The first year of the 60-year cycle.</note> until the present year, the first year of the **Jingyou** reign of the **Great Song Dynasty** (the *Jiaxu* year), the accumulated total is **10,154,950 years**.

From that same ancient Superior Epoch *Jiazi* until the present year, the 17th year of the **Hongzhi** reign of the **Great Ming Dynasty** (the *Jiazi* year) <note>1504 CE</note>, the total count is **10,155,420 years**.

Furthermore, calculating up to the first year of the **Taiding** reign of the **Great Yuan Dynasty** (the *Jiazi* year) <note>1324 CE</note>, the accumulated total was **10,155,240 years**. At this point, if one divides the total by 360 <note>The number of degrees in a circle, representing a full cosmic cycle</note>, the number is exhausted except for a remainder of one. Therefore, from this point forward, the first year of the **Taiding** reign (1324 CE) is treated as the new **Superior Epoch** for calculations.

As of the 17th year of the **Hongzhi** reign of the **Great Ming Dynasty**, the count is **180 years** <note>This "180" is the 1504 date minus the 1324 reset date</note>. In the future, add one year for each passing year; if investigating the past, subtract one year for each year prior.

At the original ancient <term>Era-Establishing Superior Epoch</term>, during the 11th lunar month <note>the month of the Winter Solstice</note>, on the first day of the month at midnight, the Winter Solstice occurred. At that precise moment, the various astronomical cycles were in total alignment. The month, day, and hour of the **Supreme One** (Taiyi) all corresponded to the first cycle of the Superior Epoch. This is why it is called the "Era-Establishing" calculation.

<summary>This page introduces the Taiyi (Supreme One) divination system by establishing its chronological starting point, providing the massive number of years passed since the cosmic beginning and updating the "reset" date to the year 1324 CE.</summary>
<keywords>Taiyi, Supreme One, Jingyou era, Hongzhi era, Taiding era, Superior Epoch, Chinese chronology, cosmic cycles, Jiazi, astronomical alignment</keywords>

## Section 2: The General Method for Calculating the Four Computations of the Supreme One

To perform these calculations, divide the accumulated years by 360, which constitutes the **Cycle Method of the Supreme One**. Any remainder that does not complete a full 360-year cycle is then applied to the **Five Zi Eras** <note>Also known as the "Five Rats." In the Chinese sexagenary cycle, years are named using "Heavenly Stems" and "Earthly Branches." The "Zi" or Rat branch marks the beginning of these specific 72-year sub-cycles.</note>. These are calculated using a **Small Cycle** of 72 years.

The five eras are:
1.  The First Era: *Jiazi* <note>The Wood Rat era</note>
2.  The Second Era: *Bingzi* <note>The Fire Rat era</note>
3.  The Third Era: *Wuzi* <note>The Earth Rat era</note>
4.  The Fourth Era: *Gengzi* <note>The Metal Rat era</note>
5.  The Fifth Era: *Renzi* <note>The Water Rat era</note>

Each individual era lasts for **72 years**; all five eras together total **360 years**.

### Astronomical Constants for Calculation

| Term | Value | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Epoch Method** | 60 | The basic 60-year cycle of the Chinese calendar. |
| <term>Solar Year Value</term> | 3,835,046.25 parts | <note>This represents the length of a tropical year (approx. 365.2425 days) expressed in "parts" for high-precision calculation.</note> |
| **Annual Excess** | 55,068 parts | The amount by which a solar year exceeds the standard lunar year. |
| **Day Constant** | 10,500 parts | The number of units that make up one full day. |
| <term>Synodic Month Value</term> | 300,071.5265 parts | The precise value of a lunar month from new moon to new moon. |
| <term>Synodic Month Duration</term> | 29 days, 5,571.24 parts | The average length of a lunar month. |
| <term>Solar Term Duration</term> | 15 days, 2,294.5 parts | <note>The "Qi" or solar term represents 1/24th of the solar year, used to track seasons.</note> |
| **Double-Hour Method** | 875 parts | The duration of one traditional Chinese "hour" (which is 2 modern hours). |
| **Half-Hour Method** | 437.5 parts | One modern hour. |
| **Mark (Ke) Method** | 105 parts | <note>A "Ke" is a unit of time often compared to a quarter-hour, though its value varied across different dynasties.</note> |

## Section 3: Determining the Location of the Great Year

<note>The "Great Year" (Taisui) refers to the position of the planet Jupiter, which was personified as a powerful deity in Chinese astrology. Knowing its location was essential for determining which directions were auspicious or forbidden for construction and travel.</note>

<summary>This page details the mathematical constants and sub-cycles (the Five Zi Eras) used in the Taiyi system to divide time and track celestial bodies. It provides the specific "parts" or denominators used for high-precision astronomical calculations equivalent to 365.2425 days per year.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of the Taiyi (Supreme One) calendrical system. The previous page established the "Accumulated Years" and the high-level constants like the 360-year cycle and the 72-year sub-cycles. This section provides the specific mathematical steps to determine the position of the "Tai-sui" (Great Age) and the movement of the "Taiyi" spirit through the celestial palaces.</meta>

Take the desired **Accumulated Years** <note>The total number of years passed since the cosmic starting point, the Superior Epoch.</note> and divide them by the **Epoch Method** of 60. The remainder that is left over should be counted starting from the *Jiazi* year <note>The first year of the 60-year cycle</note>. Counting forward from this point reveals the specific **Earthly Branch** <note>One of the twelve signs—Rat, Ox, Tiger, etc.—used to track time and direction in ancient China</note> where the <term>Tai-sui</term> <note>The "Great Age" or "Grand Commandant," a celestial spirit associated with the orbit of Jupiter and used as a primary timekeeper.</note> is currently located.

## Section 4: Determining the Method for Entering the Era and the Pattern

Take the **Accumulated Years** from the *Jiazi* of the **Superior Epoch** down to the desired year. Divide this by the **Cycle Method** of 360. The remainder is known as the **Era Remainder**. 

Divide this remainder by the **Epoch Rule** of 60; the resulting whole number is the **Era Number**. Any remaining years that do not complete a full 60 are the **Years Since Entering the Era**. To find the **Taiyi Era Year Number**, count the "Era Number" starting from the first one.

Furthermore, take the **Cycle Remainder** and divide it by the **Origin Method** of 72. This gives the **Origin Number**. The remainder from this calculation provides the **Pattern Entrance Number** <note>original: "入局數" (ruju shu). In Taiyi divination, a "Pattern" or "Bureau" refers to one of the 72 specific configurations of the cosmic board.</note>.

## Section 5: Determining the Movement of Taiyi through the Palaces

Take the **Pattern Entrance Number** for the desired year and divide it by the **Taiyi Small Cycle Method** of 24. For the remainder, divide by three to determine the **Palace Number**. Any remainder left after this division represents the **Years Since Entering the Palace**. 

To find the location, count the "Palace Number" starting from **Palace 1**. Follow the sequence through the **Eight Palaces** <note>The Eight Palaces correspond to the eight directions of the compass (North, Northeast, East, etc.).</note> in their forward order. Note that the **Middle Palace 5** is skipped and not entered. Counting outwards in this manner reveals the specific Palace where the **Taiyi** resides. The year count is also determined by counting outwards to see the current year's position.

## Section 6: Determining the Location of the Celestial Eye for Years, Months, and Days under the Yang Hidden Pattern

Take the **Pattern Entrance Number** for the desired year and divide it by the **Cycle Method** of 18. For any remainder left over, begin counting from the spirit **Martial Virtue** <note>original: "武德" (Wude), one of the sixteen transformative spirits in Taiyi cosmology</note> and proceed in a forward direction through the **Sixteen Spirits**. If you encounter the spirits **Hidden Virtue** <note>original: "陰德" (Yinde)</note> or **Great Martial** <note>original: "大武" (Dawu)</note>, stay at that position for an extra count <note>This refers to a "double stay" or "heavy retention" (zhongliu) in the calculation where the count pauses on a specific spiritual station.</note>. Counting outward in this way reveals the location of the <term>Heavenly Eye</term> <note>The "Heavenly Eye" (Tianmu) acts as a celestial scout or indicator of hidden influences in Taiyi divination.</note>.

## Section 7: Determining the Position of the Calculation Spirit

Take the **Taiyi Pattern Entrance Number** for the desired year and divide it by the **Calculation Cycle Method** of 12. For any remainder, begin counting from the **Tiger** station <note>The "Yin" branch</note> and proceed in a reverse direction through the twelve **Earthly Branches**. Counting outward in this way reveals the location of the <term>Calculation Spirit</term> <note>The "Calculation Spirit" (Jishen) is a secondary spirit used to verify the movements of the Taiyi.</note>. <gloss>The preceding steps calculated the yearly cycle.</gloss>

## Section 8: Determining the Monthly Calculation for Entering the Era and the Pattern

Take the **Accumulated Years** and multiply them by twelve; this gives the total number of months. To this, add the number of months that have passed in the current year, starting from the eleventh month <note>The "Tian-zheng" or "Heavenly Upright" month, which corresponds to the Winter Solstice and the start of the astronomical year.</note>, to find the total **Accumulated Months**. 

Divide this by the **Taiyi Universal Era Method** of 360. Any remainder enters the **Five-Zi Origin Method** <note>A system of categorizing cycles based on the five elemental "Zi" (Rat) years.</note>. Divide that result by 72; the remaining number is the **Monthly Pattern Entrance Number**.

## Section 9: Determining the Position of the Monthly Taiyi

Take the **Monthly Pattern Entrance Number** and divide it by the **Taiyi Small Cycle Method** of 24. For the remainder, divide by three to determine the **Palace Number**. If the remainder is less than three, it counts as "entering the palace." 

Starting from **Palace 1**, move in a forward direction through the **Eight Palaces**, skipping over the **Central Five Palace** <note>Taiyi divination uses a nine-palace grid (the Lo Shu), but the central palace is often bypassed as a "void" or "neutral" space in these calculations.</note>. Counting outward in this way reveals the position of the **Monthly Calculation**.

<note>This section defines key technical terms used in the manuscript:
*   **Taiyi (Supreme One):** The personification of the North Star and the governing cosmic force.
*   **Heavenly Eye (Tianmu):** A symbolic point representing visual or spiritual insight into a situation.
*   **Martial Virtue (Wude):** One of the sixteen directional spirits.
*   **Calculation Spirit (Jishen):** A shadow-point used to balance the Taiyi's position.
*   **Sixteen Spirits:** A specific set of deities or points surrounding the Taiyi board.
*   **Hidden Virtue (Yinde):** A spiritual station where the calculation cycle pauses.
*   **Great Martial (Dawu):** A spiritual station where the calculation cycle pauses.
*   **Accumulated Years/Months:** Total elapsed time from a fixed mythological starting point.
*   **Five-Zi Origin Method:** A sub-cycle within the 360-year Great Cycle.</note>

### 10. Determining the Day and Time of the Winter Solstice

Take the **Accumulated Years** <note>original: 距所求積年 (ju suo qiu ji nian); the number of years passed since the "Great Epoch" or the beginning of the current cosmic cycle.</note> and multiply them by the **Yearly Constant** <note>original: 歲實 (suishi); the precise length of a solar year used in the calendar, typically around 365.24 days.</note>. This result is the **Solar Accumulation Units** for the year's Winter Solstice. Divide this by the **Day Divisor** of 10,500 to find the **Accumulated Days**. Any remaining amount is the **Fractional Day Remainder** <note>original: 小餘 (xiaoyu); the time of day expressed as a fraction of a full day.</note>.

Divide the **Accumulated Days** by the **Cycle Constant** of 60. The remainder is the **Day Number** <note>original: 大餘 (dayu)</note>. Count forward from the day **Jiazi** <note>The first day of the sixty-day sexagenary cycle.</note>; the day reached is the specific day and time of the **Winter Solstice** for the desired year.

To find the next solar term, add the **Term Constant** <note>original: 氣策 (qice)</note> of 15 days and a fractional remainder of 2,294.5 units. If the fractional units reach the **Day Divisor** (10,500), add one to the day count. If the days exceed 60, discard 60 and count the remainder from Jiazi to find the day and time of the next solar term. To find the **Summer Solstice**, take the Winter Solstice day and fractional remainder and add 182 days and 6,534 units. If the units exceed a full day, adjust the count accordingly. To name the day, follow the same method as described above.

### 11. Determining the Day and Time of the Average New Moon for the Eleventh Month

Take the **Solar Accumulation Units** for the Winter Solstice and divide them by the **Lunar Constant** <note>original: 朔實 (shuoshi); the length of a synodic month, or the time between two new moons.</note>. The remainder is the **Intercalary Remainder** <note>original: 閏餘 (runyu); the difference between the solar year and the lunar year, used to determine when to add an extra month.</note>. Then, take the Winter Solstice Accumulation Units and subtract...

Studio of the Sincere Pavilion and Mountain Retreat <note>original: 誠閣山房 (Chengge Shanfang); the studio name of the owner or printer of this manuscript.</note>

The remaining value represents the **Mean New Moon Accumulated Units** <note>original: 經朔積分 (jingshuo jifen); the total numerical value representing the time elapsed until the average new moon of the eleventh month.</note> for the eleventh month of that year. Divide these units by the **Day Divisor** of 10,500 to obtain the number of days. Any remainder is the **Fractional Day Remainder** <note>original: 小餘 (xiaoyu); the fraction of the day that has passed at the moment of the new moon.</note>.

Divide the **Accumulated Days** by the **Cycle Constant** of 60. The remainder determines the day within the sexagenary cycle. Count forward starting from the day **Jiazi** <note>The first day of the sixty-day cycle.</note>; the day reached is the specific day and time of the **Mean New Moon of the eleventh month** for the desired year. 

To find the date for any other day, simply add the number of days elapsed to this benchmark. If the fractional units reach the **Day Divisor** (10,500), advance the day count by one to determine the final fixed date and time.

->**12. Calculating the Daily Taiyi**<-

Take the **Accumulated Days** and divide them by the **Taiyi Full Cycle Constant** of 360. The remainder is the **Cycle Remainder**. Divide this remainder by the **Cycle Constant** of 60; the resulting quotient is the number of completed cycles, and the remainder is the **Days Elapsed since entering the current Cycle**. Count forward from one; the resulting number is the day’s position within the current cycle. 

Additionally, take the **Cycle Remainder** and divide it by the **Epoch Constant** of 72. The remainder identifies the specific **Epoch Phase** <note>original: 元局 (yuanju); one of the five divisions of the 360-day Taiyi cycle, used to determine which "Bureau" or mathematical chart is currently in effect.</note>.

->**13. Determining the Location of the Daily Taiyi**<-

Take the **Day Number** within the current **Epoch Phase** and divide it by the **Taiyi Lesser Cycle Constant** of 24. Divide the remainder by three to determine the **Palace Number**. Any remainder represents the number of **Days spent within that Palace**. 

Count starting from the First Palace and proceed sequentially through the Eight Palaces <note>The Eight Palaces represent the eight compass directions; the Taiyi "resides" in one for three days before moving.</note>; the palace reached is the current location of the **Daily Taiyi**. This logic follows the same rules as the calculations for years and months.

## 14. Calculating the Time of Arrival for the Winter and Summer Solstices

Take the **Fractional Day Remainder** <note>original: 小餘 (xiaoyu); the decimal fraction of the day that has passed since midnight.</note> of the Winter or Summer Solstice and add the **Half Double-Hour Constant** of 437.5 units. Then, divide the result by the **Double-Hour Constant** of 875; the quotient is the number of **Double-Hours** <note>original: 辰 (chen); a traditional unit of time equal to two modern hours.</note>. If there is a remainder, divide it by the **Quarter Constant** of 105 to find the number of **Quarters** <note>original: 刻 (ke); a traditional unit often translated as "marks" or "quarters," though there were roughly 8.33 of these in one double-hour in this system.</note>. Any further remainder represents the **Minutes**.

When counting the quarters, begin the count from the **Start of the Zi Double-Hour** <note>11:00 PM to 1:00 AM</note>. This process yields the specific double-hour, quarter, and minute of the solstice's arrival. If seeking the arrival times for other **Solar Terms** <note>The 24 divisions of the solar year.</note>, follow this same procedure.

## 15. Calculating the Taiyi Entry into the Cycle-Phase for the Yang Hidden Period after the Winter Solstice

Take the **Accumulated Days** of the desired period and multiply them by 12. This yields the total **Accumulated Double-Hours** up to midnight of the night preceding the target day. To this, add the specific number of double-hours elapsed on the target day. 

Divide this total by the **Taiyi Full Cycle Constant** of 360. The remainder is the **Cycle Remainder**. Divide this remainder by the **Cycle Constant** of 60. The remainder of this division represents the number of double-hours elapsed since entering the current **Cycle** <note>original: 紀 (ji); a 60-unit period used to track time and deity positions.</note>. 

Count the cycles starting from the **First Cycle** to find the current **Entry Cycle Number**. Once the cycle count is established, return to the **Cycle Remainder** and divide it by the **Epoch Constant** of 72. The remaining value identifies the specific **Epoch Phase** <note>original: 元局 (yuanju); a specific configuration within the Taiyi divinatory system.</note> being entered.

## 16. Calculating the Taiyi’s Location for the Yang Hidden Period after the Winter Solstice

Take the value representing the **Entry Phase for the Target Time** and divide it by the **Taiyi Minor Cycle Constant** of 24. Any remainder should be reduced by a factor of three to determine...

<meta>This page continues the mathematical instructions for determining the position of the Hourly Taiyi deity. This page completes that calculation and moves into the instructions for the Yin Hidden period (Summer Solstice to Winter Solstice), which follows a reverse numerical order.</meta>

The remainder that does not fill a full palace is the number of double-hours elapsed since entering that palace. For the palace count, start the assignment from Palace 1 and proceed in forward order through the eight palaces. Counting out from the remainder <note>original: 筭外 (suanwai); a technical term in ancient Chinese mathematics meaning to count through the units on a counting board until the final position is reached.</note>, you will find the palace where the **Hourly Taiyi** <term>Hourly Taiyi: The specific position of the Taiyi deity calculated for a two-hour window (double-hour), used in horary divination.</term> is located for that specific time.

->**17. Calculating the Hourly Taiyi’s Entry into the Cycle-Phase for the Yin Hidden Period after the Summer Solstice**<-

Take the **Accumulated Days** and the **Fractional Day Remainder** <note>original: 小餘 (xiaoyu); the decimal remainder representing the portion of a day passed since midnight.</note> of the Summer Solstice and add 182 days and a fractional remainder of 6,534. If the fractional remainder exceeds the **Day-Denominator** <note>The total number of units in a full day for this calendar system.</note>, carry it over to the day count. This yields the accumulated days and fractions for the Summer Solstice. 

Next, add the specific days and **Double-Hours** <term>Double-Hour: (辰, chen) a traditional unit of time equal to two modern hours.</term> of the desired time to be calculated. This provides the **Total Accumulated Time Units**. Multiply this total by 12 and then add the specific double-hour of the time in question. This resulting sum is the **Accumulated Hourly Total**. 

Divide this sum by the **Full Cycle Constant** of 360. For any remainder, divide it again by 60. The remainder that does not fill 60 is the number of double-hours elapsed since entering the current **Cycle** <note>original: 紀 (ji); a major temporal division in the Taiyi system.</note>. Assign the cycle count starting from the First Cycle; the result outside the calculation is the **Cycle Number**. 

Furthermore, take the remainder from the Full Cycle and divide it by the **Origin Constant** of 72. Any remainder that does not fill 72 indicates the specific **Phase** <note>original: 局 (ju); a "game" or "set-up" of the celestial board, representing a specific configuration of deities and energies.</note> entered.

->**18. Calculating the Location of the Hourly Taiyi during the Yin Hidden Period after the Summer Solstice**<-

Take the number of the **Phase** entered for the desired time and reduce it using the **Taiyi Lesser Cycle Constant** of 24. For any remainder, divide it by 3 to determine the **Palace Time-Units**. 

For the palace count, start the assignment from Palace 9 and proceed in **reverse order** through the eight palaces, skipping the **Central Five** <note>In the Yin Hidden period, the Taiyi deity moves "backward" through the Nine Palaces (9, 8, 7, 6, 4, 3, 2, 1), bypassing the center palace (5).</note>. Counting out from the result will yield the palace where the Hourly Taiyi is located.

It focuses on converting calendar dates into specific "Phases" and "Palaces" through division by traditional astronomical constants.</summary>

## 19. Calculating the Heavenly Eye for the Two Hidden Phases

To find the **Heavenly Eye** <term>Heavenly Eye: (天目, Tianmu) Also known as the 'Civilian Assistant,' this is a moving point in the Taiyi cosmic model representing the 'eye' of the Great One, used to determine favorable directions for civilian affairs.</term> during the **Yang Hidden Phase** <note>The 'Yang Dun' period occurs between the Winter Solstice and the Summer Solstice.</note>, use the "Entry into the Cycle" method and divide the total by 18. For the remainder that does not complete a full set of eighteen, begin the assignment at the position of **Wude** <note>original: 武德 (Martial Virtue); a specific coordinate on the Taiyi board.</note> and proceed in forward order. Upon reaching the positions of **Yinde** <note>阴德 (Hidden Virtue)</note> or **Dawu** <note>大武 (Great Martial)</note>, you must double-count that single position <note>original: 重留一算 (zhongliu yisuan); a technical instruction to stay on a position for two counts instead of one as you pass through.</note>. Counting through to the end of the remainder will reveal the location of the Heavenly Eye.

To find the Heavenly Eye during the **Yin Hidden Phase** <note>The 'Yin Dun' period occurs between the Summer Solstice and the Winter Solstice.</note>, take the "Entry into the Cycle" number and divide it by 18. For the remainder, begin the assignment at the position of **Lü-shen** <note>吕申; a specific directional coordinate used as a starting point during the Yin phase.</note> and proceed in forward order. When you encounter the positions of **Dahao** <note>大昊 (Great Vastness)</note> or **Hede** <note>和德 (Harmonious Virtue)</note>, double-count that single position. Counting through to the end of the remainder will reveal the location of the Heavenly Eye.

---

## 20. Method for the Calculating Spirit’s Entry into the Cycle

To find the **Calculating Spirit** <term>Calculating Spirit: (計神, Jishen) Also known as the 'Military Assistant,' this point is the counterpart to the Heavenly Eye and is used primarily for military and strategic calculations.</term>, take the "Entry into the Cycle" number and divide it by 12. For the remainder, begin the assignment at the position of **Wude** and proceed in reverse order through the **Twelve Branches** <term>Twelve Branches: (十二辰, shi'er chen) The twelve signs—Rat, Ox, Tiger, etc.—that correspond to specific hours and compass directions.</term>. Counting out the remainder will reveal the location of the Calculating Spirit for the Yin Hidden Phase. <gloss>In the Year of the Rat, it is fully within the center.</gloss>

Furthermore, take the "Entry into the Cycle" number and divide it by 12. For the remainder, begin the assignment at the position of **Lü-shen** and proceed in reverse order through the Twelve Branches. Counting out the remainder will reveal the location of the Calculating Spirit for the Yang Hidden Phase.

---

## 21. Calculating the Eight Gates for the Taiyi Yearly Cycle

<summary>This page details the mathematical procedures for locating the Heavenly Eye (Tianmu) and the Calculating Spirit (Jishen), two essential variables in the Taiyi divination system used to assess civilian and military fortunes.</summary>
<keywords>Heavenly Eye, Calculating Spirit, Yang Hidden Phase, Yin Hidden Phase, Wude, Lü-shen, Twelve Branches, Taiyi divination</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Taiyi divination, moving from the placement of the Heavenly Eye to the calculation of the "Eight Gates" (Ba Men) that govern specific spans of time and directional influences. It provides a chronological example using historical Song Dynasty eras to demonstrate the 30-year rotation of these Gates.</meta>

<margin>Tan Zhuo's Mountain Abode</margin> <note>original: 談𤥨山居 (Tan Zhuo Shan Ju); likely an ownership mark or the name of the studio where this manuscript was held or transcribed.</note>

To determine the position, take the accumulated years from the **Upper Origin Jiazi** <term>Upper Origin Jiazi: (上元甲子) The foundational starting point of the 3,600-year grand cycle in Chinese horology, used as "Year Zero" for calculations.</term> to the desired year. Divide this total by the **Eight Gates Cycle Law** of 240. For the remainder that does not reach 240, divide it by 30 to determine the number of the **Directing Gate** <term>Directing Gate: (直使之門, Zhushi zhi Men) The specific "Gate" or cosmic portal that is "in office" or dominant for a specific period.</term>. Any further remainder indicates the specific year within the entry of that Directing Gate. The sequence begins with the **Open Gate** and proceeds in forward order; the resulting position is the location of the Directing Gate. <gloss>The calculations for months, days, and hours all follow this same example.</gloss>

For example:
In the 2nd year of the **Qiande** era of the Song Dynasty <note>964 AD</note>, which was a *Jiazi* year and the first cycle of the Taiyi, the **Open Gate** <note>original: 開門 (Kaimen); associated with new beginnings and official business.</note> was the Directing Gate.
Thirty years later, in the 5th year of the **Chunhua** era <note>994 AD</note>, also a *Jiazi* year, the **Rest Gate** <note>original: 休門 (Xiumen); associated with retirement, healing, and leisure.</note> became the Directing Gate.
Thirty years later, in the 2nd year of the **Tiansheng** era <note>1024 AD</note>, a *Jiazi* year, the **Life Gate** <note>original: 生門 (Shengmen); associated with growth, profit, and vitality.</note> became the Directing Gate.
Thirty years later, in the 1st year of the **Zhihe** era <note>1054 AD</note>, a *Jiawu* year, the **Injury Gate** <note>original: 傷門 (Shangmen); associated with hunting, debt collection, or physical harm.</note> became the Directing Gate.
Thirty years later, in the 7th year of the **Yuanfeng** era <note>1084 AD</note>, a *Jiazi* year, the **Obstruction Gate** <note>original: 杜門 (Dumen); associated with concealment, evasion, and closing off.</note> became the Directing Gate. 
The remaining gates follow this same pattern. <gloss>The same logic applies to years, months, days, and hours.</gloss>

---

### 22. Calculating the Eight Gates for the Two Hidden Phases by the Hour

In the **Yang Hidden Phase** <note>The half of the year between the Winter and Summer Solstices.</note>, the four active gates are: **Open, Life, Surprise, and Rest.**
In the **Yin Hidden Phase** <note>The half of the year between the Summer and Winter Solstices.</note>, the four active gates are: **Obstruction, Death, Injury, and Scenery.**

Using the accumulated hour counts from either the Winter or Summer Solstice, divide the total by 120. For the remainder, divide by 30. For any amount less than 30, assign the count starting from the **Open Gate**; the result is the Directing Gate for that specific hour.

End of Volume One of the *Classic of the Great One's Blessed Response*

# Classic of the Great One's Blessed Response, Volume 2

## Section 23: Explaining the Governance of the Great One

The traditions say that the Great One <note>original: 太乙 (Taiyi); the supreme celestial deity in this system of divination, representing the unity of the cosmos and the pivot of the stars.</note> is the spirit of Heaven and Earth. He commands the Sixteen Spirits <term>Sixteen Spirits: (十六神, Shiliu Shen) A group of celestial entities in Taiyi cosmography that govern specific directions and seasonal transitions.</term> to monitor the harms that can befall a nation, such as wind, rain, floods, droughts, warfare, famine, and plagues. He constantly examines and manages the affairs of the world below. Following the order of the Eight Palaces, he shifts his position once every three years.

In the first year, he governs Heaven. He aligns the Sun, the Moon, and the stars, ensuring that the Seven Luminaries <term>Seven Luminaries: (七曜, Qiyao) The Sun, the Moon, and the five visible planets—Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</term> do not deviate from their proper degrees. This illuminates the portion of the world where the "Way of Heaven" descends; if a ruler receives this Way, they will obtain a favorable assessment for their governance of Heaven.

In the second year, he governs Earth. He harmonizes the four seasonal energies and the eight winds, ensuring that rain and snow do not miss their proper seasons. This illuminates the portion where the "Way of Earth" descends. During this time, one must not undertake major construction or earthworks. If the people's farming and harvesting are all kept in harmony, the ruler obtains a favorable assessment for the governance of Earth.

In the third year, he governs Humanity <note>The text reads "第三人" (third person), but in context, it refers to the third year of the cycle governing the realm of Man.</note>, presiding over nations that lack the "Way." If a land where ritual should dwell instead takes up spears and shields, indulging in invasions and conquests, then warfare, death, floods, droughts, famine, and displacement are deployed to carry out his punishments. However, if a ruler upholds the Way, pacifies the people, ceases warfare, and forbids violence, then the Five Planets will not deviate from their paths and the four classes of people <note>The traditional hierarchy of scholars, farmers, artisans, and merchants.</note> will not lose their livelihoods. Thus, the Classic says: "In the place where the Great One descends, there is prosperity if the Way is present, but calamity if the Way is absent."

The Lechan <term>Lechan: (樂產) A technical term in Taiyi astrology referring to a specific cyclical influence or baleful star; when it is in a negative position, it triggers massive environmental disasters.</term> can cause the Sun and Moon to lose their light, the Five Planets to drift from their courses, and "bushy stars" <note>original: 彗孛 (Huibei); an ancient term for comets, which were seen as celestial brooms sweeping away the old or signaling disaster.</note> to appear. It brings omens of great misfortune, such as collapsing mountains, earthquakes, and the drying up of rivers and streams.

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Great One’s (Taiyi) system, moving from his three-year cycle of governance to the specific roles of the "Five Generals." These entities represent the planetary spirits and their influence over military strategy and state affairs based on the Five Elements and seasonal cycles.</meta>

<margin>
Notes by Tan Chuang of the Mountain Dwelling <note>original: 談創山居; likely a signature or attribution of a commentator living in a mountain retreat.</note>
</margin>

The **Five Generals** refer to the Supervising General of the Great One, the Upper and Lower Eyes, and the Great Host and Great Guest Generals. 

The **Supervising General** <note>original: 監將 (Jianjiang)</note> is the image of the Sovereign. He is the essence of the Year Star <term>Year Star: (歲星, Suixing) The planet Jupiter.</term> in the East, receiving the righteous energy of the virtue of Wood. He reigns during the three months of Spring. 

The **Lower Eye** <note>original: 下目 (Xiamu)</note> is the essence of the Sparkling Deluder <term>Sparkling Deluder: (熒惑, Yinghuo) The planet Mars.</term> in the South, receiving the righteous energy of the virtue of Fire. In the Heavens, he is Yang and is named the **Attacking General** <note>original: 始擊將 (Shiji Jiang)</note>. He belongs to the "Guest" <note>The "Guest" (客, Ke) represents the invading or attacking party in military divination.</note> and is a superior general. He reigns during the first and third months. 

The **Upper Eye** <note>original: 上目 (Shangmu)</note> is the essence of the Filling Star <term>Filling Star: (鎮星, Zhenxing) The planet Saturn.</term> in the Central Palace, receiving the righteous energy of the virtue of Earth. On the Earth, he is Yin and is named the **Literary Flourishing General** <note>original: 文昌將 (Wenchang Jiang)</note>. He belongs to the "Host" <note>The "Host" (主, Zhu) represents the defending party or the established state.</note> and is a superior general. He reigns during the four months that transition the seasons <term>Four Seasons: (四季, Siji) Specifically the third month of each season (3rd, 6th, 9th, and 12th lunar months), which are associated with the Earth element.</term>. 

The **Great Guest General** is the essence of the Hour Star <term>Hour Star: (辰星, Chenxing) The planet Mercury.</term> in the North, receiving the righteous energy of the virtue of Water. He governs matters of weapons and armor. He reigns during the three months of Winter. 

The **Great Host General** is the essence of the Great White <term>Great White: (太白, Taibai) The planet Venus.</term> in the West, receiving the righteous energy of the virtue of Metal. He governs matters of combat and battle. He reigns during the three months of Autumn. 

Each of the Five Generals takes his position according to the five directions and follows the cycles of "Resting" or "Reigning" <note>A reference to the "Five States of Energy" (Wu Wang), a system describing how elemental forces wax and wane throughout the year.</note> according to the four seasons.

> The Classic says: When the Host and the Guest face each other, the one who rides the **Life Energy** <note>original: 生氣 (Shengqi); vital, ascending cosmic energy.</note> shall be victorious; the one who rides the **Death Energy** <note>original: 死氣 (Siqi); stagnant or destructive cosmic energy.</note> shall be defeated.

For instance, if in the eighth month of Autumn one attacks the East using the "Great Cluster" <note>original: 大簇 (Dacu); the musical pitchpipe associated with the first month (Spring/East).</note> as the Attacking General, they will surely achieve a great victory. This is because they are riding the energy of Life and Sovereignty <note>Autumn belongs to Metal, but the East and the "Great Cluster" belong to Wood. In this system of divination, certain combinations allow an attacker to harness "Life Energy" regardless of the season.</note>. However, if one uses the "Southern Pipe" <note>original: 南呂 (Nanlü); the pitchpipe associated with the eighth month (Autumn/West).</note> as the Attacking General, the Host will surely suffer a great defeat, for they are riding the energy of Death.

The **Year Accountant** <note>original: 歲計 (Suiji)</note> is the messenger of the Year Star (Jupiter). This office governs the affairs of the years, months, days, and hours. Therefore, the Sages took this as a model and created the laws of...

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of the Great One’s (Taiyi) system. Having previously discussed the Five Generals, it now transitions to the "Nine Palaces" (Halls), detailing their mythological origins with the early sovereigns and the specific technical "shift" or offset that distinguishes the Taiyi system from other forms of divination.</meta>

The system of the **Great One** serves as the Ruler; the **Two Eyes** <note>Referring to the Upper and Lower Eye Generals mentioned previously.</note> function as the assistant ministers. The **Two Generals** define the "Host" and the "Guest," while the **Eight Gates** determine victory or defeat. To maneuver subtle strategies amidst wine vessels <note>original: 樽俎 (zunzu); refers to conducting high-level diplomacy during banquets.</note> and to deploy grand schemes within the commander's tent—this is indeed the essential path for one who assists a King.

### Section 26: Explanation of what the Nine Palaces Govern

Long ago, **Sui Ren** <note>The mythical "Fire Driller," credited with teaching humans how to use fire.</note> looked up at the North Star to fix the names of the directions. **Pao Xi** <note>Another name for Fuxi, the legendary creator of the Eight Trigrams.</note> followed by drawing the **Eight Trigrams**. When the **Yellow Emperor** received his mandate, he commanded **Da Nao** to create the Sexagenary Cycle <term>Sexagenary Cycle: (甲子, Jiazi) The system of 60 rotating stems and branches used to track time.</term> and **Rong Cheng** to create the calendar. The harmonics of the Eight Trigrams and the Nine Palaces arose from these events. 

The profound mystery and mechanism of the **Nine Palaces** are based upon the **Spirit Turtle** <note>A reference to the "Luo Shu" (Scroll of the River Luo), a magic square said to have been found on the back of a turtle.</note>. Two and four are the shoulders; six and eight are the feet. Left is three, right is seven. Nine is worn on the head, and one is trodden by the feet. This has been the unchanging way for ten thousand generations. 

The Yellow Emperor further commanded his minister, **Feng Hou**, to create the model for the **Great One**. In this system, the Nine Palaces all **shift by one position** <note>original: 蹉一位 (cuo yi wei); this refers to a technical displacement where the numbering of the Taiyi grid differs from the standard Luoshu square.</note>. Before the Jin Dynasty, the reason for this was not detailed. Only **Guo Pu’s** *Classic of the Radiance of Spirits* <note>original: 曜靈經 (Yaoling Jing)</note> states: "The Earth is deficient in the Southeast. Where the Palace numbers are many, they do not exceed nine; therefore, one shifts by nine to stabilize it." 

**Yue Chan** said: "The Great One’s logic clarifies human affairs to know the future. When a King obtains the 'One,' the world is rectified. Therefore, it shifts by one palace to complete the trajectory and position." 

**Wang Ximing** said: "The Great One governs human affairs to know the path of the future; thus, the Sages specifically shifted it by one position to illuminate the meaning of foreknowledge."

The **First Palace** is located in the **Qian** <note>The 'Creative' hexagram position, associated with the Northwest.</note> direction. It governs the provinces of **Bing** and **Ji**. The **Literary Flourishing General** is "Open," while "Prison" is under coercion <note>These are technical states in Taiyi divination regarding the status of the "Generals" and "Gates."</note>. It bears the image of the dwelling father.

<summary>This page explains the cosmic origins of the Nine Palaces through legendary figures like Fuxi and the Yellow Emperor, justifying the unique "one-palace shift" used in Taiyi divination to predict human affairs and statecraft.</summary>
<keywords>Nine Palaces, Yellow Emperor, Feng Hou, Spirit Turtle, Luo Shu, Wang Ximing, Guo Pu, divination, military strategy</keywords>

The Sword-Discussing Mountain Dwelling <note>original: 談劍山居 (Tanjuan Shanju); likely the studio name or pseudonym of a commentator or the transcriber.</note>

The **Second Palace** is located in **Li** <note>The Trigram representing Fire and the South.</note>; it governs **Jing Prefecture**. When the **Great One** visits it, the Sovereign shall execute generals and ministers.
<column-break/>
The **Third Palace** is located in **Gen** <note>The Trigram representing Mountain and the Northeast.</note>; it governs **Qing Prefecture**. When the **First Strike** <term>First Strike: (始擊, Shiji) One of the primary mobile spirits in Taiyi divinity, often associated with aggressive action or thunder.</term> visits it, rebellion and chaos shall enter the Central Palace, and war will arise.
<column-break/>
The **Fourth Palace** is located in **Zhen** <note>The Trigram representing Thunder and the East.</note>; it governs **Xu Prefecture**. When the **First Strike** visits it, the Western tribes <note>original: 西戎 (Xirong); ancient nomadic peoples to the west of China.</note> will come to invade.
<column-break/>
The **Sixth Palace** is located in **Dui** <note>The Trigram representing Lake and the West.</note>; it governs **Yong Prefecture**. When the **Guest Great General** visits it, the Southern state of Chu will come to invade.
<column-break/>
The **Seventh Palace** is located in **Kun** <note>The Trigram representing Earth and the Southwest.</note>; it governs **Yi Prefecture**. When the **Host Great General** visits it, war will arise in two prefectures.
<column-break/>
The **Eighth Palace** is located in **Kan** <note>The Trigram representing Water and the North.</note>; it governs **Yan Prefecture**. When the **Great One** visits it, high ministers shall be executed.
<column-break/>
The **Ninth Palace** is located in **Xun** <note>The Trigram representing Wind and the Southeast.</note>; it governs **Yang Prefecture**. When the **Guest Great General** visits it, the Northern tribes <note>original: 北狄 (Beidi); ancient nomadic peoples to the north of China.</note> will come to invade.
<column-break/>
**Zhang Liang** <note>The famous strategist and advisor to the founding emperor of the Han Dynasty.</note> said: Palaces Eight, Three, Four, and Nine are **Yang Palaces**. Palaces Two, Seven, Six, and One are **Ying Palaces**. One is known as **Severed Yang**; Nine is known as **Severed Yin**.
<column-break/>
In the fifth month, a single Yin is born, reaching the month of the Dog <note>original: 戌月 (Xuyue); the 9th lunar month.</note> as "Single Yin," and reaching the month of the Pig <note>original: 亥月 (Haiyue); the 10th lunar month.</note> as "Pure Yin." This occurs in the position of the **First Palace**, hence it is called **Severed Yang**. In the eleventh month,
<column-break/>
Yang is born, reaching the month of the Dragon <note>the 3rd lunar month.</note> as "Single Yang," and the month of the Snake <note>the 4th lunar month.</note> as "Pure Yang." This occurs in the position of the **Ninth Palace**, hence it is called **Severed Yin**. Nine, Four, and Six are the **Arising Vitality** <note>original: 起氣 (qiqi).</note>.
<column-break/>
Four (at the position of the Rabbit) and Six (at the position of the Rooster) represent the **Vernal Equinox** and the **Autumnal Equinox**. This is the beginning of the separation of Yin and Yang, hence they are called **Severed Vitality** <note>original: 絕氣 (jueqi).</note>. Two and Eight are the **Exchange Vitality**. Two (at the Horse),
<column-break/>
and Eight (at the Rat) represent the **Winter Solstice** and the **Summer Solstice**. These are the start of the mutual exchange of Yin and Yang, hence they are called **Exchange Vitality** <note>original: 易氣 (yiqi).</note>. All nine spirits of the Arising, Exchanging, and Severed Vitality of Yin and Yang are hereby presented.

## Section 27: Explaining the Domains of the Eight Gates

The **Dark Lady** <note>original: 玄女 (Xuan Nu); the "Dark Lady of the Nine Heavens," a Taoist goddess of war, sex, and longevity who supposedly imparted esoteric secrets to the Yellow Emperor.</note> says: Heaven has eight gates to communicate with the eight winds; Earth has eight directions to correspond with the eight trigrams. They regulate the four seasons to illuminate the nature of the myriad things.

The **Open Gate** (Kai Men) aligns with the **Qian** position <note>The trigram representing Heaven and the Northwest.</note> in the northwest. It governs the passage through gateways and general prosperity.

The **Rest Gate** (Xiu Men) aligns with the **Kan** position <note>The trigram representing Water and the North.</note> in the due north. It governs resting and peaceful dwelling.

The **Life Gate** (Sheng Men) aligns with the **Gen** position <note>The trigram representing Mountain and the Northeast.</note> in the northeast. It governs the birth and growth of the myriad things.

The **Injury Gate** (Shang Men) aligns with the **Zhen** position <note>The trigram representing Thunder and the East.</note> in the due east. It governs illness and calamities.

The **Hidden Gate** (Du Men) <note>original: 杜門; also translated as the Gate of Obstruction.</note> aligns with the **Xun** position <note>The trigram representing Wind and the Southeast.</note> in the southeast. It governs being blocked or unable to pass through.

The **Scenery Gate** (Jing Men) <note>original: 景門; also translated as the View Gate.</note> aligns with the **Li** position <note>The trigram representing Fire and the South.</note> in the due south. It governs the appearance of strange ghosts and apparitions.

The **Death Gate** (Si Men) aligns with the **Kun** position <note>The trigram representing Earth and the Southwest.</note> in the southwest. It governs mourning, death, and burial.

The **Fear Gate** (Jing Men) <note>original: 驚門; meaning "shock" or "alarm."</note> aligns with the **Dui** position <note>The trigram representing Lake and the West.</note> in the due west. It governs terror, panic, and fleeing.

The **Open**, **Rest**, and **Life** gates are greatly auspicious. The **Scenery** gate is secondarily auspicious. The **Death** and **Hidden** gates are greatly inauspicious. The **Fear** and **Injury** gates are minorly inauspicious. During the eight seasonal nodes, each of the eight gates governs its own period of **strength** <term>Strength: (王, Wang) A technical term in Chinese astrology meaning the peak period of influence for a specific element or gate.</term> for forty-five days. If one encounters this strength when going to war, it is of great benefit; if the gate is not strong, it is inauspicious.

## Section 28: Explaining the Locations of the Sixteen Spirits

The *Erya* <note>The oldest surviving Chinese dictionary/encyclopedia, dating back to the 3rd century BCE.</note> says: The four seasons act as the "Jade Candle" <note>A metaphor for the harmonious and balanced movement of the seasons.</note>.

The *Annals of Rhythms and the Calendar* says: The Heavenly Pole revolves through the twelve earthly branches; the five stars represent the Primordial Breath turning through the Three Systems.

The *Song of the Five Elements* says: First, discuss whether the numbers for the "Host" and "Guest" are many or few; next, observe whether Yin and Yang are in harmony. The Sixteen Spirits are held within one's heart <note>This suggests that once the practitioner understands the calculations, the positions of these spirits become intuitive.</note>.

<summary>This page defines the divinatory qualities and directional alignments of the Eight Gates (such as the auspicious Open and Life gates) and introduces the Sixteen Spirits, citing classical authorities like the Erya to ground these esoteric practices in scholarly tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Eight Gates, Taiyi divinity, Dark Lady, Trigrams, Chinese astrology, Erya, Sixteen Spirits, military divination</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the descriptions of the Eight Gates (Open, Rest, Life, etc.) to the specific attributes of the "Sixteen Spirits." These spirits comprise the Twelve Terrestrial Branches (Zi, Chou, etc.) and the four trigrams located at the corners (Gen, Xun, Kun, Qian). This section explains how each spirit governs the seasonal transition of Yin and Yang energies and the biological cycles of the myriad things throughout the lunar year.</meta>

# Heaven and Earth Encompassed in a Square Inch
<note>original: 乾坤方寸盡包羅; a poetic title suggesting that the vast complexities of the universe (Heaven and Earth) are contained within this compact system of divination.</note>

<margin>The Qianpou Mountain Dwelling</margin> <note>This likely refers to the studio name of a previous owner or the commentator who annotated this manuscript.</note>

The **Zi Spirit** is the **Master of the Earth**. During the month of Zi <note>The 11th lunar month, containing the Winter Solstice.</note>, the Yang energy first begins to move, but because the myriad things are still hidden below ground, it is called the Master of the Earth.

The **Chou Spirit** is **Yang Virtue**. During the month of Chou <note>The 12th lunar month.</note>, the "Two Yang" <note>Referring to the second line of a hexagram turning Yang, indicating increasing warmth.</note> are in effect, nurturing and distributing the seeds of the myriad things; thus, it is called Yang Virtue.

The **Gen Spirit** is **Harmonious Virtue**. This is the point where Winter and Spring first meet. The Yin and Yang energies are in harmony, and the myriad things begin to grow in clusters; thus, it is called Harmonious Virtue.

The **Yin Spirit** is **Extended Pitch** <note>original: 呂申 (Lü Shen); likely a variation of a musical pitch name or "Expanding Extension."</note>. During the month of Yin <note>The 1st lunar month, the start of Spring.</note>, the Yang energy opens into prosperity and the myriad things split open [their seeds]; thus, it is called Extended Pitch.

The **Mao Spirit** is **High Clusters**. During the month of Mao <note>The 2nd lunar month.</note>, all things emerge and grow in thick clusters from the earth; thus, it is called High Clusters.

The **Chen Spirit** is the **Great Solar**. During the month of Chen <note>The 3rd lunar month.</note>, thunder emerges to rouse hibernating creatures, and the Yang energy reaches its full height; thus, it is called the Great Solar.

The **Xun Spirit** is **Great Dread**. As Spring transitions into Summer and the peak heat is about to flourish, the Yang fire becomes scorching and cruel; thus, it is called Great Dread.

The **Si Spirit** is the **Great Spirit**. During the month of Si <note>The 4th lunar month.</note>, the Fire-Yin <note>The transition where the peak of Yang starts to harbor the seed of Yin.</note> is in effect, and the shifts between Yin and Yang are unpredictable; thus, it is called the Great Spirit.

The **Wu Spirit** is **Great Majesty**. During the month of Wu <note>The 5th lunar month, containing the Summer Solstice.</note>, the Yang energy begins to decline as Yin is born, and the season of harshness and "punishment" <note>In traditional cosmology, the waning of life-giving energy is associated with "punishment" or "execution" (xing).</note> begins; thus, it is called Great Majesty.

The **Wei Spirit** is the **Heavenly Way**. During the month of Wei <note>The 6th lunar month.</note>, the Fire element is capable of producing Earth, and it reigns supreme in the direction of Wei; thus, it is called the Heavenly Way.

The **Kun Spirit** is the **Great Martial**. As Summer and Autumn meet, the Yin energy begins to issue commands to strike down and harvest the myriad things; thus, it is called the Great Martial.

The **Shen Spirit** is **Martial Virtue**. During the month of Shen <note>The 7th lunar month.</note>, the [spring/summer] growth of the myriad things comes to an end, and buckwheat is about to sprout; thus, it is called Martial Virtue.

The **You Spirit** is the **Great Crowd** <note>original: 大簇 (Da Cu); also the name of a standard musical pitch pipe associated with spring, though here it refers to the density of the harvest.</note>. During the month of You <note>The 8th lunar month.</note>, the myriad things have all reached completion, resulting in a great abundance; thus, it is called the Great Crowd.

The **Xu Spirit** is the **Master of Yin**. During the month of Xu <note>The 9th lunar month.</note>, the Yang energy is no longer stored and Yin energy is in full command; thus, it is called the Master of Yin.

The **Qian Spirit** is **Yin Virtue**. As Autumn and Winter meet, the Yin precedes the birth of the Yang, and it possesses great virtue in its capacity to cycle life; thus, it is called Yin Virtue.

The **Hai Spirit** is **Great Righteousness**. During the month of Hai <note>The 10th lunar month.</note>, the myriad things... <note>Text ends mid-sentence.</note>

# Chapter 29: Explaining the Ritual Instruments of the Great One
<note>original: 太乙式儀; *Taiyi Shiyi*. The "Shi" is the physical divination board or cosmograph used to calculate celestial movements.</note>

In ancient times, when the **Yellow Emperor** <note>The legendary ancestor of Chinese civilization and patron of the esoteric arts.</note> received his Mandate of Heaven, a **Dragon-Horse** emerged from the Yellow River bearing a diagram on its back. Based on the patterns of that diagram, the Emperor fashioned the **Great One’s Divination Board**. The body of the board consists of three layers: the upper layer is blue, modeling **Heaven**; the lower layer is yellow, modeling **Earth**; and the middle layer is red, symbolizing **Humanity**. Thus, the "Three Powers" <note>Heaven, Earth, and Man.</note> are fully provided for. Within these layers, the twelve Chronograms of Heaven, the twelve Branches of Earth, the Four Dimensions <note>the corners: Gen, Xun, Kun, and Qian</note>, the Eight Gates, the Nine Palaces, and the Sixteen Spirits all have their symbolic representations.

# Chapter 30: The Method for Operating the Great One Board

The Classic says: In the method of operating the board, its people are as the Eight Horses. <note>original: 其人八馬; this is an esoteric mnemonic likely referring to the speed and sequence of calculations or a specific configuration of spirits known as the Eight Horses.</note>

**First:** Detail the location of the **Grand Duke** <note>original: 太歳; *Tai Sui*, the Jupiter-based presiding deity of the year.</note>. Because one seeks the **Calculating Spirit** and the **Conjoined Spirit**, one must detail this first.

**Second:** Detail the Palace in which the **Great One** <note>The central star/deity of the system.</note> resides in order to establish the **Overseeing General**.

**Third:** Detail which spirit serves as the **Heavenly Eye** <note>A crucial prognosticator in Taiyi used to determine the "Civil" or "Literary" side of an engagement.</note> in order to position the **Literary Radiance**, the **Initial Strike**, and the various other generals.

**Fourth:** Detail which spirit serves as the **Calculating Spirit** in order to find the **Guest Eye** <note>The "Guest Eye" represents the opposing or incoming force in a divination, whereas the "Heavenly Eye" represents the host or self.</note>.

**Fifth:** Detail the Calculating Spirit as it is added to **Harmonious Virtue**. <gloss>Plundering the People is the direction of the Ghost Gate; it is used to seek the auspiciousness or inauspiciousness of the Underworld.</gloss> <note>The "Ghost Gate" is typically located in the Northeast (Gen) and is the portal through which spirits and hidden energies pass.</note>

---

Sixth: Detail the locations of the **Heavenly Eye** and **Earthly Eye** <note>The "Eyes" are specific moving markers on the Taiyi board used to calculate the positions of the "Host" and "Guest" forces.</note> to determine the calculation for the **Host** and the **Guest**.

Seventh: Detail the number of counters to be set in order to fix the Palaces of the **Host Great General** and the **Guest Great General**.

Eighth: Detail the reduction of the Host and Guest counters: if eleven is obtained, set it as one; if thirty, set it as three; if fourteen, set it as four; if sixteen, set it as six; if thirty-four, discard thirty and set it as four. All other calculations follow this pattern. <note>This describes a method of mathematical reduction, likely a modulo-10 system, to fit the results within the specific divisions of the divination board.</note>

If performing the calculation based on the Palace where the **Heavenly Eye** is located: if the Heavenly Eye is in a cardinal palace <note>The four "straight" directions: North, South, East, or West.</note>, use the number of that cardinal palace to calculate. If it is in an **interstitial branch** <note>original: 間辰; *jianchen*. These are the non-cardinal directions or "corners" of the board, such as Northeast or Southwest.</note>, simply advance one branch and calculate from there. In all cases, stop the calculation before the **Palace of the Great One**; the result of this combined calculation is called the **Triad General** <note>original: 參將; *Sanjiang*. This spirit acts as a secondary or "lieutenant" general to assist the Great General in divination outcomes.</note>.

### Chapter 31: Explaining the Different Methods for Using the Great One Board

Ritual distinguishes the honorable from the humble; the *Changes* <note>The *I Ching* or *Book of Changes*, the foundational text of Chinese divination and philosophy.</note> discerns the noble from the lowly. If a minister walks the path of a ruler, governance becomes chaotic; if a son walks the path of a father, righteousness is subverted. Therefore, the use of the Board's **forward or reverse** <note>original: 逆順; *ni shun*. Refers to clockwise and counter-clockwise movements on the cosmograph.</note> motions differs according to the specific nature of the affair.

The **Commentary on the Great Plan** <note>original: 洪範傳; *Hongfan Zhuan*. The *Hongfan* is a famous chapter of the *Book of Documents* (Shujing) that outlines the "Nine Categories" of governance and natural law.</note> says: "*Hong*" means "Great." "*Fan*" means "Law." This refers to the Great Law of the Way of Heaven.

The Classic says: "The King’s inspection is as the **Year**." The commentary states: The King holds an office that unifies all officials, just as a Year encompasses the four seasons. "The **High Ministers** are as the **Month**." High ministers each have their specific jurisdictions, just as the months are distinct from one another. "The **Officers** are as the **Day**." The various officials and clerks divide and govern their duties, just as a month is divided into days. <note>This passage establishes a macrocosmic/microcosmic link: just as time is nested (days within months, months within years), the bureaucracy of the state is nested under the Emperor, and the movements of the Taiyi board reflect this hierarchical order.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the technical rules for calculating the "Triad General" and begins a new chapter justifying the Taiyi system's complexity through the lens of Confucian hierarchy and the "Great Plan" of the Book of Documents.</summary>
<keywords>Heavenly Eye, Earthly Eye, Triad General, Host and Guest, Hongfan, Book of Documents, cosmic hierarchy, divination math</keywords>

Years, months, days, and hours do not change; each follows its constant course. Therefore, the Sovereign uses the **Year Count** <note>The Year Count is used for grand state affairs, such as the fate of dynasties or national harvests.</note>; the Ministers and Nobles use the **Month Count**; the Officers and Clerks use the **Day Count**. However, the **Hour Count** <note>The "Hour" (shi) refers to the two-hour periods of the traditional Chinese day.</note> connects the high and the low; from the Son of Heaven above to the commoners below, all may use it.

## Chapter 32: Explaining the Method of the Imperial Era-Year Chronology

The records of Emperors and Kings began at the **Supreme Origin** <note>original: 上元甲子; *shangyuan jiazi*. In Chinese cosmogony, this is the theoretical starting point of time when all celestial cycles aligned.</note>. Through successive dynasties, time has stretched long and wide, and discrepancies in the records of years and months have appeared. Although the existing writings are voluminous and technically complete, they have become difficult to apply. Your servants have now compared the astronomical calculations with the original records, arranged them in order, and fixed this text to establish the rules for the divination board. We respectfully record this for your inspection.

Starting from the 6th year of the **Yuanshou** era of **Emperor Wu of Han** <note>117 BCE</note>, which was a *Jiazi* year, the details are provided as follows:

In the 1st year of the **Taiding** era of the **King of Jin** of the **Yuan Dynasty** <note>1324 CE</note>, a *Jiazi* year, the count entered the **First Cycle** at the **Door of Obstruction** <note>original: 杜門; *Dumen*. One of the Eight Doors in Taiyi, associated with concealment and stagnation.</note>. By the *Jiawu* year of the **Zhizheng** era <note>1354 CE</note>, it reached the **Door of Brilliance** <note>original: 景門; *Jingmen*. Associated with light, documents, and outward appearance.</note>.

In the 17th year of the **Hongwu** era of the **Great Ming** <note>1384 CE</note>, a *Jiazi* year, it entered the **Second Cycle** at the **Door of Death**. By the *Jiawu* year of the **Yongle** era <note>1414 CE</note>, it reached the **Door of Shock**.

In the 9th year of the **Zhengtong** era <note>1444 CE</note>, a *Jiazi* year, it entered the **Third Cycle** at the **Door of Openness**. By the 10th year of the **成化** era <note>1474 CE</note>, a *Jiawu* year, it reached the **Door of Rest**.

In the 17th year of the **Hongzhi** era <note>1504 CE</note>, a *Jiazi* year, it entered the **Fourth Cycle** at the **Door of Life**. By the 13th year of the **Jiajing** era <note>1534 CE</note>, a *Jiawu* year, it reached the **Door of Injury**.

When the **Heavenly Eye** is in a cardinal palace..."
