<margin>Sincerity is where one dwells</margin> <note>This marginalia likely refers to the spiritual state required for the divination to be effective; only a sincere heart can "dwell" in the truth of the calculation.</note>

When a **Yang Branch** <term>Yang Branch: The celestial branches associated with Yang energy: Zi (Rat), Yin (Tiger), Chen (Dragon), Wu (Horse), Shen (Monkey), and Xu (Dog)</term> acts as the basis for the **Yin Position**, and the **Yang Count** <note>original: 陽筭; *yangsuan*. A numerical result in Taiyi calculation.</note> yields an odd number, it is called **Double Yang**. If the **Yin Count** yields an even number, it is called **Double Yin**. Both of these states represent a lack of harmony. When the **Upper Eye** <note>The "Heavenly Eye" (Tianmu), a key indicator on the Taiyi divination board.</note> overlooks a **Correct Palace** and the count is odd, it is **Double Yang**. When the **Lower Eye** <note>The "Earthly Eye" (Dimu).</note> overlooks an **Intermediary Branch** and the count is even, it is **Double Yin**. 

If a count is even in a **Yang position**, or odd in a **Yin position**, these are both considered to be in **Harmony**. Harmony brings good fortune, while discord brings disaster. If the calculated counts result in 11, 13, 17, 19, 31, 33, or 39, these are **Yang Numbers**; if they overlook a Yang position, they become **Double Yang**. If the results are 20, 22, 24, 26, or 28, these are **Yin Numbers**; if they overlook a Yin position, they become **Double Yin**. Both cases result in discord.

When the **Great One** <note>original: 太乙; *Taiyi*. The personification of the supreme celestial pivot and the central focus of this divination system.</note> is in a **Yang Palace** and the count is odd, this is the number of **Double Yang**. The palaces 8, 3, 4, and 9 are the **Yang Palaces** where the **Great One** resides as Sovereign. When the **Great One** is in a **Yin Palace** and the count is even, this is the number of **Double Yin**. The palaces 2, 7, 6, and 1 are all **Yin Palaces**. In these cases, there is no harmony. However, if the **Great One** is in a **Yang/Odd Palace** and the calculated count is **Yin/Even**, this is the **Harmony of Yin and Yang**. Likewise, if the **Great One** is in a **Yin/Even Palace** and the count is **Yang/Odd**, this is also **Harmony**.

**Wang Ximing** <note>A famous Tang dynasty astronomer and master of Taiyi divination, often cited as an authority in later manuals.</note> says: 
*   3, 9, and the branches **Tiger** (Yin) and **Dragon** (Chen) are **Pure Yang**.
*   4, 8, and the branches **Snake** (Si) and **Ox** (Chou) are **Mixed Yang**.
*   2, 6, and the branches **Pig** (Hai) and **Goat** (Wei) are **Pure Yin**.
*   1, 7, and the branches **Monkey** (Shen) and **Horse** (Wu) are **Mixed Yin**. 
*   33 and 39 are **Pure Yang**. 
*   26 and 22 are **Pure Yin**. 
*   24 and 28 are **Mixed Double Yin**. 
*   13, 19, 31, and 37 are **Mixed Double Yang**. 
*   11 and 17 are **Double Yang within Yin**. 
*   28 is **Double Yin within Yang**. 

If the **Great One** or the **Heavenly Eye** is in a **Yang Position** and the calculation results in **Pure Yang**, it is... <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the categorization of numerical results on the next page.</note>

When a **Yin Position** yields a calculation of **Pure Yin** <note>original: 純陰; *chunyin*. A result where both the position and the numerical count are Yin (even), creating an imbalance of passive energy.</note>, it is always a **Great Omen of Disaster**. This is especially severe if the calculation falls into a state of **Exhaustion** <term>Exhaustion: *Jue* (絕), the lowest point in the twelve-stage cycle of energy, signifying a total lack of vital Qi.</term>. 

When the **Great One** and the **Heavenly Eye** <note>The *Taiyi* (Great One) and *Tianmu* (Heavenly Eye) are the two primary moving components on the Taiyi divination board used to calculate cosmic trends.</note> reside in a **Yang Palace** and the result is **Mixed Double Yang**, or if they reside in a **Yin Position** and the result is **Mixed Double Yin**, it indicates that there are plots and schemes being hatched both internally and externally. This is also significantly worsened in a state of **Exhaustion**. 

If the **Great One** and the **Heavenly Eye** are in a **Yin Position** and the calculation yields **Double Yang within Yin**, there are plots unfolding internally. If they are in a **Yang Position** and the calculation yields **Double Yin within Yang**, there are plots unfolding from the outside.

The following numerical results determine the degree of **Harmony**:
*   Counts of **14** and **18** are considered **Superior Harmony** <note>original: 上和; *shanghe*.</note>.
*   Counts of **23**, **29**, and **32** are considered **Secondary Harmony** <note>original: 次和; *cihe*.</note>.
*   Counts of **12**, **16**, **18**, **34**, and **38** are considered **Inferior Harmony** <note>original: 下和; *xiahe*. Note that 18 appears in both Superior and Inferior categories in the source text, likely indicating different interpretations based on the specific palace occupied.</note>.

If the **Great One** and the **Heavenly Eye** are established in their respective Yin or Yang positions, a result with a **Preponderance of Yang** <note>original: 陽多; *yang duo*. A higher count of odd numbers.</note> is **Advantageous to the Guest** <term>Guest: *Ke* (客), the party initiating action, such as an attacking army or a traveler.</term>. A result with a **Preponderance of Yin** is **Advantageous to the Host** <term>Host: *Zhu* (主), the party receiving action, such as a defending city or a local ruler.</term>. 

One must carefully examine the "depth or shallowness" of the **Qi** <note>Referring to the strength and seasonal timing of the life-force energy.</note> to clearly distinguish victory from defeat. 

From the **Sixth Year of the Yuanshou Era** of **Emperor Wu of the Han** [117 BCE], a *Shangyuan Jiazi* year <note>The *Shangyuan Jiazi* is the theoretical starting point of the grandest cycle in Chinese chronology.</note>, until the **Eighth Year of the Zhenghe Era** [1118 CE], a *Wuxu* year, a total of **1,296 years** have accumulated.

# Volume 3 of the Classic of Blessed Response
## Section 34: Explaining Occlusion

The Scripture states: "Occlusion" <note>original: 掩; *yan*. In the Taiyi system, this refers to a specific celestial configuration where a subordinate "General" spirit occupies the same position as the Great One.</note> occurs when the First Strike General <note>The "First Strike General" (*Shiji Jiang*) is one of the primary spirits in Taiyi divination, representing military aggression, swift action, and the "striking" force of cosmic energy.</note> arrives at the Palace of the Great One. 

When this configuration is encountered in the calculations for the Annual Cycle, it signifies that the fundamental order of the state has been lost: ministers become overbearing while the monarch grows weak; the nation faces ruin and families are destroyed. These are times of sudden raids and great calamities. One should cultivate virtue and perform rituals of atonement to avert these disasters. This "Occlusion" carries the meaning of "Robbery and Slaughter." 

If Occlusion occurs while the Great One is in a "Yang Extremity" position, it is a sign of great misfortune. If it occurs in a "Yin Extremity" position, it portends a minister executing his sovereign. If the Great General is "occluding" the sovereign, the Great General faces defeat. If the mathematical counts are in harmony, there is good fortune; if they are discordant, there is disaster. If the Assistant General’s <note>The "Assistant General" (*Canjiang*) is a secondary spirit used to determine the success of auxiliary or supporting forces.</note> count is in harmony, it is favorable for the Assistant General to lead an expedition, as some profit may still be found. However, if the Sovereign, the Assistant General, and the Junior General are all in a state of misfortune due to Occlusion, one must not deploy troops.

In the second year of the *Tianfu* era of Emperor Zhaozong of the Tang Dynasty (the *Renxu* year) <note>902 AD</note>, the Great One was positioned in the *Renzi* direction <note>North</note>. This corresponded to the Eleventh Bureau and the Fourth Palace. The "High Thicket" <note>An astronomical coordinate or spirit name</note> served as the Celestial Eye. 

The Sovereign's calculation resulted in a count of four, with the Sovereign’s Great General in the Fourth Palace. The Guest calculation <note>The "Guest" (*Ke*) represents the external party, challenger, or invading army in a divination.</note> resulted in a count of one, with the Guest’s Great General in the Second Palace. 

In that year, Emperor Zhaozong’s carriage was moved to Fengxiang. Li Maozhen served as the Palace Intermediate and was on good terms with Han Quanhui and others. Each harbored the ambition of "holding the Son of Heaven hostage to command the feudal lords." In the fourth month, the King of Liang dispatched the General Zhu Wen and others to station their great army at Xingping. The forces of Qi <note>Li Maozhen's power base</note> established a camp, but they were surrounded and besieged. By the eleventh month, food supplies within the city were exhausted. Maozhen marched out to do battle but was defeated and forced to sue for peace. In the sixth month, Zhu Quanzhong <note>Also known as Zhu Wen</note> led fifty thousand troops from Hezhong to besiege Fengxiang. In the twelfth month—

<margin>Residence at Mount Tan-yan</margin> <note>original: 談剡山居; *Tan Yan Shan Ju*. This likely refers to the studio name or location of a previous owner or the transcriber of this specific manuscript copy.</note>

In that month, at Fu Prefecture, the wife and children of Tong Yi were captured. Zhou Yi immediately surrendered with his troops. At this time, the prefectures of Fu, Ning, and Fang all came into the possession of Zhu Quanzhong. This was the cosmic response signaling Quanzhong's victory.

# Section 35: Explaining "Striking"

The Scripture states: In the palace where the Great One <note>term: Great One (*Taiyi*). The supreme celestial deity and the central point of this system of divination, representing the cosmic pivot and the Emperor.</note> resides, if the Guest Eye is to the left, it is considered a "Front Strike." If it is to the right, it is a "Rear Strike." If it is in the palace immediately preceding the Great One, it is an "External Strike." If it is in the palace immediately following it, it is an "Internal Strike."

This "Striking" refers to acts of insulting, grappling, hitting, or stabbing. It describes a situation where those below insult those above, or where the inferior attempts to replace the superior. When the "Striking" configuration is encountered in the Annual Calculation, the Sovereign, the Generals, and the Ministers should dread it.

Wang Ximing <note>A renowned 8th-century astronomer and master of the Taiyi system during the Tang Dynasty.</note> says: When the Great One is near the "First Strike" <note>term: First Strike (*Shiji*). One of the three main "General" spirits in Taiyi, representing aggressive action, military force, and the initiative in conflict.</note>, it is conceptually named "Striking." 

Being in front of the Palace of the Great One is a "Front Strike." Being behind the Palace is a "Rear Strike." Being behind is also called an "Internal Strike." If the Primary forces <note>The "Primary" (*Zhu*) forces usually represent the home side, the ruler, or the status quo.</note> are internal, it portends deposition and execution; the omen falls upon those of the same surname as the ruler, close officials, empresses, consorts, or members of the royal clan. 

A "Front Strike" is considered an "External Strike." This portends encroachment and insults coming from external officials or feudal lords. If this occurs on a "Branch" <note>term: Branch (*Chen*). The twelve Earthly Branches used to track time and direction; here, it suggests a more specific, immediate timeframe.</note>, the matter is urgent. If it occurs in a "Palace" <note>The eight or nine divisions of the Taiyi cosmic board; here, it suggests a broader, more gradual timeframe.</note>, the matter is slow. One must evaluate whether the Yin and Yang energies are in harmony or "severed," and whether they are present or absent, to clearly determine victory or defeat.

In the first year of the Tongguang era of Emperor Zhuangzong of the Later Tang <note>923 AD</note>, a year of the Water-Goat (*Gui-wei*), the Great One was in the Sixth Cycle, the *Ren-zi* Epoch, Bureau 32. The Great Spirit <note>original: 大神; *Dashen*. A significant spirit in the Taiyi calculation.</note> was in the Third Palace, serving as the Celestial Eye. The Primary Count was 25. The Primary Great General and Small General were "blocked without a gate" <note>term: Blocked without a gate (*Du sai wu men*). A technical term for a configuration where the mathematical counts provide no "opening" or favorable direction for action, signifying entrapment.</note>. Before the Calculating Spirit could act, the "First Strike" approached the Earthly Primary position. The Guest Count was 8. The Guest Great General was in the Eighth Palace, and the Assistant General was in the Fourth Palace. In this year, the Prince of Liang <note>Zhu Youzhen, the last ruler of the rival Later Liang dynasty.</note> dispatched his army first. The Emperor personally crossed north of the Yellow River...

They attacked Zhongdu, where the Liang army suffered a crushing defeat. The senior general Wang Yanzhang was captured. In the tenth month, Emperor Jun of Liang committed suicide, and his entire imperial clan was utterly exterminated.

Because in this configuration the Primary Calculation was precarious <note>term: Precarious (*wùniè*). A technical term in divination meaning unstable or dangerously balanced, suggesting a situation on the brink of collapse.</note> and the Guest Eye "internally struck" the Great One, it was unfavorable for any proactive undertakings. This was the cosmic response to the Liang King’s defeat and downfall.

### Section 36: Explaining "Oppression"

The Scripture states: Regarding "Oppression" <note>term: Oppression (*pò*). A configuration where a secondary spirit occupies a position immediately adjacent to the Great One, suggesting pressure, constraint, or a threat to the sovereign’s authority.</note>, the position in front is called "External Oppression," and the position behind is called "Internal Oppression." Whenever the Two Eyes <note>The Primary Eye and the Guest Eye.</note> or the four Great and Assistant Generals are situated to the immediate left or right of the Great One, it is considered "Oppression."

Wang Ximing says: The Lower Eye <note>The Terrestrial Eye.</note> does not carry the meaning of Oppression; it pertains only to the Upper Eye <note>The Celestial Eye.</note>. When a Palace is Oppressed, it indicates the mechanism of disaster is forming. When a Chronogram <note>term: Chronogram (*chen*). Specifically referring to the twelve earthly branches representing time and direction.</note> is Oppressed, the disaster is imminent. If the Annual Calculation encounters Oppression, the Sovereign should loathe it. The palace one step ahead is "External Palace Oppression," and the palace one step behind is "Internal Palace Oppression."

In the second year of the Tianyou era of Emperor Ai of the Tang Dynasty (the year *Yi-Chou*, 905 CE), the Great One was located at *Ren-Zi* <note>The first position in the cosmic cycle.</note>, in Circle 14, Palace 6. The Great Deity was the Celestial Eye. The Primary Calculation resulted in the number ten. The Primary Great General was in Palace 1, and the Assistant General was in Palace 3. The branch *Chou* served as the Calculation Spirit, and "Great Might" was the First Strike. The Guest Calculation was a single 9; the Guest Great General was in Palace 9, and the Assistant General was in Palace 7.

In that year, Zhu Quanzhong, the King of Liang, executed nearly all the Tang Grand Councilors and the officials of the Three Departments <note>The central administrative branches of the Tang government.</note>. In this configuration, the Great One's Primary Great General was in a state of "External Palace Oppression." Although it assisted the Guest Calculation and harmonized with the timing, because the Great General was "Externally Oppressing" the Great One, this was the cosmic response to the massacre of the Tang Grand Councilors and their subordinates.

## Section 37: Explaining "Imprisonment"

The Scripture states: "Imprisonment" <note>term: Imprisonment (*qiú*). A specific configuration in Taiyi divination where subordinate spirits occupy the same "palace" or sector as the Great One spirit. In political terms, this symbolizes subordinates encroaching on the ruler's space.</note> signifies the themes of usurpation and regicide. Whenever the Wenchang <note>The Primary Eye, representing the chief counselor or civil authority.</note> and the four Great and Assistant Generals of the Primary and Guest sides are in the same palace as the Great One, it is collectively named "Imprisonment."

If this occurs in a "Place of Transformation or Extinction" <note>Technical divinatory positions suggesting volatile or terminal energy.</note>, it is extremely ominous. If it occurs in a place of "Absolute Yin" or "Absolute Yang," one will suffer a self-inflicted defeat. If the various generals are in the same palace as the Great One, or if a general is near the Great One and plots for his own kind, it is said that "the plot is within." If the calculations are in harmony, the plot will succeed; if they are not in harmony, the plot will fail.

In the second year of the Longde era of the Later Liang dynasty [922 CE], a *renwu* year, the Taiyi Sixth Cycle entered the *renzi* epoch, Bureau 31. The Great One was in Palace 3. The "Great Decline" star served as the Celestial Eye. The Primary Calculation was thirty-three. The Primary Great General was in Palace 3. The Assistant General was in Palace 9. The spirit *Shen* [the Monkey] served as the Calculator Spirit. The "Initial Strike" spirit <note>term: Initial Strike (*shǐjī*). A spirit associated with aggressive action and the vanguard of an army.</note> presided over the Yin position. The Primary Guest Calculation was ten. The Guest Great General was in Palace 1, and the Guest Assistant General was in Palace 3.

In the first month of that year, the Liang general Dai Siyuan launched a surprise attack on the Prince of Jin <note>Li Cunxu (885–926), who would later become Emperor Zhuangzong of the Later Tang.</note> at Zhenzhou. Siyuan took advantage of the city's emptiness to capture Chengan and then returned. He then hastily dispatched generals to exploit their victory and attack the North City. In the second month, the Prince of Jin’s troops arrived; the siege was lifted, and Dai Siyuan retreated of his own accord.

In this Bureau, the Primary Great General and the Guest Assistant General were both in the same palace as the Great One. Although the calculations were "long" <note>In Taiyi, a "long" calculation (over 20) usually suggests strength or duration.</note>, they were not in harmony. Thus, the cosmic response was that although Dai Siyuan captured the city fortifications, he achieved no lasting merit and was forced to retreat.

## Section 38: Explaining "Closure"

The Scripture states: "Closure" <note>term: Closure (*guān*). A divinatory configuration where the primary and secondary spiritual agents align in a way that suggests a "bottleneck" or "barricade." Symbolically, it represents a state where the ruler's intentions are blocked by his own ministers.</note> occurs when the four generals of the Primary and Guest sides, along with the Celestial Eye, all occupy the same palace or share the same numerical value. 

"Closure" means the light of the King or Emperor is obscured. In terms of its significance, "Closure" indicates that officials, generals, and ministers are consumed by mutual jealousy. Consequently, the benevolence and favors of the sovereign are prevented from reaching the people.

### Case Study: The Revolt at Xingyuan

In the fourth year of the Taihe era of Emperor Wenzong of the Tang Dynasty [830 CE], a *gengxu* year, the Taiyi cycle entered the *gengzi* epoch, Bureau 11. The Great One spirit was in Palace 4. The "High Thicket" <note>A technical name for a specific star or position within the Taiyi system.</note> served as the Celestial Eye. The Primary Calculation was a single 4. The Primary Great General was in Palace 4. The Guest General was in Palace 2. The Guest Calculation was a single 4. The Guest Great General and the Primary Great General were in the same palace [Palace 4]; thus, the nature of the generals was identical.

In the second month of that year, a mutiny broke out in the Xingyuan Army. An imperial decree had ordered the Military Governor, Li Jiang, to dispatch troops to assist in a nearby conflict. When the troops reached Ling, they retreated and returned. A subsequent decree ordered them all to be dismissed. Li Jiang, acting on the imperial mandate to recruit and then discharge these men, discussed the decree's intent to provide them with gifts of granary wheat and provisions before sending them home. However, the soldiers were deeply dissatisfied and retreated in a surly mood.

The Army Supervisor, Yang Shuyuan, had long harbored a grudge against Li Jiang, viewing him as disrespectful. He exploited the meager nature of the granary gifts to provoke the soldiers' anger. The mob erupted in a great clamor, looted the storehouse, and stormed the governor's headquarters.

At that moment, Li Jiang was hosting a banquet for his guests and subordinates. An officer rushed in to report: "The recruits are revolting! Please, you must flee!"

Li Jiang replied, "I am the commander of this army; how can I run away?" He signaled to his Judicial Officer, Zhao Cunyue, urging him to escape. 

Cunyue responded, "I have been honored by your recognition and wisdom, my lord. How could I seek a shameful escape now?"

The headquarters general, Wang Jingyan, fought to defend them, but Li Jiang, Zhao Cunyue, and their companions were all murdered. Yang Shuyuan then sent a memorial to the throne, falsely claiming that Li Jiang had embezzled the recruitment funds, which triggered the riot. It was only after the Three Departments <note>term: Three Departments (*sān shěng*). The highest administrative arms of the central government: the Secretariat, Chancellery, and State Affairs.</note> submitted a joint memorial arguing Li Jiang's innocence and exposing Yang Shuyuan's crime in provoking the troops that the Emperor finally realized the truth. He then ordered the Xingyuan garrison to suppress the rebels and punish those responsible.

## Section 39: Explaining "Obstruction"

The Scripture states: "Obstruction" <note>term: Obstruction (*gé*). A configuration in the Taiyi system where key divinatory agents occupy the palace directly opposite the Great One spirit, representing a blockage or confrontation.</note> occurs when the Two Eyes <note>The Celestial Eye (Primary) and the Guest Eye.</note> and the Great and Small Generals are in the palace opposite the Great One. This signifies that government affairs are blocked between the higher and lower levels of administration. If this occurs in a "Severed" or "Exhausted" position <note>Technical terms for specific weak points on the divinatory board.</note>, failure is certain. In the Annual Calculation, encountering "Obstruction" makes it unfavorable to take any action. 

What is called "Obstruction" carries the meaning of "Change" as found in the *I Ching* <note>The *I Ching*, or Book of Changes, is the foundational text of Chinese divination.</note>. If the Great One is obstructed, it is the image of a thief insulting their sovereign. If the Primary and Guest sides are equal yet discordant, they will face defeat.

### Case Study: The Barbarian Invasion and the Hongzhou Rebels

In the twelfth year of the Dazhong era of Emperor宣宗 (Xuanzong) of the Tang Dynasty [858 CE], a *wuyin* year, the Great One cycle entered the *gengzi* epoch, Bureau 39. The Great One <note>term: The Great One (*Tàiyǐ*). The supreme celestial deity/spirit whose movement through the "palaces" of the cosmic board determines the fortune of the state.</note> was in Palace 6. The Yin Master served as the Celestial Eye. The Primary Calculation was 35. The Primary Great and Small Generals were "blocked with no exit" <note>A state where the divinatory markers have no mathematical path to move forward.</note>. The Guest Calculation was 34. The Guest Great General was in Palace 4, creating an "Obstruction" against the Secondary General <note>term: Secondary General (*cānjiàng*). A supporting spirit in the Taiyi system.</note> who was in Palace 2.

In that year, the Southern Barbarians attacked An Prefecture. The Southern General and Protector General, Li Zhu, had committed acts of plunder and robbery, which incited the barbarians to become bandits. In the eighth month, rebels from Hongzhou attacked Xuanzhou. An imperial decree ordered the troops from West Zhejiang to pacify them.

In this configuration, the Primary side (the government/host) was "blocked with no exit," and the Guest Great General "obstructed" the Great One. This is unfavorable for the one who takes the first move. The outcome confirmed this: both the campaign against the barbarian bandits and the efforts against the Hongzhou rebels resulted in failure.

This creates the "Four Solid Walls." 

Some say that when the "Host" <note>The side being attacked or the domestic side.</note> occupies these "Walls," they should maintain a firm defense and wait for the enemy to achieve victory; it is unfavorable to take the initiative and attack first. These "Four Fortifications" represent the strength of "Four Seizures." 

When the Annual Calculation encounters this, it primarily portends the calamities of usurpation, deposition, or being held captive. A sovereign should cultivate their virtue in order to avert these disasters through ritual.

## 42. Explaining the Walls and Blockages

The Classic says: "Four Walls and Blockages" <note>original: "郭杜", Guodu</note> occurs when the Guest Associate General merges with the "Literary Brilliance" spirit <note>Wenchang</note>, or when the Primary Associate General merges with the Guest Great General. If this is further combined with the conditions of "Masking," "Pressure," "Closure," or "Blocking," <note>yan, po, guan, and ge; technical terms for specific spatial overlaps of divinatory spirits that signal interference</note> then deploying troops will be "Shut and Blocked." No matters will find success. If a year encounters this configuration, it must be calamitous.

In the fourth year of the Zhenming era of Emperor Mo of the Later Liang <note>918 CE</note>, a *wuyin* year, the Great One cycle entered the twenty-seventh calculation of the *renzi* epoch. The Guest Great General was in the Eighth Palace, and the Guest Associate General was in the Fourth Palace. In the first month of that year, the Jin army <note>The rival forces of the Shatuo Turks who would eventually establish the Later Tang</note> raided the Liang territory...

After the battle at Chanzhou <note>A strategic location on the Yellow River in modern Henan.</note>, the army returned. The Jin forces resisted our army <note>The text is written from the perspective of the Later Liang dynasty.</note>; we faced unfavorable conditions and retreated. The Jin forces, by rule, should not have moved first, yet in the end they broke the encirclement to meet the enemy. This is a case of "Responding to Trouble" <note>Yingchuan: a tactical success achieved by reacting correctly to an unfavorable situation.</note>.

### 43. Explaining "Hold and Lift"

The Classic says: "Hold and Lift" <term>Zhitie|A technical term for a restrictive alignment where a presiding spirit is in direct opposition to its home gate</term> refers to when the Great One <note>Taiyi, the supreme celestial deity or star used to track cosmic cycles</note> aligns in opposition <note>original: "chong," a 180-degree celestial alignment</note> with the two gates of "Open" and "Life." This is unfavorable and is named the "Hold and Lift Blockage." If encountered in the Annual Calculation, one must not initiate any major undertakings.

For example, if the Open Gate is the Duty Officer <term>Zhishi|The specific spirit or "Gate" assigned to govern the current time period</term>, it must not be in opposition with the Open Gate.

If the Life Gate is the Duty Officer, it must not be in opposition with the Life Gate.

In the second year of the Qianhua era of Emperor Taizu <note>Zhu Wen (852–912 CE), the founder of the Later Liang dynasty.</note> of the Liang (a *Renshen* year), the Great One <term>Taiyi|The supreme celestial deity and the central moving point in this system of cosmic divination</term> enters the twenty-first configuration of the *Renzi* cycle. The Great One resides in the Eighth Palace. The Yin Master serves as the Heavenly Eye <term>Tianmu|A symbolic star in Taiyi used to calculate the "Host" or internal state</term>. The Host Calculation is a single two. The Host Great General is in the Second Palace, forming a "clash" <note>An unfavorable alignment suggesting internal conflict or obstruction.</note>. The Assistant General is in the Sixth Palace. The direction of *Wu* <note>The South</note> acts as the Calculating Spirit. The First Strike General oversees the Great Deity. The Guest Calculation is seventeen. The Guest Great General is in the Seventh Palace, and the Assistant General is in the First Palace.

In the leap month of this year, the Emperor’s illness became critical. He summoned the princes and close relatives to his imperial bedside, bestowing upon each a belt of gold and jade. He said: "For thirty years, I have managed this royal enterprise. I did not expect the remnants of Taiyuan <note>Referring to the rival Jin state forces led by the Li family.</note> to be so rampant. Observing them now, I see their ambition is not small. If my life is cut short, my sons will be no match for them after I am gone. I fear I shall have no place for my own burial." 

In the sixth month, on the *Wuyin* day, the Prince of Ying <note>Zhu Yougui, the Emperor's son.</note> of the Liang and his associates assassinated their lord, Zhu Huang <note>The personal name of Emperor Taizu.</note>, in his bedchamber, and the Prince established himself as the ruler.

In this layout, the Host Great General is "clashed" and the Gate of Death serves as the Directing Messenger. The Great One's annual calculation has passed through this point; therefore, one must not undertake any major affairs. This was the omen of a minister killing his sovereign, which ultimately led to the dynasty's destruction.

**End of Volume Three of the Classic of the Great One’s Blessed Response**

# The Great One’s Classic of Blessed Response, Volume 4

## Section 44: Explaining the Completeness or Incompleteness of the Three Gates

The Classic says: The "Three Gates" <note>These are part of the "Eight Gates" system in ancient Chinese metaphysics, representing different qualities of cosmic energy or "Qi."</note> refer to the Gate of Opening, the Gate of Rest, and the Gate of Life. If the Heavenly Eye <term>Tianmu|A symbolic point used in Taiyi calculations to represent the "Host" or internal affairs</term> of the Great One <term>Taiyi|The supreme celestial deity and the central moving point in this system of cosmic divination</term> is positioned beneath the Gate of Opening or the Gate of Life, it is defined as "Two Gates Incomplete." If it is positioned beneath the Gate of Rest, it is defined as "Three Gates Incomplete" <note>In Taiyi divination, "completeness" of the gates indicates a state of harmony and readiness. "Incompleteness" suggests a lack of support or a vulnerability in the situation being analyzed.</note>.

In the Annual Calculation, encountering the Heavenly Eye beneath these three auspicious gates is greatly lucky. The Gate of Radiance is minorly lucky. However, if it falls beneath the Gates of Death, Fear, Stop, or Injury, these are all unlucky.

To calculate this, set the number of accumulated years from the Great Beginning <note>The "Shangyuan Jiazi," a theoretical point of origin for all Chinese calendrical cycles.</note> of the Calculated Era up to the year desired; the method is the same as the "Small Advancement" calculation. Every thirty years, the position shifts once; two hundred and forty years completes one full cycle <note>A cycle of 240 years contains eight 30-year shifts, corresponding to the Eight Gates.</note>. Therefore, divide the total years by 240. Use the remainder and divide it by 30 to find the "Directing Gate" <term>Zhishi|The specific 'Gate' that governs a particular period of time</term>; the count begins from the outside of the calculation to obtain the result.

Once the Directing Gate for the governed year is determined, the sequence begins with the Gate of Opening, followed by the Gate of Rest, proceeding in clockwise order to start the cycle again.

For example, if at the Great Beginning *Jiazi*, the Gate of Opening is the Directing Gate, then after thirty years, the Gate of Rest will become the Directing Gate. The rest follows this pattern.

Whenever a *Jiazi* day occurs—specifically from *Jiazi* to *Jiawu*, or from *Jiawu* back to *Jiazi* <note>*Jiazi* is the 1st and *Jiawu* is the 31st term in the 60-unit cycle, marking the two midpoints.</note>—the Directing Gate changes. This rule applies equally to years, months, days, and hours. The Annual Calculation shifts every thirty years, the Monthly every thirty months, the Daily every thirty days, and the Hourly every thirty hours. The points of *Jiazi* and *Jiawu* always serve as the periods of transition.

# The Great One’s Classic of Blessed Response, Volume 4

## Section 45: Explaining the Activation or Non-activation of the Five Generals

The Classic says: The "Five Generals" <note>In Taiyi divination, these are five specific celestial points or deities—the Literary Radiance, the Initial Strike, the Great General, the Small General, and the Secondary General—whose positions determine military readiness.</note> are said to be "activated" or "not activated" based on their coordination. 

If the **Initial Strike** <term>Shiji|A point representing the spearhead of an attack or the Guest’s momentum</term> is not supported by the **Covering Strike**, if the **Literary Radiance** <term>Wenchang|The chief celestial official, representing the Host’s strategy and administration</term> lacks **Causal Pressure**, or if the **Great General** and **Small General** are not in a harmonious relationship, then one must look behind to the **Three Gates**. 

If the **Three Gates** are complete, then the **Five Generals** will naturally come into alignment. However, if the Three Gates are not complete, one must not deploy troops. If the Five Generals do not activate, one must not force a battle. <note>This emphasizes that military action requires a synergy of various cosmic factors; without this "activation," even the most powerful army lacks the spiritual momentum necessary for victory.</note>

## Section 46: Explaining the Relationship between the Host and the Guest

The ancients spoke of the **Host** and the **Guest** in the Great One’s system through metaphors: "One forest cannot hold two tigers," and "One pool cannot hold two snakes." These metaphors express the principle that opposing forces cannot both prevail; one must eventually yield. 

In general, when encountering an enemy and conflict is unavoidable, one must examine the **Gate** <term>Guan|A "barrier" or "checkpoint" determined by the interaction of the Five Elements</term>. 
*   If the **Guest's Gate** overcomes the **Host**, then the Guest <note>The Guest usually represents the invading or initiating party.</note> will be victorious. 
*   If the **Host's Gate** overcomes the **Guest**, then the Host <note>The Host usually represents the defending party or the local authority.</note> will be victorious. 

These outcomes are decided using the **Day Count**, the **Two Eyes** <note>The Heavenly Eye and the Earthly Eye, two primary indicators in a Taiyi chart.</note>, and the **Melodic Elements** <term>Nayin|A complex system that assigns one of the Five Elements to pairs of signs in the sexagenary cycle</term>. The so-called "Gate" is derived from the way the **Five Elements** (Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, Water) naturally control or restrain one another.

For example:
Suppose **High Thicket** <note>Gaocong, a specific position on the divination board.</note> serves as the **Earthly Eye**, which belongs to the **Wood** element. If **Great Clan** <note>Dazu, another position.</note> serves as the **Heavenly Eye**, which belongs to the **Metal** element. Since Metal can restrain Wood, this means the Guest’s Gate has overcome the Host. In this case, it is advantageous to act as the Guest, and the Guest will win.

For example:
Suppose the **Yin Master** <note>Yinzhu.</note> serves as the **Earthly Eye**, which belongs to the **Earth** element. If the **Earth Master** <note>Dizhu.</note> serves as the **Heavenly Eye**, which belongs to the **Water** element. Since Earth can restrain Water, this constitutes a victory for the Host...

# When the Gate of Humanity is Open, the Guest Benefits; But if Taken as the Host, the Host Wins
## Section 47: Explaining the Host and the Guest

The Classic says: The **Host** and the **Guest** represent the etiquette of "First and Last" and the principle of "Movement and Stillness." 

The ancient classics say: When armies are arrayed upon the open plains and the banners and drums face one another, the one who initiates action first is the **Guest**, and the one who moves later is the **Host**. In a world at peace, the one who first raises a matter <note>This refers to legal petitions, administrative initiatives, or diplomatic overtures.</note> is the **Host**, and the one who responds later is the **Guest**. 

If the **Guest** obtains the **Three Gates** <note>The Gate of Opening, the Gate of Rest, and the Gate of Life.</note> in their entirety, the **Five Generals** <note>See Section 45 for the specific celestial officers making up this group.</note> are activated, and **Yin and Yang** are in harmony, then harmony and the military will be victorious wherever they are directed. In such a case, the one who initiates first wins, and the one who acts later loses. 

However, if the Three Gates are not complete, the Five Generals are not activated, and Yin and Yang are not in harmony, it is not advantageous to raise an army. In these circumstances, it is auspicious to remain peaceful and maintain a firm defense. 

If you wish to know the "Entries and Exits" for the Host and the Guest: 
* Departing from the East follows the **Hidden Virtue** <term>Yinde|A celestial spirit associated with the wood element and the direction of growth</term>.
* Departing from the South follows the **Harmonious Virtue** <term>Hede|Associated with the fire element and the peak of vital energy</term>.
* Departing from the West follows the **Great Brightness** <term>Dajing|Associated with the metal element and the setting sun; sometimes translated as "Great Heat"</term>.
* Departing from the North follows the **Great Martial** <term>Dawu|Associated with the water element and the power of the North</term>.

These are the directions for initial deployment, used to determine which spirits the Host and Guest will encounter or rely upon. If you wish to know the fate of the **Host**, observe the calculated number of the **Host's Eye** <term>Zhumu|The primary mathematical point in a Taiyi chart representing the defender or the established party</term> to make a judgment. If you wish to know the fate of the **Guest**, observe the calculated number of the **Guest's Eye** <term>Kemu|The point representing the attacker or the newcomer</term> to make a judgment.

---

In the second year of the **Quanning** era of Emperor Zhaozong of the Tang Dynasty [896 CE], a *Bingchen* year, the **Great One** <note>The Taiyi deity or celestial pivot.</note> entered the *Gengzi* Cycle, Origin 5, Bureau 5. The Great One was positioned in the Second Palace. 

The **Hidden Virtue** served as the **Host's Eye**. The **Host's Calculation** was twenty-five. The Great and Small Generals were blocked, findng no "Gates." The **Strike** spirit <term>Gujie|A secondary celestial officer associated with unexpected attacks or calamities</term> bordered the **Pitch-pipes** <term>Lülü|The twelve musical pitches which correspond to the twelve months and directions</term> at the position of *Shen* <note>The direction of West-Southwest.</note>. 

The **Guest's Calculation** was fourteen. The **Great General** was in the Fourth Palace, and the **Small General** was in the Second Palace. In this year, **Li Maozhen** of Fengxiang approached the capital with his army. The Emperor’s carriage fled to Hua Prefecture. His Majesty ordered...

### Section 48: Explaining the Dispatching of Troops and Seizing Territory

The Classic says: When dispatching an army, you must exit through "their gate" <note>The auspicious gates identified through Taiyi calculation.</note>. When seizing territory, you must use "the two" <note>This refers to the dual application of the "Host" and "Guest" positions to secure a location.</note>. The laws of war state: While the "dispatching" of an army refers to its movement, "seizing territory" refers to its placement and security.

When the military calculations reach the counts of twelve, twenty-two, or thirty-two, and the **Gates** are complete and the **Generals** are activated, then the army may be set in motion. Therefore, it is said that seizing territory requires using the two. Exiting through "their gate" refers to using the **Three Auspicious Gates**: the Gate of Opening, the Gate of Rest, and the Gate of Life.

In the fourth year of the Qianfu era of Emperor Xizong of the Tang Dynasty <note>877 CE</note>, a *Ding-You* year, the **Great One** was at the *Geng-Zi* position in the 58th Cycle. The Great One resided in the Fourth Palace, associated with **Hidden Virtue** <term>Yinde|A celestial spirit or coordinate used to determine auspicious movement and internal strength</term>.

### Section 49: Explaining the Facing and Backing of Deployed Troops

The ancient methods state: when deploying troops, they must depart from their "home" <note>This refers to the auspicious cardinal direction determined by the calculation</note>. This means that during the time of deployment, the numerical results determine the directions to face or avoid. 

*   If the calculation results in **One**: the troops should depart toward the **Northwest**.
*   If the calculation results in **Two**: the troops should depart toward the **due South**.
*   If the calculation results in **Three**: the troops should depart toward the **Northeast**.
*   If the calculation results in **Four**: the troops should depart toward the **due East**.
*   If the calculation results in **Six**: the troops should depart toward the **due West**.
*   If the calculation results in **Seven**: the troops should depart toward the **Southwest**.
*   If the calculation results in **Eight**: the troops should depart toward the **due North**.
*   If the calculation results in **Nine**: the troops should depart toward the **Southeast**.

What is called "departing from the home" refers to the principle of choosing the proper direction when first exiting the gates.

*   **Water** formations are curved or winding.
*   **Fire** formations are sharp or pointed.
*   **Wood** formations are straight.
*   **Metal** formations are square.
*   **Earth** formations are round. 

These are all chosen according to the sequence of the **Host** and **Guest** in the Fifth...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of military deployment, transitioning from the historical example of Huang Chao to a theoretical discourse on how the physical environment dictates the geometry of battle. **Complete Essentials for the Military Classics**, Volume 8
Page 8

When formations are set in opposition to one another, commanders often focus only on the balance of victory and defeat, remaining unaware that the method for deploying an army must be derived from the terrain. 

If the rear is high and the front is low, one should utilize a **Pointed Formation** <term>Ruizhen|A wedge-shaped deployment designed to concentrate force at a single point for a breakthrough</term>; this is advantageous for advancing into battle to shatter the enemy. If the front is high and the rear is low, it is not suitable to advance the troops; instead, it is advantageous to deploy nearby in a **Straight Formation** <term>Zhizhen|A linear deployment used to present a solid front and stabilize a position</term> to defend and exhaust the enemy. 

If the terrain is sloped or uneven, making it inconvenient for maneuvering, one should deploy a **Circular Formation** <term>Yuanzhen|A 360-degree defensive posture with no exposed flanks, ideal for holding difficult ground</term> to maintain a firm defense. If the terrain is high but uneven, allowing for movement in all directions, a **Square Formation** <term>Fangzhen|A four-sided deployment that allows for balanced combat and easy redirection of forces</term> should be used to facilitate fighting. If there are high ridges to the left and right, one should use a **Curved Formation** <term>Quzhen|A formation with "wings" or a concave center designed to envelop or 'swallow' an incoming enemy</term> to swallow the foe. 

When the terrain aligns with the direction of the troops' movement, it is auspicious; when the terrain opposes that direction, one must occupy it and adapt to the land. Now, as the **Great One's** <term>Taiyi|The supreme celestial deity and the complex system of mathematical divination used to predict cosmic and earthly events</term> military methods arise from the alignment of direction and the formation arises from the land, observing the location to control the transformation is what we call "divine." This is truly the Divine Way of employing troops.

### Section 51: Explaining the Adaptation of Formations Based on Topography

Chao Cuo <note>Chao Cuo (c. 200–154 BCE) was a prominent statesman and advisor of the Han Dynasty known for his strategic writings on border defense.</note> said: In the employment of troops and the urgency of clashing blades, there are three critical factors. First is obtaining the advantage of the terrain; second is the thorough training and obedience of the soldiers; third is the sharpness and quality of the weapons. 

The laws of war state: ditches five feet deep, waters that gradually submerge a chariot, mountain forests, piled rocks, winding rivers, hills and mounds, and places thick with grass and trees—this is the terrain for **Infantry** <term>Bubing|Foot soldiers who rely on maneuverability in broken or cluttered environments</term>. In such places, two units of chariots or cavalry are not the match for one unit of infantry. 

Where hills and mounds roll into one another, and plains and wide wildernesses stretch out—this is the terrain for **Chariots and Cavalry** <term>Cheqi|Mobile, mounted forces that require open space to build momentum</term>. In such places, ten units of infantry are not the match for one unit of cavalry. 

Where hills are far apart with river valleys lying between them, and one can look down from a height to strike below—this is the terrain for **Bows and Crossbows** <term>Gongnu|Projective weapon units that rely on line-of-sight and elevation</term>. In such places, a hundred units equipped with short-range weapons are not the match for one unit of archers. 

Where two formations are close together on flat ground with short grass, allowing for movement forward or backward—this is the terrain for **Long Halberds** <term>Changji|Pole-arms used to keep the enemy at a distance in open melee</term>. Swords and shields...

In such conditions, three men are not the equal of one. Where reeds, bamboo, and dense brush grow in tangled thickets with branches and leaves interlocking, this is "Spear and Shaft Terrain" <note>original: 矛鋌之地也. This refers to overgrown areas where long-handled weapons like spears become entangled and useless.</note>. These are the faults of failing to discipline and train the troops.

One hundred are not the equal of ten when weapons are not sharp and effective—this is the same as being empty-handed. When armor is not sturdy and dense, it is the same as being bare-chested. When crossbows cannot reach a distance, they are the same as short-range weapons. When archers cannot hit their marks, it is the same as having no arrows. When arrows hit but cannot penetrate, it is the same as having no arrowheads. These are the disasters caused by a general who does not inspect the weaponry.

Thus, five are not the equal of one. As the *Art of War* <term>Bingfa|Military strategies and treatises</term> states: If the equipment is not effective, you are giving your soldiers to the enemy. If the soldiers cannot be used, you are giving your general to the enemy. If the general does not understand warfare, you are giving your sovereign to the enemy. If the sovereign does not choose his generals wisely, he is giving his state to the enemy. These four are the ultimate essentials of military affairs.

## 52. Interpreting the Great One in the Palaces of the Heavenly Outer and Earthly Inner

To understand the "Heavenly Outer" <term>Tianwai|The outer circuit of the Taiyi divination board, favoring the 'Guest' or attacker</term> and the "Earthly Inner" <term>Deinei|The inner circuit of the Taiyi divination board, favoring the 'Host' or defender</term> Palaces, there are fixed rules within the Eight Trigrams:

Look to 9, 2, 7, and 6 for the Heavenly Outer.
1, 8, 3, and 4 are the Earthly Inner.
The Inner assists the Host <note>The 'Host' (Zhu) usually represents the Emperor, the defender, or the home territory.</note>; the Outer assists the Guest <note>The 'Guest' (Ke) represents the invader, the challenger, or the traveling party.</note>.
If the Gates and Generals are all aligned, it brings great good fortune and profit.

The ancient methods state: When the **Great One** <term>Taiyi|The 'Great One' or 'Great Unity,' the supreme celestial deity and the pivot of this divination system</term> resides in the 1st, 8th, 3rd, or 4th Palaces, these are the Earthly Inner Palaces, which assist the Host. When the **Great One** resides in the 9th, 2nd, 7th, or 6th Palaces, it is...

If the alignment aids the Host and they desire to engage in battle, then the Host must not be the first to act; if they do, the Guest will be the one to maintain a firm defense.

If you wish to act as the Host <note>the defender</note>, wait for the Great One to reside in the Palace Inside Earth. ## Section 53: Explaining the Overlapping Battle Signs of the Great One: Wind, Clouds, and Flying Birds

The Classic says: In the methods of combat, one must observe the momentum of the wind and clouds and examine the disposition of flying birds. 

If wind, clouds, or flying birds come to clash with, block, or strike the Palace of the Great One, it signifies a great defeat. If they strike the palace of the Guest’s Grand General, the Guest shall be defeated. If they strike the palace of the Host’s General, the Host shall be defeated. 

If these omens come from the direction of the Host’s "Punishment" position <note>original: 刑. A technical astrological point representing conflict or penalty</note>, the Host will be defeated. If they come from the Guest’s "Punishment" position, the Guest will be defeated. 

If they move from the Host’s "Eye" <note>original: 目. A calculation point in the Taiyi system used to "see" the opponent’s weaknesses</note> to strike the palace of the Guest’s Grand General, the Guest shall be defeated. If they move from the Guest’s "Eye" to strike the palace of the Host’s Grand General, the Host shall be defeated.

If wind, clouds, or flying birds come from the directions of the Grand Duke <term>Taisui: The "Great Year," an influential celestial position associated with the planet Jupiter</term>, the Great Yin <note>the Moon</note>, or the Monthly Establisher <note>the branch associated with the current lunar month</note> to strike the Host’s formation, then the Host is defeated; if they strike the Guest’s formation, the Guest is defeated. 

If wind, clouds, or flying birds come to flank the front of the Host’s formation like wings, the Host will be victorious. If they come to flank the Guest’s formation, the Guest will be victorious. 

If, on the day of battle, a whirlwind rises or birds circle over the formation, or if a flagpole snaps, these are omens of a catastrophic defeat. If a multitude—

<summary>This page explains the strategic timing for attackers and defenders based on the Great One’s cosmic position and details how natural phenomena like wind and birds serve as battlefield omens.</summary>

If the wings of birds flap and cry in unison with the wind and clouds, and these forces come clashing toward the battle lines: should they clash against the Host's <note>the defender</note> formation, the Host shall be defeated. Should they clash against the Guest's <note>the attacker</note> formation, the Guest shall be defeated.

# Section 54: Explaining Surprise Ambush

The Classic says: One who can recognize the "Surprise and Hidden" <note>original: 奇伏 (qifu). This refers to the art of ambush and the use of unconventional forces.</note> can turn misfortune into blessing. This is the essential meaning of the "Surprise Force" <term>Qi Bing: In traditional Chinese strategy, the 'Qi' (unconventional/surprise) forces work in tandem with the 'Zheng' (standard/orthodox) forces to achieve victory.</term>. The method of the Surprise Force uses the number "three" as its rule: if the army consists of one hundred men, thirty shall be the Surprise Force; if one thousand men, three hundred shall be the Surprise Force; if ten thousand men, three thousand shall be the Surprise Force. They should follow the terrain to hide their forms and must not maintain a fixed position.

The ancients said: The Surprise Force must always emerge from the position of the "Great Slaughter" <term>Da Sha: A directional deity or cosmic influence in Taiyi divination associated with extreme military power and destruction.</term>. When the terrain is high and situated three to five *li* <note>approximately 1.5 to 2.5 kilometers</note> from the enemy, the troops should then beat their drums, raise a shout, and launch the ambush.

The area directly beneath the "Heavenly Eye" <term>Tianmu: A specific technical marker in the Taiyi cosmic layout used to determine the direction of initiative and observation.</term> is precisely where the Great Slaughter resides.

The Classic says: To hide away and conceal one's tracks, one must reside in "One." This means that an ambush must be calculated to occur during the times of 11, 21, or 31 <note>These numbers refer to specific cycles or "years" within the complex mathematical progression of the Taiyi system.</note>. At such times, hide within mountain forests or deep ravines where people cannot see. It is also said: Ambush troops must be utilized during "Enveloping and Pressing" <term>Yanpo: A specific technical state in Taiyi divination where certain markers 'envelop' or 'press' the Great One, indicating an opportune moment for a sudden strike.</term> times.

For instance, if the Great One <note>Taiyi</note> is in the Sixth Palace, one must use the hours of the Monkey, Rooster, or Dog <note>original: 申酉戌時. These correspond to late afternoon and evening, roughly 3:00 PM to 9:00 PM.</note> to achieve victory. If one wishes to hide troops to await the enemy for a surprise raid, one must choose the "Enveloping and Pressing" time of the Great One to deploy the army. If the enemy is already near, then the troops should be hidden at strategic "vital gates" <note>chokepoints or essential terrain features</note>.

# The Jingyou Era Classic of the Great One’s Blessed Response, Volume 5

## Section 55: The Sovereign Base of the Great One

The <term>Sovereign Base of the Great One</term><note>Junji Taiyi: One of the three fundamental "bases" in Taiyi astrology, specifically representing the Emperor and the central government.</note> is the celestial symbol of the human ruler. Therefore, it governs the affairs of the nation. If a ruler can successfully defend their territory, they will remain a sovereign lord until the end. If they cannot defend their borders, they will find themselves clad in armor, fleeing as a fugitive. 

It is said that in whatever land the Sovereign Base currently resides, the ruler of that land should cultivate their virtue so that it aligns with the <term>Way of Heaven</term><note>Tian Dao: The natural and moral order of the universe.</note> above and harmonizes with the hearts of the people below. Through such teaching and transformation, peace will prevail, the people will enjoy longevity and blessings, the soldiers will be strong, and the generals will be brave; people from both near and far will submit to their rule. 

However, if the ruler recklessly initiates <term>forced labor</term><note>Yaoyi: Corvée labor, where commoners were required to provide unpaid work for state projects.</note>, delights in military conflict, or deviously imposes heavy taxes to build sprawling palaces, then floods, droughts, and disasters will strike. Warfare and epidemics will arrive simultaneously. On the day the Sovereign Base enters a land, the ruler should avoid presenting meat offerings and instead offer humble petitions to serve the <term>Sacred Self</term><note>Shengong: A respectful reference to the Emperor's person.</note>. The ruler should inhabit that region or travel to that direction to follow the Way of Heaven; doing so will bring good fortune.

---

To calculate its position: Set the <term>High Epoch</term><note>Shangyuan: The start of the grandest cycle in Chinese chronology, used as a fixed starting point for astronomical calculations.</note> at the Wood Tiger year (*Jiayin*). Count the accumulated years up to the desired year. For the present first year of the <term>Jingyou Era</term><note>The Jingyou era (1034–1038 AD) during the reign of Emperor Renzong of the Song Dynasty. The year mentioned here, Jiaxu, corresponds to 1034 AD.</note>—the Wood Dog year (*Jiaxu*)—the total count is 424,040 years. 

When examining the past, subtract one year for every year prior; when predicting the future, add one year for every year forward. The accumulated years for the Three Bases and the Five Blessings are all calculated using this same total. 

The Sovereign Base completes a "Great Cycle" every 360 years. Cast off the multiples of 360 from the total. With the remainder, divide by 30 to determine the "State Number." For any remainder that does not fill a full count, it represents the specific year within that state's cycle. The count begins at the position of the <term>Horse</term><note>Wu: The southern cardinal direction in the twelve earthly branches.</note> and proceeds clockwise through the twelve earthly branches. Excluding the Rabbit (*Mao*) count, it is then—

# Determining the Land where the Sovereign Base Resides and the Duration of its Stay

Ancient methods for calculating fate began at the <term>State of Xu</term><note>Xu Bang: The direction of the Northwest in the Chinese zodiac, associated with the transition between life and death.</note>. However, your servants <note>Referring to the author, Yang Weide, and his team of court astronomers.</note> now calculate its beginning from the <term>State of Wu</term><note>Wu Bang: The direction of the South. In traditional Chinese cosmology, the Emperor faces South to rule.</note>. This is because the Sovereign Base is the celestial image of the human ruler. It is said that the Sage <note>The Emperor.</note> sits <term>facing South with robes hanging and hands folded</term><note>Nanmian chuigong: A classic idiom from the Book of Changes and the Analects describing the effortless rule of a virtuous sovereign who governs through moral influence rather than force.</note> to bring order to all under Heaven. Therefore, the Sovereign Base begins at the State of Wu and moves clockwise through the twelve stations of the zodiac, as it is honorable for it to align with the Sage’s duty to follow the will of Heaven and transform the common people.

The Minister Base also begins at the State of Wu. This is based on the principle that the sovereign and his ministers support one another, working together to uphold the royal cause and govern the masses in succession. The People Base, however, begins at the State of Xu. This is to clearly show that the commoners reside below and cannot walk the same path as the Sacred Emperor and his virtuous ministers.

In the second year of the <term>Kaiyuan</term><note>714 CE.</note> era of Emperor Xuanzong of the Tang Dynasty—a <term>Jia-Yin</term><note>The 51st year of the 60-year sexagenary cycle.</note> year—the Sovereign Base entered the State of Wu, which corresponds to the region of the Eastern Capital, <term>Luoyang</term><note>Luojing: The secondary capital of the Tang Dynasty, a major cultural and economic hub.</note>. In that land, the people were wealthy and numerous, and the five grains produced a magnificent harvest. 

After ten years had passed, the Emperor personally performed sacrifices to the <term>Goddess of the Earth</term><note>Houtu: The deity governing the soil and fertility.</note> at the altar in Fenyin. Near the altar, an ancient bronze tripod was discovered. After twelve years, he performed the <term>Feng</term><note>The most prestigious imperial sacrifice, performed at Mount Tai to report the success of the dynasty to Heaven.</note> sacrifice at the Eastern Peak. Auspicious clouds followed the sun, appearing as double rings of light. He bestowed the title of "Sacred Emperor Equal to Heaven" upon the God of Mount Tai. 

At that time, in the Eastern Capital, one <term>dou</term><note>A dry measure equivalent to about 10 liters.</note> of rice cost only seven <term>wen</term><note>Copper coins.</note>. The empire recorded 7,609,565 official households. After twenty-seven years, the world was free of troubles, and the land within the four seas was extraordinarily wealthy. In the Western Capital <note>Chang'an.</note>, the price of rice did not exceed two hundred wen, and the price of silk was similarly low. Travelers could journey ten thousand miles without carrying a weapon or even bringing their own rations. Such was the state of Great Peace. It was not until the thirteenth year of the <term>Tianbao</term><note>754 CE.</note> era—a <term>Jia-Wu</term> year—that the Sovereign Base finally departed.

It cites the prosperous Kaiyuan era of the Tang Dynasty as historical proof of the peace that occurs when the Sovereign Base is properly positioned.</summary>

# The Minister Base of the Great One, Number 56

The Minister Base of the <term>Great One</term><note>Taiyi (The Great One/Great Unity): A supreme celestial deity and a pivotal point in ancient Chinese pole-star uranology, used here to refer to a specific system of divination that tracks the movement of invisible celestial "bases" through the zodiac.</note> is the symbolic image of the assistant or the high official. In whichever regional state this base resides, the people of that land may be recruited as generals or ministers; they are equipped to take on both civil and military responsibilities and are of great benefit to the Royal House.

---

To calculate its position: Take the cumulative years from the <term>Yanji Epochal Beginning</term><note>Yanji Shangyuan: The starting point of the current great astronomical cycle used in this system, beginning in a Jiayin year.</note> to the desired year. Divide this sum by the "Cycle of the Hundred Bases," which is 360, until a remainder is left. Further divide this remainder by 36. Take the resulting remainder and divide it by 3. Each unit resulting from this division represents one year spent in a station. Start the count from the <term>State of Wu</term><note>Wu Bang: The direction of the South, associated with the fire element and the peak of Yang energy.</note> and move clockwise. The resulting location is the land where the Minister Base resides, as well as the number of years it has been present in that station.

---

In the 16th year of the <term>Zhenguan era</term><note>642 AD, during the reign of Emperor Taizong of the Tang Dynasty.</note> (a Renyin year), the Minister Base was located in the South, in the division of the <term>Eastern Capital of Luoyang</term><note>Luoyang: One of the dual capitals of the Tang Dynasty, located in the central plains.</note>. During the twelve years it resided there, many significant events occurred: Prince Tai of Wei presented the *Compendium of Earth Records* <note>original: "Kua Di Zhi"; a massive 500-volume geographical survey of the empire.</note>; <term>Yan Shigu</term><note>A famous Tang scholar and linguist.</note> provided the annotations for the 130 volumes of the *Book of the Former Han*; and in the Guimao year [643 AD], the Emperor commissioned portraits of twenty-four meritorious officials to be displayed in the <term>Lingyan Pavilion</term><note>A gallery established by Emperor Taizong to commemorate the generals and officials who helped found the Tang Dynasty.</note>.

In the Jiachen year [644 AD], the Emperor addressed his attending ministers, saying: "I wish to hear of my own faults. I ask that you speak without concealment."

<term>Chu Suiliang</term><note>A high-ranking official and one of the great calligraphers of the Tang period, known for his integrity.</note>, the Cavalier Attendant-in-Ordinary, replied: "I have observed that whenever Your Majesty sees the ancients being praised for a virtue, you immediately attribute it to yourself. However, I fear this may not be the way to truly encourage open speech."

The Emperor replied: "Your words are correct. I shall certainly change my ways."

This is a clear correspondence of the "Sagely Ruler and Worthy Minister sharing the same virtue" <note>Shengjun xianchen tongde: A Confucian ideal where the monarch and his advisors work in perfect moral harmony.</note>, occurring precisely where the Minister Base was located.

<summary>This page explains the astrological significance and calculation of the Minister Base, which governs the presence of talented officials. It provides a historical example from the reign of Tang Emperor Taizong to demonstrate how the presence of this base coincides with great scholarly achievements and honest communication between the sovereign and his ministers.</summary>

# The People’s Base of the Great One, Number 57

The People’s Base of the <term>Great One</term><note>Taiyi (The Great One): The supreme celestial pivot in this divination system. Its various "bases" or astral points rotate through the geography of the empire to predict the fortunes of different social classes.</note> is the symbolic image of the common people. In whichever regional state this base resides, the people of that land will be wealthy and numerous, the five grains will ripen in abundance, and the land will be free from the calamities of war, rebellion, pestilence, or plague.

---

To calculate its position: Take the cumulative years from the <term>Yanji Epochal Beginning</term><note>Yanji Shangyuan: The starting point of the current astronomical cycle, which begins in a Jiayin year.</note> to the desired year. Divide this sum by 360 and take the remainder. Divide that remainder again by 12. With the final remainder, start the count from the stations of <term>Xu and Hai</term><note>Xu (Dog) and Hai (Pig): These correspond to the Northwest direction in the traditional Chinese duodecimal cycle.</note>, moving in clockwise order. The station where the count ends is the regional state where the People’s Base is currently located.

---

In the first year of the <term>Zhenguan Era</term><note>627 CE, the start of the reign of Emperor Taizong of the Tang Dynasty, often regarded as a golden age of Chinese history.</note> of Emperor Taizong of the Tang (a <term>Dinghai</term> year), the People’s Base resided in the division of <term>Qin</term><note>Qin Fen: The astrological region corresponding to the ancestral lands of the Qin state, primarily the Guanzhong plain (modern Shaanxi).</note>. In the first month of that year, on the New Moon of the <term>Yiyou</term> day, a general amnesty was declared for the empire, the era name was changed, and a banquet was held for the assembled ministers. 

During the feast, the musicians performed *The Prince of Qin Smashes the Frontlines*<note>Original: "Qinwang Pozhen Yue." A famous grand-scale military dance celebrating Taizong's victories before he became Emperor.</note>. The Emperor remarked: "In the past, I was entrusted with the command of military expeditions. This music was composed among the common people. Although it does not represent the peaceful harmony of <term>Civil Virtue</term><note>Wende: The Confucian ideal of governing through culture, ethics, and ritual rather than force.</note>, it is the source from which my great achievements arose. Therefore, I have included it in the musical repertoire, for one must never forget one's roots."

The official <term>Feng Deyi</term><note>A high-ranking advisor known for his tactical mind, though sometimes criticized for flattery.</note> responded: "Your Majesty’s divine martial prowess pacified all within the four seas. How could mere Civil Virtue compare to such merit?"

The Emperor replied: "To suppress disorder, one uses military force; to maintain success, one uses culture. The ways of the <term>Military and the Civil</term><note>Wu and Wen: The two pillars of traditional Chinese statecraft—the martial power to conquer and the cultural refinement to rule.</note> each have their proper time. For you to suggest that 'letters and culture' are not as important as 'bow-cases and quivers'<note>Original: "Jiangao," referring to military equipment. Taizong is rebuking the idea that military strength is the only measure of a state's worth.</note>—these words of yours go too far." 

Feng Deyi bowed in apology and said: "I am foolish and was not wise enough to understand this." 

This illustrates how, when the People’s Base is present in a land, a virtuous ruler will respond to its influence.

# The Five Blessings Great One, Number 58

<summary>This page defines the "People's Base" as an omen of prosperity and health for the populace and provides a historical anecdote featuring Emperor Taizong of Tang to illustrate how the base's presence in a region corresponds with balanced and virtuous governance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the Taiyi divination system, moving from the People’s Base (Minji) discussed on the previous page to the "Five Blessings Great One." This specific aspect of the Great One system focuses on long-term eras of prosperity and the geographical movement of "blessings" across the empire.</meta>

### The Traveling Palaces of the Five Blessings Great One

The Classic says: Regarding the **Traveling Palaces**<note>Xinggong: Literally "temporary palaces," these represent the stations or positions the celestial deity occupies during its cycle.</note> of the **Five Blessings Great One**:

1.  The first is the **Yellow Secret Palace**, located in the Northwest, in the lands of the <term>Qian</term><note>The "Qian" trigram in the Bagua (Eight Trigrams) corresponds to the Northwest direction.</note> direction.
2.  The second is the **Yellow Beginning Palace**, located in the Northeast, in the lands of <term>Liaodong</term><note>Liaodong: A historical region in Northeast China; the direction corresponds to the "Gen" trigram.</note> and the <term>Gen</term> direction.
3.  The third is the **Yellow Chamber Palace**, located in the East, in the lands of the <term>Xun</term><note>The "Xun" trigram corresponds to the Southeast, though the text associates it here with the Eastern sector.</note> direction.
4.  The fourth is the **Yellow Court Palace**, located in the West, in the lands of the <term>Kun</term><note>The "Kun" trigram corresponds to the Southwest.</note> direction.
5.  The fifth is the **Yellow Mystery Palace**, located in the lands of the Imperial Capital, within <term>Yuzhou</term><note>Yuzhou: Traditionally considered the "Central Province" of the ancient Nine Provinces of China, representing the heart of the empire.</note>.

At the establishment of the <term>Yanji Epochal Beginning</term><note>Yanji Shangyuan: The starting point of the current astronomical cycle used in this system, which began in a Jiayin (Wood Tiger) year.</note> in a <term>Jiayin</term> year, the Five Blessings Great One was located in the **Yellow Secret Palace**. After forty-five years, it enters the **Yellow Beginning Palace**. It moves in a forward (clockwise) direction, completing its cycle and then beginning again.

In the lands where the **Five Blessings** reside, the sovereign possesses fortunate virtue, and the people are wealthy and enjoy long lives. There are no wars, military conflicts, droughts, floods, disasters, or diseases.

---

#### Method of Calculation

To determine the location: Take the accumulated years from the **Yanji Epochal Beginning** in the <term>Jiayin</term> year to the year sought. Divide this by the **Five Blessings Great Cycle** of 225 years and discard the remainder. Take the remaining years and divide by 45 to determine the palace number. For any years left over that do not fill a full 45-year count, these represent the current year within the palace.

The sequence of the palaces begins at <term>Qian</term> (Northwest), then <term>Gen</term> (Northeast), then <term>Xun</term> (Southeast), then <term>Kun</term> (Southwest), and finally the **Central Palace**. Following this calculation, one can find where the Five Blessings reside and the number of years it has occupied that palace.

---

#### Historical Evidence: The Tang Dynasty

In the seventh year of the **Wude** era of <term>Emperor Gaozu</term><note>Emperor Gaozu: The founder of the Tang Dynasty. The 7th year of Wude corresponds to 624 CE.</note> of the Tang (a <term>Jiashen</term> year), the Five Blessings Great One entered the **Central Palace**, which corresponds to the division of **Luoyang**.

By the year <term>Bingxu</term><note>626 CE.</note> in the eighth month, the throne was passed to <term>Emperor Taizong</term><note>Taizong: The second emperor of the Tang, widely considered one of China's greatest rulers.</note>. By the fourth year of the **Zhenguan** era <note>630 CE.</note>, throughout the entire empire, only twenty-nine people were sentenced to death. Law enforcement reached a state where "punishments were set aside"<note>original: "刑措" (xingcuo). An idealized historical state where laws are so well-respected and the era so prosperous that punishments are no longer necessary.</note>. People did not lock their doors at night, and travelers did not need to carry their own rations. The empire was governed with great order; one <term>dou</term><note>Dou: A unit of dry measure, roughly 10 liters.</note> of rice cost only three <term>wen</term><note>Wen: Small copper coins (cash).</note>.

This was all because the Five Blessings had arrived in that division. It is the celestial response to the abundance, joy, and Great Peace of the common people.

It cites the early Tang Dynasty's prosperity as historical proof of the blessings brought when the deity occupies the Central Palace.</summary>

# Chapter 59: The Great Wandering Great One

The Classic says: The **Great Wandering Great One**<note>The "Great Wandering" (Dayou) is a specific cycle within the Taiyi system that lasts 288 years, moving through the eight directions to oversee terrestrial affairs.</note> patrols through the eight directions. Its oversight and governance are conducted in the same palaces as the **Small Wandering Great One**<note>The Small Wandering (Xiaoyou) is a shorter-term cycle that works in tandem with the Great Wandering.</note>. One "Examination" period lasts thirty-six years: for twelve years it governs Heaven, for twelve years it governs Earth, and for twelve years it governs Humanity. It examines and weighs the good and evil deeds of those living within its influence. A full cycle of its rewards and punishments is completed every 288 years.

Therefore, when the Great One resides in a **Yang Palace**, there will be no warfare in **Liaodong**<note>Liaodong: A strategic region in Northeast China. In this system, certain directions are designated as "Yang" (active/light) and others as "Yin" (receptive/dark).</note>. When the Great One resides in a **Yin Palace**, one can find safety and preserve their life in **Shuhan**<note>Shuhan: A region corresponding to modern-day Sichuan in Southwest China.</note>.

If the Great One is in sectors eight, three, four, or nine, these are considered **Yang Palaces**; thus, the territory of Liaodong remains free of soldiers. If it is in sectors two, seven, six, or one, these are considered **Yin Palaces**; thus, one may dwell in peace within the territory of Western Shu.

### Calculation Method
To find the position, take the number of accumulated years from the **Epochal Beginning**<term>Yanji Shangyuan: The "Grand Inception of the Calculation Era," a theoretical starting point in 10,153,917 BCE used by Song dynasty astronomers for these cycles.</term> in the year of the <term>Wood Tiger</term><note>Jiayin: The 51st year of the 60-year sexagenary cycle.</note> up to the desired year. Divide this by the **Great Wandering Epochal Constant** of 4,320. The remainder represents the years elapsed since the current era began.

Next, divide that remainder by the **Great Wandering Minor Cycle Constant** of 288. With the new remainder, divide by the **Great One Palace Transit Rate** of 36. Any remaining amount that does not reach 36 represents the number of years the deity has spent within its current palace. The count of the palaces begins at Palace Seven and proceeds in order through the eight palaces, skipping the **Central Palace Five**<note>In the nine-palace grid (Lo Shu), the center is often skipped in "wandering" calculations as it represents the pivot point rather than a directional destination.</note>. Counting outward in this manner, you will find the current palace and year-count of the Great Wandering Great One.

In the first year of the **Tianyou era** of **Emperor Zhaozong of Tang**<note>904 AD, during the final years of the Tang Dynasty.</note>, a *Jiazi* year, the Small Wandering entered the **Sixth Palace**, which corresponds to the region of **Yongzhou**<note>Yongzhou (modern Shaanxi) was the administrative region containing the capital, Chang'an.</note>.
