He further ordered **Jiang Xuan** to force the civil and military officials to march east. As the city’s residents were driven out and displaced, the sound of their wailing filled the roads. The palaces, government offices, and private residences were dismantled so their timber could be floated down the Wei and Yellow Rivers. **Chang’an** was thus left a hollow ruin. The Emperor and his officials wept for over a month without ceasing. 

Because the place where the Small Wandering resides is a land of **Examination and Governance**<note>Kaozhi (Examination and Governance): A period when the heavens judge the conduct of the people and rulers of a specific region.</note>, it inevitably responds with warfare, funerals, floods, droughts, displacement, or famine, ultimately leading to the loss of that territory.

In the first year of the **Changxing era** of **Emperor Mingzong of the Later Tang**<note>930 AD, during the chaotic Five Dynasties and Ten Kingdoms period.</note>, the Great One entered the **Seventh Palace**, which corresponds to the regions of **Liang and Yi**<note>Ancient administrative divisions covering modern-day Shaanxi and Sichuan.</note>. From then on, the military forces of Western Sichuan caused continuous disturbances for years. By the fourth year of the era, Meng Zhixiang was appointed the Military Governor of both the Eastern and Western **Jiannan** circuits and was enfeoffed as the **King of Shu**. Thus, the central government eventually lost control of the western territories. This occurred because the Palace of the **Great Wandering** had issued a 'punishment' upon the region, yet the Han <note>meaning the central Chinese authority</note> was able to preserve its integrity elsewhere.

### The Four Spirits Great One, Number Sixty-Two

This spirit resides near the south of the Great One and is also known as the **Sixth Spirit**. It commands sixteen subordinate spirits. Depending on the direction it faces, it governs occurrences of floods, droughts, military conflicts, famines, and plagues. The *Classic* says: The **Four Spirits Great One** is specifically the **Water Spirit**. It presides over the 'Constitutional Bonds' <note>the fundamental laws and moral order of a dynasty.</note>. In an era where the Way is followed, it brings prosperity; in an era without the Way, it brings calamity. If it descends upon a region governed by a ruler lacking in virtue, then rebels and bandits will arise in abundance, and disasters such as floods, droughts, war, famine, plague, and the displacement of the people will respond to this lack of virtue. This spirit's influence is strongest during the three months of winter.

To calculate its position, take the number of years elapsed since the **Grand Epoch**<term>Grand Epoch (Yanji Shangyuan): The theoretical starting point of the Chinese calendar where all cycles began together at the first hour of a Jiazi day.</term> until the desired year. Divide this by the **Great Cycle** of 180. With the remainder, divide by 30...

### The Heavenly One Great One, Section 63

If military conflicts persist for many years, the arrival of the **Heavenly One** brings victory or defeat, as he is the arbiter of decisive outcomes. He presides over funerals and the death of soldiers, and his power is at its peak during the three months of **autumn**.

### The Earthly One Great One, Chapter 64

The Classic states: The **Earthly One**<term>Earthly One (Diyi): A spirit associated with the element of Earth; it governs the stability of the land, agricultural fertility, and the welfare of the common people.</term> is the deity of the soil. If it approaches a realm that lacks the "Way" <note>The "Way" (Dao) here refers to moral governance and political legitimacy.</note>, the five grains will fail to ripen, the common people will be forced into exile, and disasters such as earthquakes, mountain collapses, and plagues of insects will arise. It normally resides in the "Center Five" palace and rules during the four seasonal transitions. The method for its "entry into the cycle" is the same as that of the **Four Spirits**. The naming of its position begins from the Ninth Palace and proceeds in order; the **Crimson Palace**, **Bright Hall**, and **Jade Hall** follow thereafter. The final calculation identifies the palace where the Earthly One resides and the year count.

The following year, **Meng Zhixiang** usurped the title of Emperor in Yizhou, establishing a platform for his officials and changing the era name to *Mingde*. He passed away in the eighth month of that year, and his son, **Meng Chang**, succeeded him. This clearly illustrates the significance of the Earthly One’s location regarding shifts in regional power.

# The Directing Talisman Great One, Chapter 65

The **Directing Talisman Great One**<term>Directing Talisman Great One (Zhifu Taiyi): The third of the three primary spirits in this system, associated with the element of Fire and acting as an inspector or envoy for the celestial bureaucracy.</term> is a fire deity. It serves as the envoy of the Celestial Emperor, sent by the Heavens to observe the "Way of Governance" and to audit the conduct of the myriad people. If it approaches a state that lacks the Way—where leadership is corrupt or failing—it brings about the omens of war, floods, droughts, famines, epidemics, locust outbreaks, and strange disasters. It reigns supreme during the three months of summer.

To calculate its entry into the chronological year-count, use the same method as the **Four Spirits**<note>The Four Spirits (Sishen) are another group of celestial regulators whose movements are calculated using a specific divisor to determine their influence over long cycles of time.</note>. Divide the total year count by three. The remainder represents the base number; any surplus beyond that indicates the number of years that have passed since the spirit entered its current palace. The assignment of these palace numbers begins from the **Fifth Palace** and moves in sequence through the **Nine Palaces**. It is then connected to the **Three Palaces**<note>Referring to the extra-mural palaces: the Crimson Palace, Bright Hall, and Jade Hall, as described on the previous page.</note>. The final result reveals the specific palace where the Directing Talisman Great One resides and the year-count of its presence there.

In the first year of the **Taiping Xingguo** era of Emperor **Taizong** of the Song Dynasty (976 CE), a *Bingzi* year, the Directing Talisman Great One entered the **Ninth Palace**, which corresponds to the terrestrial division of **Yangzhou**<note>In traditional Chinese cosmography, the land is divided into regions corresponding to celestial sectors. Yangzhou covered much of the southeastern coastal region.</note>. In this year, the **King of Wuyue**<note>Qian Chu (r. 947–978), the last king of the Wuyue Kingdom, who eventually surrendered his territory to the Song Dynasty to ensure a peaceful transition.</note>, along with his wife and son, entered the imperial court to pay homage. Their territory was entirely within the division of Yangzhou. This clearly demonstrates that in the land where the Directing Talisman resides, the ruler will submit to an enlightened master; those with virtue shall flourish in response to the spirit’s presence.

# General Preface to the Seven Arts of the Great One

The "Seven Arts of the <term>Great One</term><note>Taiyi (太乙): The supreme celestial principle or deity in this system, whose movements across the heavens govern the fate of kingdoms and the outcome of wars.</note>" were once bestowed by the <term>Mysterious Lady</term><note>Xuannü (玄女): The Mysterious Lady of the Nine Heavens, a Taoist goddess of war, sex, and longevity who traditionally aids heroes in times of crisis.</note> upon the <term>Yellow Emperor</term><note>Huangdi (黃帝): The legendary ancestral sovereign of China, credited with the invention of the calendar and the compass.</note>. Having obtained them, he defeated <term>Chiyou</term><note>A mythical tribal leader and rival to the Yellow Emperor, often associated with the invention of metal weapons.</note> at the Battle of <term>Zhuolu</term><note>A legendary battlefield where the foundations of Chinese civilization were purportedly established.</note>; this was the very art he used. 

Later, <term>Fan Li</term><note>A famous advisor to King Goujian of Yue (c. 5th century BCE), known for his strategic brilliance in the war against the State of Wu.</note> obtained them to assist the state of <term>Yue</term> in pacifying <term>Wu</term>, eventually establishing a hegemony. When the <term>Qin</term> dynasty lost its grip on the world and the <term>Han</term> arose, <term>Zhang Liang</term><note>One of the "Three Heroes of the early Han," a master strategist who helped Liu Bang win the empire.</note> used them to assist <term>Pei Gong</term><note>The title of Liu Bang, the first emperor of the Han dynasty, before he took the throne.</note>. Moving west into the <term>Guanzhong</term> pass, <term>Ziying</term><note>The last ruler of the Qin dynasty.</note> surrendered while holding the ritual jade disk in his mouth; moving east to the <term>Battle of Gaixia</term>, he broke the vanguard of <term>Xiang Yu</term><note>The "Hegemon-King of Western Chu," a tragic hero and the primary rival of the Han founder.</note>. 

If one obtains this book, they may serve as a minister and a mentor to emperors within the court, or as a commander and great strategist on the battlefield. Whether attacking or defending, the crucial moment must not be missed. This is because the method consists of seven distinct arts.

---

## Section 66: The Art of Asking the Way at the Front Line

The Classic states: In the art of "Asking the Way at the Front Line" <note>This refers to a method of divination performed immediately before an engagement or at the edge of a body of water to determine the enemy's vulnerability.</note>, one always takes <term>Lü Shen</term><note>Lü Shen (呂申): A technical celestial coordinate in Taiyi divination, sometimes associated with the "Traveling Deity" that moves through the hexagrams.</note> and adds it to the year the enemy army mobilized. Observe the position where the <term>Great Deity</term><note>Dashen (大神): A powerful spirit in the Taiyi system that governs specific directions and temporal cycles.</note> resides; this is designated as the "Broken Year." 

Then, place <term>Lü Shen</term> at the position of the Great Deity in that "Broken Year"; this is the "Broken Month." 

Again, place <term>Lü Shen</term> at the position of the Great Deity in that "Broken Month"; this is the "Broken Day." 

Finally, place <term>Lü Shen</term> at the position of the Great Deity in that "Broken Day"; this is the "Broken Hour."

->The location where the enemy army rises marks the Broken Hour;<-
->If the Broken Month is in direct opposition, have no doubt.<-
->With four "Broken Years" in front and four "Days" behind—<-
->When all four "Broken" markers meet, examine the details with care.<-

Consider the year the enemy rises: look for the hour of destruction, the month of opposition, the four destructions of the year, and the four destructions of the day. Wherever it reaches a state of <term>Exhaustion</term><note>Fei (廢): A state in five-element theory where an element is completely spent or "waste," indicating a period of extreme weakness or failure.</note>, that is the point of destruction.

For example, if the enemy's troops rise in a <term>Jiazi</term><note>The first year of the sixty-year sexagenary cycle.</note> year, take the <term>Heavenly Plate</term><note>Tianpan (天盤): The rotating upper disc of a cosmic board (divination tool) representing the movements of the heavens.</note> and add the "hour of destruction" to the *Zi* (Rat) year. When it reaches the *Mao* (Rabbit) month, the *Wu* (Horse) day, and the *You* (Rooster) hour, and the time added reaches the *Zi* (Rat) position, then the destruction occurs in the *Mao* year, the fifth month, on the *You* day at the *Zi* hour. Then, place <term>Lu Shen</term><note>A specific spirit or computational officer in the Taiyi system used to track strategic advantages.</note> over the *Jiazi* position and the <term>Great General</term><note>Dashen (大神): Also known as the Great Spirit, a primary celestial officer in Taiyi divination that governs military movements.</note> over the *Mao* month. If Lu Shen is placed over *Mao*, the Great General will oversee *Wu*. Thus, one knows the enemy shall be destroyed in the *Dingmao* year, fifth month, *You* day, at the *Zi* hour.

## 67. The Lion’s Back-Leap

The "Lion’s Back-Leap" method always places Lu Shen over the year the enemy rose to power. One must then observe the position of the Great General and look down toward the "Year of Destruction." If the destruction falls within the <term>Four Corners</term><note>Siwei (四維): The four diagonal directions—Northeast, Southeast, Southwest, and Northwest—associated with the transitional "gateways" of the compass.</note>, it will take eighteen years for the destruction to be complete. If it falls under the "Prosperous" or "Minister" phases, the enemy is difficult to break. If it falls under the phases of "Rest," "Prison," "Death," or "Grave," the enemy is easily broken.

For example, if the enemy raises troops in a <term>Jiaxu</term><note>The 11th year of the sixty-year cycle.</note> year, place Lu Shen over the *Xu* (Dog) position. The Fire Spirit then oversees *Chou* (Ox), which belongs to the <term>Gen</term><note>The trigram associated with the Northeast direction and the element of Earth.</note> trigram in the Northeast Corner.

### White Clouds Rolling Through the Sky, Chapter 68

"White Clouds Rolling Through the Sky" refers to a situation where two armies are facing one another. In such a case, one must calculate the <term>Taiyi</term><note>The Great One (太乙): A complex form of divination used by the imperial court to predict grand events, weather, and military outcomes.</note> to determine the advantages for the <term>Host and Guest</term><note>Zhu Ke (主客): In military divination, the "Host" is usually the defending or home army, while the "Guest" is the invading or attacking army.</note>. One should then place <term>Lu Shen</term><note>A computational spirit or "officer" in the Taiyi system used to track shifting strategic advantages.</note> over the palace of the Great General and observe the location where the <term>Great Spirit</term><note>Da Shen (大神): A primary celestial officer on the Taiyi board that governs the strength of military movements.</note> resides. 

If the Great Spirit falls upon the hours of <term>Rest, Exhaustion, Death, or Burial</term><note>Xiu Qiu Si Mu (休囚死墓): Four of the five seasonal states in Five Element theory that indicate weakness, stagnation, or failure.</note>, then in the clash of formations and the exchange of blows, both offense and defense will suffer a great defeat. If it falls upon the signs of Approaching Office or <gloss>Capping and Girding</gloss><note>Guan Dai (冠帶): A phase in the cosmic cycle representing a young man's coming-of-age ceremony; it signifies readiness and burgeoning strength.</note>, the soldiers will fight well. If it falls upon <term>Imperial Prosperity</term><note>Di Wang (帝王): The peak phase of an element's strength; to "touch" or provoke an army in this state is considered suicidal.</note>, the enemy must not be provoked.

For example, suppose two armies face each other. The Taiyi enters the First Cycle of the <term>Jiazi</term><note>The first year of the sixty-year cycle.</note> Era, Layout One. The Taiyi is in Palace 1. <term>Military Virtue</term> serves as the <term>Heavenly Eye</term><note>Tian Mu (天目): A specific position on the Taiyi board used to calculate the "Host's" tactical awareness.</note>. The Host Count is seven. The Host Great General is in Palace 7, and the Adjutant General is in Palace 1. The <term>Initial Strike</term><note>Shi Ji (始擊): A position on the board representing the "Guest's" spearhead or vanguard.</note> falls upon the <term>Great Warrior</term>. The Guest Count is thirteen. The Guest Great General is in Palace 3, and the Adjutant General is in Palace 9. 

First, place Lu Shen over the Host Great General in Palace 7. The Great Spirit resides in Palace 1, which belongs to the element Metal. The Great Spirit itself belongs to Fire. Fire overcomes Metal. The classic says: "That which I overcome is 'Exhaustion'." This is known as the Great Spirit riding upon the Qi of Exhaustion. Since the Host's Great and Minor Generals belong to Metal, they are both defeated. 

Next, place Lu Shen over the Guest Great General in Palace 3. The Great Spirit resides in Palace 9, which belongs to Wood. Wood produces Fire. The classic says: "That which produces me is 'Minister'." This is known as the Great Spirit riding upon the Qi of the Minister. Therefore, the Guest's Great and Minor Generals will both be victorious.

### Fierce Tigers Resisting Each Other, Chapter 69 <note>original: "猛虎相拒" — This chapter title describes a stalemate or a direct confrontation between two powerful forces.</note>

When "Fierce Tigers Resist Each Other," one must observe the day the enemy establishes their camp. Place the <term>Lu Shen</term><note>A computational spirit or "officer" in the Taiyi system used to track shifting strategic advantages.</note> over the palace where the <term>Taiyi</term><note>The Great One (太乙): The supreme celestial deity in this divination system, whose movement through nine "palaces" or sectors determines cosmic favor.</note> resides. Observe where the <term>Great Spirit</term><note>Da Shen (大神): A primary celestial officer on the Taiyi board that governs the strength of military movements.</note> lands. If it lands upon a "Prosperous" or "Ministerial" position, <note>original: "王相之地" — In Five Element theory, these are the two strongest seasonal states, indicating peak energy and support.</note> that camp cannot be attacked. If it lands upon a position of "Prisoner," "Death," or "Tomb," <note>original: "囚死墓者之地" — The three weakest seasonal states, indicating stagnation, decay, or being trapped.</note> then that camp will collapse on its own without even being attacked.

For example: Suppose the enemy sets camp on a *Gengwu* day <note>The 7th day of the first 60-day cycle (*Jiazi*).</note> during the 7th Bureau of the Superior Cycle. On that day, the Great One is in the 3rd Palace. One then places the *Lu Shen* over the 3rd Palace. The Great Spirit consequently lands in the 9th Palace. Since Wood (associated with the 3rd Palace) can produce Fire (associated with the 9th Palace), the Classic states: "That which produces me is the Ministerial." This is called the Great Spirit "riding the Ministerial Qi." Such a camp cannot be attacked.

Another example: During the 34th Bureau of the *Jiazi* cycle, the enemy sets camp on a *Dingyou* day. On that day, the Great One is in the 4th Palace. One then places the *Lu Shen* over the 4th Palace. The Great Spirit lands in the 2nd Palace. The Great Spirit here meets Fire and "rides the Prosperous Qi." It likewise cannot be attacked.

If it overcomes the palace of the Lieutenant General, the same fate applies. If it overcomes the Guest's Great General, the Guest's General will die; if it overcomes their Lieutenant General, the same applies. If death does not occur, the result will be soldiers fleeing in panic or staging a mutiny.

On such a day, both the Host and the Guest may suitably deploy troops and establish camps; both will find success.

### Returning the Chariot in Silence, Chapter 72

"Returning the Chariot in Silence" refers to applying the *Lushen* calculation to the time when the enemy first arrives. If the Great Spirit lands on the palace of the enemy's arrival time and "rides" the Flourishing or Ministerial energy, there are <term>hidden troops</term><note>original: 伏兵 (Fubing) — Soldiers lying in ambush.</note> lying in wait; one must be prepared for them.

If the Great Spirit lands on a state of Resting, Trapped, Death, or Grave <note>These are the weaker states in the five-stage elemental cycle (Xiu, Qiu, Si, Mu).</note>, the enemy will defeat themselves. If it lands on your own army while in a Flourishing or Ministerial state, it is suitable for you to set an ambush for your own soldiers.

### 73. Explaining the Three Talents to Divinate Heaven, Earth, and Humanity

For example: If <term>Gaocong</term><note>Gaocong (高叢): A technical officer in the Taiyi system, here assigned the element of Wood for the calculation.</note> belongs to Wood, then use *Lushen* and *Dahao* (both being Wood) as the "King." <term>Great Righteousness</term><note>Dayi (大義): A celestial officer.</note> and the "Earth Lord" act as the "Minister." <term>Martial Virtue</term><note>Wude (武德): A celestial officer.</note>, the "Great Cluster," and <term>Hidden Virtue</term><note>Yinde (陰德): A celestial officer representing hidden or merciful influences.</note> act as "Death." "Harmonious Virtue," the "Sun," the "Heavenly Way," and the "Yin Lord" act as "Prisoners." The "Great Spirit" and "Great Martial" act as "Rest." All others follow this pattern.

> The Classic says: The "Three Talents" are Heaven, Earth, and Humanity. If the tally of a calculation does not contain a "ten" (a multiple of ten), it is called "Heavenless." This indicates that Heaven will manifest omens of change, such as the eclipsing or diminishing of the two luminaries <note>The "two luminaries" refers to the Sun and the Moon.</note>.

### Chapter 74: Explaining Long and Short Tallies to Divine Urgency and Leisure

The Classic says: Where the <term>Sky Eye</term><note>Tianmu (天目): The "Sky Eye" or "Heavenly Eye" is a key moving component in the Taiyi cosmic board used to determine the direction of action.</note> is positioned, a **Long Tally** suggests that one should be leisurely or deliberate, while a **Short Tally** suggests that one should act with urgency. A result of a "Single 9" or below is considered **Short**. A result of 11 or above is considered **Long**. 

In military matters, a Long Tally favors deep incursions into enemy territory. A Short Tally favors shallow or cautious incursions. Long indicates victory; Short indicates defeat.

In the fourth year of the Zhenguan era of Emperor Taizong of the Tang Dynasty <note>630 CE</note>, a <term>Gengyin</term> year, the Great One entered the 27th Bureau of the <term>Jiazi</term> epoch in the First Cycle. The Great One was in the 1st Palace. The deity <term>Harmonious Virtue</term><note>Hede (和德): An auspicious spirit in the Taiyi system.</note> served as the Sky Eye. The Host’s tally was 31, and the <term>First Strike</term><note>Shiji (始擊): A star/deity representing the commanding general of the "Guest" or opposing side.</note> was in the 4th Palace. The Guest’s tally was 28. In this Bureau, the Host’s tally was **Long**, which favored a deep incursion. In that year, the general Li Jing dealt a massive defeat to the <term>Xieli Khan</term><note>The leader of the Eastern Turkic Khaganate.</note>. He captured Empress Xiao of the Sui Dynasty and Yang Zhengdao <note>A grandson of the former Sui Emperor</note> and sent them back to the capital.

# Chapter 75: Explaining the Five Tones to Divine Calamities and Anomalies

The Classic says: If the calculation <note>The "Host Calculation" in a Taiyi cosmic board setup.</note> results in a **1**, it corresponds to the <term>Gong</term><note>The first note of the Chinese pentatonic scale, traditionally symbolizing the Sovereign.</note> tone. If the result is a **2**, it is a **Comparative Gong**. If there is an anomaly in the *Gong* tone, the omen concerns the **Sovereign**.

In the second year of the Yongchun era of Emperor Gaozong <note>The original text erroneously attributes this era to Emperor Gaozu; Yongchun 2 corresponds to 683 CE.</note> of the Tang Dynasty, a *Guiwei* year, the <term>Great One</term><note>Taiyi (太乙): The supreme celestial deity and the name of this divination system.</note> entered the 8th Bureau of the *Bingzi* Epoch. The Great One resided in the 3rd Palace. The "Yang Virtue" deity served as the <term>Sky Eye</term>. The Host’s calculation resulted in a **Single One**. In the twelfth month of that year, the Emperor passed away <note>Emperor Gaozong died in 683 CE.</note>, and the Crown Prince ascended the throne; he was known as Emperor Zhongzong.

The Classic says: If the calculation results in a **3**, it corresponds to the <term>Zhi</term><note>The fourth note of the pentatonic scale, associated with the Fire element and ritual matters.</note> tone. If the result is a **4**, it is a **Comparative Zhi**. If there is an anomaly in the *Zhi* tone, the omen concerns the **Ancestral Temple**.

In the 24th year of the Kaiyuan era of Emperor Xuanzong of the Tang Dynasty <note>736 CE</note>, the 41st Bureau of the *Bingzi* Epoch, the Great One resided in the 9th Palace. The Host’s calculation resulted in **fourteen** <note>In this system, the last digit is used, so 14 corresponds to the number 4.</note>, which is a **Comparative Zhi**. Because there was an anomaly concerning the Ancestral Temple, in the fifth month of that year, the Imperial Ancestral Temple was expanded to nine chambers, and the spirit tablet of Emperor Zhongzong was moved into the temple.

The Classic says: If the calculation results in a **5**, it corresponds to the <term>Yu</term><note>The fifth note of the pentatonic scale, associated with the Water element and feminine or subordinate roles.</note> tone. If the result is a **6**, it is a **Comparative Yu**. If there is an anomaly in the *Yu* tone, the omen concerns **Consorts and Empresses assuming higher status**.

In the 9th year of the Wude era of Emperor Gaozu of the Tang Dynasty <note>626 CE</note>, a *Bingxu* year, the 23rd Bureau of the *Jiazi* Epoch, the Great One resided in the 9th Palace. The Host’s calculation resulted in **sixteen**, which is a **Comparative Yu**. In that year, Emperor Taizong ascended the throne <note>Following the Xuanwu Gate Incident, where he seized power from his brothers.</note>. He dispatched the Minister of Works to announce the ascension at the Southern Suburb and declared a general amnesty, releasing over three thousand palace women. His consort, from the Lady Zhangsun clan, was instated as the **Empress**.

The Classic says: If the calculation results in a **7**, it is the <term>Shang</term> tone <note>The second note of the traditional Chinese pentatonic scale, associated with the element of Metal, the season of Autumn, and the "Minister."</note>. If the result is an **8**, it is a **Comparative Shang**. If there is an anomaly in the *Shang* tone, the omen concerns the **Children and Grandchildren** <note>In the context of the imperial household, this refers to the younger generations of the ruling family.</note>.

In the third year of the Wude era of Emperor Gaozu of the Tang Dynasty <note>620 CE</note>, a *Jiazi* year, the 17th Bureau of the *Jiazi* Epoch, the <term>Great One</term> <note>Taiyi (太乙): The supreme celestial deity and the name of this divination system.</note> was in the 7th Palace. The Host’s calculation resulted in a **Single Seven**. In that year, the Emperor's second son, the Prince of Qin <note>The future Emperor Taizong, Li Shimin.</note>, returned from pacifying Bingzhou. Following this success, the Emperor ennobled **eight imperial grandsons** as Princes.

The Classic says: If the calculation results in a **9**, it is the <term>Jue</term> tone <note>The third note of the pentatonic scale, associated with the element of Wood, the season of Spring, and the "People."</note>. If the result is a **10**, it is a **Calling Jue** <note>original: 叱角 "chì jué". This likely refers to an intensified or "shouted" version of the note.</note>. If there is an anomaly in the *Jue* tone, the omen concerns the **People** <note>The common populace or subjects of the realm.</note>.

In the second year of the Zhenming era of the Later Liang Dynasty <note>916 CE</note>, a *Renzi* year, the 25th Bureau of the *Renzi* Epoch, the Great One was in the 1st Palace. The Host’s calculation resulted in **Twenty-Nine**, which corresponds to the **Jue** tone. In the third month of that year, General Liu Xun fought a great battle against the Prince of Jin at Yuancheng. Liu's army suffered a crushing defeat; the people were struck by disease and forced into exile, and the dead were beyond counting.

## Chapter 76: Explaining Solitary and Single Numbers to Divine Success or Failure for the Host and Guest

The Classic says: The numbers **1, 3, 7, and 9** are categorized as **Single Yang**. The numbers **2, 4, 6, and 8** are categorized as **Single Yin**. The numbers **10 and 30** are categorized as **Solitary Yang**. The numbers **20 and 40** are categorized as **Solitary Yin**. The combination of Single Yang and Solitary Yang is called **Double Yang**. The combination of Single Yin and Solitary Yin is called **Double Yin**. Generally, Single Yin or Double Yin is unfavorable for the **Guest** <note>The "Guest" (客) usually represents the attacking force, the traveler, or the external party in a conflict.</note>. Single Yang or Double Yang is unfavorable for the **Host** <note>The "Host" (主) usually represents the defending force, the local ruler, or the internal party.</note>.

In the third year of the Guanghua era of the Tang Dynasty <note>900 CE. The text attributes this to Emperor Mingzong, but Mingzong reigned during the Later Tang; the Guanghua era belongs to Emperor Zhaozong of the original Tang Dynasty.</note>, a *Gengshen* year, the 9th Bureau of the *Renzi* Epoch, the Great One was in the 3rd Palace. The Host’s calculation resulted in a **Single Three**, categorized as **Single [Yang]**.

The Yang is unfavorable for the **Host** <note>In Taiyi divination, the "Host" usually represents the established authority or the side defending, while the "Guest" represents the challenger or attacker.</note>. In the eleventh month of that year, the eunuchs Liu Jishu and Wang Zhongxian, along with others, established the Prince of De, Li Yu, as the **Regent** <term>Jianguo</term> <note>A title for a temporary ruler or supervisor of the state during an interregnum or crisis.</note>. The Emperor was moved to the Eastern Palace to pay respects.

### Chapter 77: Explaining "Inner and Outer" to Divine Attacks

The Classic says: Regarding the use of "Inner and Outer" to divine attacks: what lies in front of the **Heavenly Eye** <term>Tianmu</term> <note>A pivotal moving point in the Taiyi cosmic board used to determine the direction of favor or misfortune.</note> is considered "Inner," and can be used to attack the "Outer." What lies behind it is "Outer," and can be used to attack the "Inner."

When the position of the Heavenly Eye is "Outer," it is termed **Solitary** <term>Gu</term>; when it is "Inner," it is termed **Void** <term>Xu</term>. For any undertaking, if the calculation results in the "Outer," one should aim for an external objective; the calculation for the "Outer" corresponds to the Ninth Palace <note>Associated with the south and the peak of Yang energy.</note>. If the calculation results in the "Inner," one should aim for an internal objective; the calculation for the "Inner" corresponds to the First Palace <note>Associated with the north and the beginning of Yin energy.</note>. When the deity **Yin Virtue** reaches the **Great Sun** <term>Taiyang</term>, it is considered "Inner." When the **Yin Sovereign** reaches the **Great Vastness** <term>Dahao</term>, it is considered "Outer."

In the second year of the Zhide era of Emperor Suzong of the Tang Dynasty <note>757 CE</note>, a *Dingyou* year, the 10th Bureau of the *Wuzi* Epoch, the **Great One** <term>Taiyi</term> was in the Fourth Palace. The Host’s calculation resulted in a **Single One**. In the ninth month of that year, the Prince of Guangping, Li Chu <note>The eldest son of Emperor Suzong and the future Emperor Daizong.</note>, and the general Guo Ziyi used 200,000 troops of both **Barbarian and Han** origins <note>original: 蕃漢兵 "fán hàn bīng". This refers to a mixed force including Tang regulars and allied foreign tribes, such as the Uyghurs.</note> to recapture the Western Capital <note>Chang’an, which had been fallen to the rebels.</note>. They beheaded 60,000 enemy cavalry. An Qingxu, the son of the rebel An Lushan, fled to Hebei. In the eleventh month, the Imperial Carriage returned to the capital. In the twelfth month, the Retired Emperor <note>Emperor Xuanzong, who had fled the rebellion to Sichuan.</note> returned from Shu; the Emperor traveled to Wangxian Station to welcome him.

In this configuration, **Lushen** <note>A specific name for the Heavenly Eye when it occupies certain positions on the board.</note> served as the Heavenly Eye located in the "Inner" position. This corresponds to the principle that one "can attack the Outer," and the outcome proved this.

### Chapter 78: Explaining "Greater and Lesser Numbers" to Divine Victory and Defeat

The Classic says: Regarding the use of "Greater and Lesser Numbers" to know victory or defeat: it means that if the **Guest** <note>The attacking or initiating party.</note> approaches with a greater calculation value against the **Host's** lesser calculation value, the Host will be defeated. If the Guest approaches with a lesser calculation...

### Greater Calculations Lead the Host to Victory

In the first year of the Zhide era of Emperor Suzong of the Tang Dynasty [756 CE], the **Taiyi** <term>Taiyi</term> <note>The "Great One," a personification of the cosmic axis used in high-level divination to predict state fortunes and military outcomes.</note> resided in the Third Palace. The calculation for the **Host** <term>Zhu</term> <note>Representing the established ruler or the defending side.</note> resulted in a "Single 3," while the calculation for the **Guest** <term>Ke</term> <note>Representing the challenger, invader, or rebel side.</note> resulted in 15. Within the *Kan* <note>One of the eight trigrams, associated with Water and the North.</note> seasonal configuration, the Guest won because of the higher count, and the Host was defeated because of the lower count. 

In that year, the rebel An Lushan first usurped the imperial title in the Eastern Capital [Luoyang], naming his state "Great Yan" and changing the era name to Shengwu. He appointed Zhang Tongru as Grand Secretary and Sui Xi—a man of "dog and horse" [lowly] character—as Chancellor. The regions of Guandong and Henan both fell to the rebels. Yan Gaoqing, the Prefect of Changshan, died in the conflict. This serves as historical verification that a "lesser calculation" leads to defeat and ruin.

### Chapter 79: Explaining Yin and Yang to Divine Calamities and Conjunctures

> The Classic says: When Taiyi is in a **Yang Palace** <note>Palaces 1, 3, 7, and 9 on the Taiyi board, associated with active, masculine energy.</note> and the calculation results in an odd number, it is called a "Heavy Yang Calamity" occurring in the Great [Heaven]. When Taiyi is in a **Yin Palace** <note>Palaces 2, 4, 6, and 8, associated with receptive, feminine energy.</note> and the calculation results in an even number, it is called a "Heavy Yin Calamity" occurring in the Water [Earth]. If the Host receives the calculation, the calamity falls upon the Host; if the Guest receives it, the calamity falls upon the Guest.

In the second year of the Qianfu era of Emperor Xizong of the Tang Dynasty [875 CE], a *Bingshen* year, during the 57th configuration of the *Gengzi* cycle, Taiyi resided in the Third Palace. The Host's calculation was a "Single 10." In this secondary configuration, Taiyi was in a Yang Palace and the calculation was odd <note>In Taiyi mathematics, specific residues determine "odd" or "even" status regardless of the base number; here it signifies a "Heavy Yang" omen.</note>, indicating a "Heavy Yang Calamity" in the Great. 

The calculation indicated the calamity was positioned for the Host [the Imperial government]. Subsequently, the rebels Huang Chao and Wang Xianzhi captured Caozhou and soon attacked Xiang and Ru. The "Great Qi" army <note>The name of the rebel state declared by Huang Chao.</note> rose up and took the common people captive, gathering a mass of 300,000 men. They further captured Dengzhou. The various prefectures east of the pass were powerless to resist them. This event directly responded to the divination.

### Chapter 80: Explaining the Objects of Calculation to Divine Auspiciousness and Ill Omen

The Classic states that the results of the **Calculations** <term>Suan</term> <note>The numerical values derived from the positions of the Taiyi and other celestial markers on the divination board. These numbers determine the strength of the forces involved.</note> are assigned to specific objects to clarify whether an outcome will be auspicious or ill-omened. For instance, in a calculation:
* A value of ten represents the **General**.
* A value of five represents the **Officer**.
* A value of one represents the **Soldier**.

If a calculation fails to meet these specific counts, it is considered inauspicious. However, if the total calculation is sixteen or higher <note>10 (General) + 5 (Officer) + 1 (Soldier) = 16. This represents a "complete" or fully equipped military force.</note>, then the General, the Officer, and the Soldiers are considered fully prepared. In such cases, all affairs will be auspicious.

In the tenth year of the Tianbao era of Emperor Xuanzong of the Tang Dynasty [751 CE], a *Xin-Mao* year, in the *Wu-Zi* month under the Fourth Bureau of the Origin, the **Taiyi** <term>Taiyi</term> <note>The "Great One," the central celestial deity/star used in this system of divination to predict state destiny.</note> resided in the Second Palace. The calculation for the **Host** <term>Zhu</term> <note>The "Host" typically represents the established authority or the side being defended—in this case, the Tang Imperial court.</note> was twenty-five, a state described as "Blocked and Without an Exit." The calculation for the **Guest** <term>Ke</term> <note>The "Guest" represents the challenger, the attacker, or the external force.</note> was seventeen.

In that year, the Chancellor **Yang Guozhong** <note>An influential official often blamed for the corruption and military failures that led to the An Lushan Rebellion.</note> dispatched **Xianyu Zhongtong**, the Military Governor of Jiannan, to lead a punitive expedition against the "Southern Barbarians" <note>Referring to the Nanzhao Kingdom in the southwest.</note>. The imperial army suffered a total defeat; one after another, two hundred thousand troops perished, and not a single soul returned. In this specific configuration, the **Soft-shelled Turtle Cap** <term>Bieguan</term> <note>The name of a star or a specific point on the Taiyi board; here it acts as an omen for the Guest's movements.</note> acted as the Guest and moved first. Because the Guest’s calculation reached seventeen, it fulfilled the requirement for the General, Officer, and Soldiers to be fully prepared, which was the omen for their victory over the imperial Host.

# Volume 7 of the Classic of the Great One’s Blessed Response

## Chapter 81: The Accumulated Years of the Ten Essences of the Taiyi

Calculating from the **Great Origin** <term>Shangyuan</term> <note>The theoretical starting point of the current cosmic cycle in Chinese calendrical science, beginning with a Jia-Zi year.</note> to the current first year of the **Jingyou era** <note>1035 CE</note> of the Great Song Dynasty, a *Jia-Xu* year, the total is 10,154,950 years.

## Chapter 82: The Celestial Emperor Taiyi

The Classic states: The **Celestial Emperor Taiyi** <term>Tianhuang Taiyi</term> resides within the **Purple Forbidden Enclosure** <term>Ziwei Yuan</term> <note>The central region of the night sky in Chinese astronomy, representing the celestial palace of the Emperor.</note>, specifically the star within the mouth of the **Curved Array** <term>Gouchen</term> <note>An asterism within Ursa Minor; in this context, it refers to the North Star or a nearby significant celestial marker.</note>. 

The eulogy says: The Great Emperor of the Celestial Emperor is the deity of the East.

---

->[Illustration]<-

<note>A diagram or celestial map (implied) depicting the relative positions of the Celestial Emperor star and the Taiyi within the Eastern, Western, Southern, and Northern quadrants of the heavens.</note>

When the Celestial Emperor and the Taiyi align in the **East**: there will be solar halos and great winds. 

When they align in the **West**: there will be lunar halos and cloudy vapors. 

When they align in the **South**: [The outcome is determined by the specific essence present].

The **Great Stay** <term>Daliu</term> <note>One of the "Ten Essences" or spiritual energies used in this system to modify a reading.</note> aligning in the **North** indicates the **Yin Node** <term>Yinjie</term> <note>A specific seasonal or ritual marker associated with the cooling, dark, or receptive principle.</note>.

Conjunction with the **Heavenly Lord** <term>Tianzun</term> brings Great Yin; the sun and moon will undergo changes <note>original: "日月有變" — this often refers to eclipses or unusual colorations.</note>.

Conjunction with the **Flying Bird** <term>Feiniao</term> brings Lesser Yin and rain.

Conjunction with **Heavenly Season** <term>Tianshi</term> brings Lesser Yin.

Conjunction with the **Five Winds** <term>Wufeng</term> causes gale winds to arise.

Conjunction with the **Taiyi Number** <term>Taiyi Shu</term> indicates great wind and rain.

Take the accumulated years from the start of the era to the desired year and divide them by the **Celestial Emperor's Small Cycle** <term>Tianhuang Xiaozhou Fa</term> of twenty years. ## Chapter 83: The Earthly Sign Taiyi

The Classic states: The **Earthly Sign Taiyi** <term>Difu Taiyi</term> is the envoy of the **Celestial Tally** <term>Tianjie</term>. 

The *Star Eulogy* says: The Celestial Tally serves as an envoy, responding with specialized purpose and leaving no room for doubt. 

If the Earthly Sign aligns with the **Taiyi** <note>The primary "Great One" star or calculation point.</note>, there will be solar halos and great winds. If they align within a **King or Minister position** <term>Wangxiang</term> <note>Terms referring to favorable seasonal strengths of the Five Elements; for example, Wood is "King" in Spring and "Minister" in Winter.</note>, there will be light rain and light overcast skies, but fierce winds will rise suddenly. 

If it aligns with the **Celestial Eye** <term>Tianmu</term> <note>Another critical calculated point in the Taiyi system, often associated with the "civilian" or "calculating" aspect of the divination.</note>, there will be light overcast and swift winds, or perhaps anomalous changes in the sun and moon.

Take the accumulated years to the desired year and divide them by the **Earthly Sign's Small Cycle** of twenty. For any remainder, begin counting from the **Yin Lord** <term>Yinzhu</term> and proceed clockwise through the Sixteen Gods until you reach the **Earthly Lord**, **High Thicket**, **Great Majesty**, and **Great Crowd**. When the count is exhausted, the final position marks the location of the **Earthly Sign Taiyi**.

## Chapter 85: The Flying Bird Taiyi

The Classic states: The **Flying Bird Taiyi** <term>Feiniao Taiyi</term> is the envoy of the **Seven Stars** <note>Refers to the stars of the Big Dipper.</note> and is the essence of the **Vermilion Bird** <note>The constellation of the Southern Palace, associated with the element of fire.</note>. 

The *Star Eulogy* says: The deity of the Flying Bird is the **essence** <gloss>essence</gloss> within the Big Dipper.

If the Flying Bird aligns with the **Taiyi** while in a **King or Minister position** <term>Wangxiang</term> <note>These are seasonal strengths; for instance, when an element is at its peak (King) or supported (Minister).</note>, it governs anomalies among the celestial stars or the rising of great winds. <gloss>If it aligns with the Celestial Season, there will be dark, cold winds.</gloss>

Take the accumulated years for the desired period and divide them by the **Flying Bird Small Cycle Method** <term>Feiniao Xiaozhou Fa</term> of nine. For any remainder, begin counting from Palace 1 and proceed clockwise through the **Nine Palaces** <note>The standard 3x3 divinatory grid.</note>. When the count of the remainder is exhausted, one obtains the location of the **Flying Bird Taiyi**.

## Chapter 86: The Heavenly Sovereign Taiyi

The Classic states: The **Heavenly Sovereign Taiyi** <term>Tianzun Taiyi</term> is the leader of the **Yellow Stars** <note>Stars associated with the center and the Earth element in traditional star mapping.</note>. 

The *Star Eulogy* says: The single star of the Great Sovereign possesses a majesty comparable to that of the **Sacred Duke** <note>A high-ranking celestial title used in Song dynasty state religion and Daoist liturgy.</note>.

If the Heavenly Sovereign aligns with the Taiyi in a **King or Minister position**, there will be heavy overcast skies, rain, and cold. If they align in **Palace 8** <note>The Gen Palace, located in the Northeast.</note>, there will be solar halos. If they align in **Palace 2** <note>The Kun Palace, located in the Southwest.</note>, there will be great overcast skies and lingering cold. If they align in **Palace 4** <note>The Xun Palace, located in the Southeast.</note>, there will be solar halos.

The *Star Eulogy* <note>original: 星读 (Xingdu)</note> says: Its transformations are like the resonance between a form and its shadow, or a sound and its echo. | | When the count of the remainder is exhausted, one obtains the location of the **Heavenly Sovereign Taiyi**. | Take the accumulated years for the desired period and divide them by the **Heavenly Sovereign Small Cycle Method** <term>Tianzun Xiaozhou Fa</term> of four. For any remainder, begin counting from **Palace 8** and proceed in **reverse** through the **Four Cardinal Palaces**. | Aligned with the **Heavenly Eye**, the sky will have overcast skies and rain. | Aligned with the **Flying Bird**, the weather will be warm with light rain. |

### 88. The Eight Winds Taiyi

The Classic states: The **Eight Winds Taiyi** <term>Bafeng Taiyi</term> is the envoy of the **Net mansion** <note>original: 畢星 (Bixing). The Net is a lunar mansion associated with the constellation Taurus; traditionally, it was believed to govern rain and wind.</note>. The *Star Eulogy* says: The Net consists of eight stars, and thus there are eight winds.

When the **Eight Winds** aligns with the **Taiyi** while in a **King or Minister position** <term>Wangxiang</term> <note>These terms refer to the seasonal strength of elements; a "King" position is where an element is at its seasonal peak, and "Minister" is its supportive phase.</note>, clouds will rise and there will be light rain.

If it aligns with the **Five Winds**, it governs great winds. If it aligns with **Heaven’s Time** <term>Tianshi</term>, there will be overcast skies for a ten-day period <term>xun</term>, and the sun and moon will undergo changes <note>Historical astronomers used "changes" to refer to abnormal colors, halos, or eclipses.</note>.

If it aligns with the **Earthly Tally** <term>Difu</term>, there will be overcast rain.

If it aligns with the **Heavenly Sovereign** <term>Tianzun</term>, there will be overcast gales, and the sun and moon will undergo changes.

If it aligns with the **Taiyi**: in a **Yin Palace**, there will be rain; in a **Yang Palace**, there will be wind.

To find its location: take the accumulated years for the desired period and divide them by the **Eight Winds Small Cycle Method** <term>Bafeng Xiaozhou Fa</term> of nine. For any remainder, begin counting from **Palace 2** and proceed in **forward order** through the nine palaces. When the count is exhausted, one obtains the location of the **Eight Winds Taiyi**.

### 89. The Five Winds Taiyi

The Classic states: The **Five Winds Taiyi** <term>Wufeng Taiyi</term> is the envoy of the **Winnowing Basket mansion** <note>original: 箕星 (Jixing). The Winnowing Basket is a lunar mansion in the constellation Sagittarius, traditionally associated with the wind.</note>. The *Star Eulogy* says: The Basket consists of five stars, and thus it is called the Five Winds.

When the **Five Winds** aligns with the **Taiyi** while in a **King or Minister position**, the sun and moon will undergo changes, continuous overcast will hide the heavens and the sun, and violent gales and driving rain will occur together.

When it aligns with the **Heavenly Venerable** <term>Tianzun</term>, there is slight overcast, and the sun and moon undergo changes <note>In traditional Chinese astronomy, "changes" (bian) refers to celestial anomalies such as solar halos, unusual coloring, or eclipses.</note>.
When it aligns with the **Flying Bird** <term>Feiniao</term>, strong winds will rise.

When it aligns with **Heavenly Time** <term>Tianshi</term>, there will be great winds.

When it aligns with the **Heavenly Eye** <term>Tianmu</term>, there will be heavy overcast, light wind, and the sun and moon will undergo changes.

For any remainder, count starting from Palace 1, then proceed through Palaces 3, 5, 7, 9, 2, 4, 6, and 8. The palace reached at the end of the count <note>original: 算外 (suanwai), referring to the final position after the calculation is exhausted.</note> is where the **Five Winds Taiyi** resides.

## 90. The Three Winds Taiyi <gloss>Part Two</gloss>

The Classic states: The **Three Winds Taiyi** <term>Sanfeng Taiyi</term> is the envoy of the **Heart mansion** <note>original: 心宿 (Xinxiu). The Heart is one of the twenty-eight lunar mansions, located in the center of the Azure Dragon of the East; it corresponds to the constellation Scorpius.</note>. The *Star Eulogy* says: The Heart consists of three stars, and thus there are "Three Winds."

When the **Three Winds** aligns with the **Taiyi** while in a **King or Minister position** <term>Wangxiang</term> <note>These terms describe the seasonal strength of an elemental spirit; "King" is the peak of its power, and "Minister" is its supportive phase.</note>, the sun and moon will lose their luster and cold clouds will rise on all sides.

When it aligns with **Heavenly Time** <term>Tianshi</term>, there will be slight overcast and wind.
When it aligns with the **Flying Bird** <term>Feiniao</term>, there will be swift winds, overcast clouds, and the sun and moon will undergo changes.

When it aligns with the **Heavenly Venerable** <term>Tianzun</term>, there will be slight overcast and wind.
When it aligns with the **Earthly Tally** <term>Difu</term>, there will be slight overcast.

When it aligns with the **Five Winds** <term>Wufeng</term>, the sun and moon will undergo changes and there will be slight overcast.
When it aligns with the **Heavenly Sovereign** <term>Tianhuang</term>, there will be warm weather, slight overcast, wind, and the sun and moon will undergo changes.

When it aligns with the **Heavenly Eye** <term>Tianmu</term>, there will be scattered overcast with wind and rain.

### 91. The Taiyi Number

The Classic states: The **Taiyi Number** <term>Taiyi Shu</term> refers to the calculation of the seventy-two configurations within the **Five Cycles of the Child** <term>Wuziyuan</term> <note>These are cycles of sixty years each, named after the 'zi' or 'Child' branch, used as chronological markers in Taiyi divination.</note>.

When it aligns with **Taiyi**, there will be a solar halo <note>original: 日暈 (riyun), an atmospheric phenomenon where a circle of light appears around the sun, often interpreted as an omen of change or wind.</note> and great winds.
When it is in opposition to **Taiyi**, a solar halo appears and winds will rise.

If the calculated number is thirty, there will be a solar halo and great winds.
If the number is forty, there will be overcast rain and yellow fog.
If the number is fifty, it aligns with the **Heavenly Eye** <term>Tianmu</term>.
If it resides in a **Prosperous or Ministering position** <term>Wangxiang zhi di</term> <note>Refers to the seasonal strength of the elements; 'Prosperous' is the peak of an element's power, and 'Ministering' is its supportive phase.</note>, a solar halo will appear.

When the Number and **Taiyi** flank the **Heavenly Eye**, there will be overcast rain and a solar halo.
When the Number joins with **Heaven** or **Earth**, there will be a solar halo. (Note: Heaven corresponds to the number ten and Earth to the number five).
When it aligns with the **Calculating Eye** <term>Jimu</term>, there will be a solar halo.
When it corresponds to **Heaven** or **Earth**, there will be great winds.

When it aligns with the **Taiyi Flying Bird** <term>Taiyi Feiniao</term>, there will be gale-force winds <term>jifeng</term>.

To find the location: take the accumulated years for the desired period and divide them by the **Taiyi Configuration Method** <term>Taiyi Jufa</term> of seventy-two. For any remainder, count starting from one. The position reached at the end of the count is where the **Taiyi Number** resides.

In former times, various worthies discussed the **Ten Essences** <term>Shi Jing</term> <note>A group of ten specific celestial spirits or "stars" in Taiyi divination, such as the Heaven Talisman and Earth Talisman, used to predict earthly events.</note> of the **Great One** <term>Taiyi</term> regarding the years, months, days, and hours in separate chapters. However, after investigating and calculating them, I, Weide, and my colleagues have now pruned the redundant details and extracted the core essentials. We have calculated the epochs and sought the origins, measuring the calendar numbers and using the accumulated counts of the years, months, days, and hours from the **Superior Epoch Jiazi** <term>Shangyuan Jiazi</term> <note>The theoretical starting point of the Chinese calendar where all cycles align.</note> using a unified method. For the **Yin Hidden** <term>Yin Dun</term> cycle of the Ten Essences, one simply looks at the position directly opposite the **Yang Hidden** <term>Yang Dun</term> <note>These terms refers to the two primary modes of the Taiyi cosmic compass; Yang corresponds to the period of increasing light, and Yin to increasing darkness.</note> to find where the Yin essence resides.

## 92. Calculating the Omens of the Ten Essences Taiyi Cloud Vapors

One should always observe the day when the **Great One** <term>Taiyi</term> moves to a new palace. Watch the sun at sunrise, at noon, and during the **late afternoon** <term>bushi</term> <note>Specifically the period from 3:00 PM to 5:00 PM.</note> to observe the wind and cloud vapors beside the sun; do not use the remaining two days of the cycle. When the hour-count is harmonious and the palace movement is calculated, do not perform divination during the remaining two hours. One should observe the areas above, below, left, and right of the sun.

If the color of the cloud vapor is **azure** <note>original: 青 (qing), a color spanning blue and green, associated with the East and the element Wood.</note> and the day-hour calculation results in three or four, then the change will manifest during the **Yin or Mao hours** <note>3:00 AM to 7:00 AM.</note>. 

If the color of the cloud vapor is **red** and the day-hour calculation results in nine or two, then the change will manifest during the **Si or Wu hours** <note>9:00 AM to 1:00 PM.</note>.

If the color of the cloud vapor is **white** and the day-hour calculation results in seven or six, then the change will manifest during the **Shen or You hours** <note>3:00 PM to 7:00 PM.</note>. 

If the color of the cloud vapor is **black** and the day-hour calculation results in eight or one, then the change will manifest during the **Hai or Zi hours** <note>9:00 PM to 1:00 AM.</note>.

> The Classic states: When the **Earth Talisman** <term>Difu</term>, **Heaven Sovereign** <term>Tianzun</term>, **Heaven Talisman** <term>Tianfu</term>, **Five Elements** <term>Wuxing</term>, **Eight Winds** <term>Bafeng</term>, **Five Winds** <term>Wufeng</term>, or **Three Winds** <term>Sanfeng</term> align with the **Great One** <term>Taiyi</term> in a **Yang Palace**, it indicates clear skies or drought. If they align in a **Yin Palace**, it indicates overcast skies or rain. Alignment with Yang occurs in Yang positions; alignment with Yin occurs in Yin positions. When the **Heavenly Gaze** <term>Tianwang</term> aligns with the Great One in a Yang Palace, there will be a solar halo and darkness during the day; if they align in a Yin Palace, there will be a lunar halo and gloom at night. When the **Flying Bird** <term>Feiniao</term> aligns with the Great One, there will be wind.

...and the atmospheric vapors. From that point on, if the celestial energy is pure and thick, then it will rain; if the celestial energy is thin and sparse, there will be wind. If the celestial energy appears as a yellow mist, there will be a solar halo <note>original: "日暈" (riyun) - an optical phenomenon where a circle of light surrounds the sun, often interpreted as an omen of change.</note>. If the celestial energy is black and red, there will be wind. If the celestial energy is blue-white, it will be cold. If it is condensed and moist, it will be mist or rain. 

When cloud vapors look as if they have been swept clean, the sky will be clear. If the cloud colors are variegated, swirling, or sparse, the sky will be very clear. If there is a great drought, one should always use **Yang** <term>Yang</term> <note>The active, solar principle. In divination, this refers to using calculations or observations associated with heat and light.</note> to divine the outcome. If it is raining, one should always use **Yin** <term>Yin</term> <note>The receptive, lunar principle associated with moisture and darkness.</note> to divine it. If the energies attain the **Prosperous or Ministering** <term>Wang Xiang</term> <note>Technical terms from the Five Elements theory: "Prosperous" (Wang) is when an element is at its seasonal peak, and "Ministering" (Xiang) is when an element is being produced by the current season's element.</note> states, then the transformations will occur rapidly as the cycles move.

->93. Calculating the Disasters of the Great One’s Yang Nine<-

The Classic says: The **Great One** <term>Taiyi</term> has disasters known as the **Yang Nine** <term>Yang Jiu</term> <note>A traditional Chinese numerological concept referring to a major cycle of cosmic misfortune, often associated with drought or social upheaval.</note>. A "Yang Nine" period consists of four hundred and fifty-six years. Ten such periods, totaling four thousand five hundred and sixty years, constitute one **Cycle** <term>Yuan</term>, during which disasters occur.

To calculate this, take the accumulated years from the **Superior Epoch Jiayin** <term>Shangyuan Jiayin</term> <note>The theoretical starting point of the calendar used in the "Yanji" (Extended Era) astronomical system, when all celestial cycles are said to align.</note> of the **Extended Era** <term>Yanji</term> until the first year of the **Jingyou era** <term>Jingyou</term> <note>1034-1035 AD. Though the text mentions "Taizong," Jingyou was an era name of Emperor Renzong; this may refer to a dynastic record-keeping style.</note> (a **Jiaxu** year). The accumulated count is 424,040 years. 

To investigate the past, subtract one year for each year gone by; to verify the future, add one year for each year to come. Divide this total by the **Great Limit** <term>Daxian</term> number of 4,560 (representing the full Cycle). With the remainder, divide it by the **Small Limit** <term>Xiaoxian</term> number of 456. This determines the current number of "Yang Nine" periods passed. Any remaining years that do not complete a full count are designated as the specific year within the current Yang Nine cycle.

->94. Calculating the Calamities of the Great One’s One-Hundred-Six<-

> The Classic says: The **Great One** <term>Taiyi</term> <note>A supreme celestial deity and the name of a sophisticated system of divination used for predicting state affairs and natural disasters.</note> possesses the **Disasters of the One-Hundred-Six** <term>Bailiu zhi e</term> <note>A traditional Chinese chronological concept of a major period of cosmic upheaval, often associated with floods or social chaos.</note>. The One-Hundred-Six consists of 4,320 years, which constitutes one **Great Cycle** <term>Yuan</term>. Using 288 years as a sub-cycle, it rotates and begins again. If one enters the beginning of a Great Cycle or reaches the end of its count, the disasters are especially severe.

To calculate this, take the number of years accumulated from the "Superior Origin Jia-Yin" <note>The fixed chronological starting point for this system of divination, occurring on a Jia-Yin year—the 51st year of the 60-year sexagenary cycle.</note> of the Epochal Calculation to the year being queried; this uses the same base number as the **Yang Nine** <term>Yang Jiu</term> <note>Another major disaster cycle, traditionally consisting of 4,617 years, often paired with the One-Hundred-Six cycle.</note> calculation. Divide this by the Major Limit Great Cycle number of 4,320. With the remainder that cannot be fully divided, divide it again by the Minor Limit number of 288. This result provides the count of "One-Hundred-Six" sub-cycles. Any years remaining that do not fill a full count <note>original: "筭外" (suanwai) - a technical term for the remainder in a division operation, literally "that which falls outside the calculation."</note> represent the specific year within the current One-Hundred-Six cycle; designate the year by this remainder.

# Volume 8 of the Classic of the Great One’s Blessed Response
## Section 95: The Twelve Astral Fields

In the beginning of the Primordial Era <term>Yuanshi</term> <note>The earliest stage of the universe's creation in Daoist cosmology, preceding physical form.</note>, there were no images, and the Great Element <term>Taisu</term> <note>The state where primal matter begins to take shape but is not yet solid.</note> flowed into various forms. Aligned with the Heavens above to serve as the Primal Record, the chronicles say that in ancient times men lived in caves during winter and in nests during summer. Palaces and houses did not yet exist; they ate raw meat and drank blood, for silk and hemp had not yet been discovered. 

When Suiren <note>A mythological figure credited with teaching humans how to create fire by drilling wood.</note> produced fire and Fuxi <note>The first of the Three Sovereigns, credited with creating the Eight Trigrams and teaching fishing and animal husbandry.</note> emerged from the "Resonance of the Wind," the Red Summer <note>A poetic term for ancient China.</note> began its martial lineage and the foundations of prosperity were laid. Across the boundless wilderness, these principles were passed down to the Yellow Emperor. He then ascended to the south of the Yellow River and the north of the Yangtze, climbed into the emptiness and trod upon Mount Tai. He reached as far as the peaks of Kunlun, shaking the phoenix and coiling the wind <note>Metaphorical language for his majestic and spiritual presence.</note>, exploring deep mountains to seek the Dao. These events are preserved in the bamboo and silk records and cannot be dismissed as false.

Emperor Gaoyang <note>Also known as Zhuanxu, a grandson of the Yellow Emperor.</note> took office and relied on the spirits. Emperor Ku followed the Heavens and walked the paths of the sun and moon. Their influence reached the Coiled Wood in the east, the Flowing Sands in the west, the Dark Tombs <term>Youling</term> in the north, and Jiaozhi <note>An ancient region in what is now northern Vietnam.</note> in the south. Wherever the sun and moon shone and wherever ships and carts could reach, all were the King's servants and did not stray from these domains. 

In the time of Emperor Yao, the Great Yu calmed the waters and soil, dividing the land into the Nine Provinces <term>Jiuzhou</term> <note>The traditional geographic divisions of ancient China.</note>. When Emperor Shun took the throne, his achievements were even more magnificent. He raised the standards of classification to divide the universe, distinguishing the mountains and rivers to verify the boundaries. He established the land systems, merging the names of capitals. Starting with the surveys of the Dark Palace <note>Likely referring to astronomical observations for territorial alignment.</note>, the old divisions were expanded to create the Twelve Provinces and the Twelve Sacred Mountains. 

The achievements of the Xia Dynasty were based on those of the era of Yao. The Yin <note>The Shang Dynasty.</note> also made no significant additions or subtractions. When King Wu of the Zhou defeated the Shang, he moved from Feng to Hao. By the time of King Cheng, the "Tribute of Yu" <note>original: "禹貢" (Yu Gong), a chapter in the *Book of Documents* detailing the geography of ancient China.</note> was revised. The regions of Liang and Xu were incorporated into Qing, Yong, and Ji, while the wilderness was divided into You and Bing. The Director of Land and Maps <term>Zhifang</term> <note>A high official in the Zhou bureaucracy responsible for maps and censuses.</note> took charge of the governance of the realm to distinguish its provinces, while the Preserver of Regulations <term>Baozhang</term> <note>An official astronomer/astrologer responsible for watching the stars to predict the fortunes of the provinces.</note> differentiated the Twelve Astral Fields of the Nine Provinces...

<summary>This page introduces Volume 8 by transitioning from cosmological time to sacred geography. It traces the history of China's territorial divisions from mythical origins and the Yellow Emperor down to the administrative offices of the Zhou Dynasty.</summary>
<keywords>Twelve Astral Fields, Yellow Emperor, Nine Provinces, Great Yu, Tribute of Yu, Zhifang, Baozhang, Sacred Geography</keywords>

This serves as a guide for how the Great One (Taiyi) movements correlate with specific earthly provinces.</meta>

...and the wilderness regions all possess their respective astral divisions <term>Xingfen</term> <note>The "Field Division" system, which mapped specific constellations in the sky to corresponding geographical regions on Earth, allowing for omens in the sky to be localized to specific provinces.</note>.

### Palace 1: Jizhou

Jizhou, according to the *Tribute of Yu* <note>An ancient geographic survey from the *Book of Documents*.</note> and the *Rites of Zhou*, consists of the lands within the River <note>The Yellow River.</note>. When Emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures <term>Shi’er Mu</term> <note>The administrative divisions of ancient China under the legendary Emperor Shun.</note>, this was the first among them. The *Spring and Autumn Annals: Primal Command of the Chart* <note>An apocryphal text from the Han dynasty.</note> states that the constellation Mao <gloss>The Pleiades; sometimes written with the alternative character "Ang"</gloss> scattered its essence to become Jizhou <gloss>Some sources say Bing Province</gloss>; its terrestrial division is the State of Zhao. 

Its terrain contains both treacherous passes and easy plains, and it has served as the capital for emperors and kings. In times of disorder, Ji Province provides stability; in times of weakness, Ji Province provides strength; in times of famine, Ji Province provides abundance. The province is vast from north to south. Its western division at Wei became Bing Province, and the area north of Yan became You Province. The people of the Zhou Dynasty followed these divisions.

|---|---|---|---|
| Jizhou | Jinzhou <gloss>located in Ping</gloss> | Weizhou | Tanzhou |
| Xingzhou | Jinzhou <note>original: "晋州", a variant or distinct administrative seat from the "晉州" listed above</note> | Zhaozhou | Yingzhou |

### Palace 2: Jingzhou

Jingzhou, according to the *Tribute of Yu*, is the land south of Mount Heng <term>Hengyang</term>. When Emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures, this was one of them. The *Rites of Zhou* states: "The region directly to the south is called Jing."

## Jingzhou

The *Spring and Autumn Annals: Primal Command of the Chart* states that the "Tail of the Quail" <note>The "Tail of the Quail" (Chunwei) is one of the twelve Chinese astral stations, roughly corresponding to the sign of Virgo in Western astrology.</note> scattered its essence to become Jingzhou. *Jing* means "strong"; <note>The author uses a homophone or semantic link between 荆 (Jing) and 強 (Qiang/Strong).</note> it is said that its *Qi* <term>Qi</term> <note>The vital energy or "breath" of a region.</note> is dry and strong. It is also called "Startled" <gloss>meaning</gloss> <note>original: "驚" (Jing), a homophone for the province name.</note> because its population is abundant and it serves as a leader <gloss>a mark</gloss>. Its people possess the Way <note>The *Dao* or moral path.</note>; therefore, those who do not follow the Way will eventually submit, while those who are strong remain constantly vigilant. It also takes its name from Mount Jing. During the Period of the Six Kingdoms <note>The Warring States Period (475–221 BCE).</note>, it was the State of Chu.

## The Third Palace: Qingzhou

According to the *Tribute of Yu*, Qingzhou comprises the lands between the Sea and Mount Dai <note>Mount Dai is an ancient name for Mount Tai, the most sacred of the Five Great Mountains.</note>. When Emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures, this was one of them. <gloss>Because Qingzhou crossed the sea and was further divided</gloss> it became Yingzhou; thus, Liaodong was originally part of Qingzhou. The *Rites of Zhou* states that the "True East" is called Qingzhou. Because the land resides in the position of Lesser Yang <term>Shao Yang</term> <note>In the system of the Five Elements, the East corresponds to "Lesser Yang," the season of Spring, and the color green/azure.</note>, its color is azure-green; therefore, it is called Qingzhou <note>Qing (青) can mean green, blue, or azure.</note>. The *Spring and Autumn Annals: Primal Command of the Chart* states that the constellations Void and Rooftop <note>The constellations Xu (Void) and Wei (Rooftop) are part of the Northern Palace (the Black Tortoise) in Chinese uranography.</note> flowed down to become Qingzhou.

## The Sixth Palace: Yongzhou

<gloss>Regarding the location of Yongzhou:</gloss> According to the "Tribute of Yu" <note>The *Yu Gong*, a chapter in the *Book of Documents* detailing the geography of ancient China.</note>, Yongzhou covers the lands of the Black Water and the Western River. When the sage-emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures <note>The administrative divisions of the legendary era.</note>, this was one of them. It contains the mountains of Liangzhou <gloss>the Liangzhou mountains</gloss> which encircle the gardens; thus, it was named "Yong" <note>original: "雍" (Yong).</note>. Being situated in the northwest, it is a place where the *Yang* energy <term>Yang</term> <note>The active, bright, and masculine principle in Chinese cosmology.</note> cannot reach, and thus the *Yin* energy <term>Yin</term> <note>The passive, dark, and feminine principle.</note> is "congested" or "blocked" <note>The author provides an etymological pun: "Yong" (雍) is synonymous with "Yong" (壅), meaning obstructed or blocked.</note>. During the Zhou dynasty, this land was granted to Duke Xiang and became the territory of the State of Qin.

<column-break/>

## The Fifth Palace: Yuzhou

<gloss>Regarding the location of Yuzhou:</gloss> According to the "Tribute of Yu," this was originally the land of Jingzhou. When Shun established the Twelve Pastures, this was one of them. *The Rites of Zhou* states that the land south of the Yellow River is called Yuzhou. "Yu" means "relaxed" or "at ease" <note>original: "舒" (Shu).</note>; it is imbued with the *Qi* <term>Qi</term> <note>Vital energy or life force.</note> of central harmony. The *Spring and Autumn Annals* states that the "Junqian" stars <note>Part of the "Room" (Fang) mansion in the Chinese zodiac, corresponding to stars in the constellation Scorpio.</note> mark the boundaries of Yuzhou. Its territory stretches from the east of Mount Hua to the Huai River and the sea; to the south, it borders Jingzhou. When the Qin dynasty unified the world, they established it as a formal province.

### Liangzhou

According to the "Tribute of Yu" <note>The *Yu Gong*, an ancient geographical treatise within the *Book of Documents*.</note>, Liangzhou consists of the lands between Huayang <note>The region "south of Mount Hua."</note> and the Black Water. When the sage-emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures <note>The legendary administrative divisions of ancient China.</note>, this was one of them. It is said that the *Qi* <term>Qi</term> <note>The vital energy or breath of the universe.</note> of Western Metal is firm and strong <gloss>its strength</gloss>; thus, the region was named for this quality <note>The author links the name "Liang" (梁), meaning a strong beam or ridge, to the "strong" (强) nature of the element Metal, which corresponds to the West in Five Elements theory.</note>. In the thirty-sixth year of the First Emperor of Qin, the empire was divided <gloss>middle</gloss>, and the lands of Yizhou were incorporated into Liangzhou at Hanzhong.

### The Eighth Palace: Yanzhou

According to the "Tribute of Yu" <note>The *Yu Gong*, a classic geographical text.</note>, Yanzhou consists of the lands between the Ji and Yellow Rivers. When the sage-emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures <note>The legendary administrative divisions of ancient China.</note>, this was one of them. The *Rites of Zhou* <note>An ancient manual of organizational theory.</note> refers to this region as "Hezhong" <note>Literally "Between the Rivers."</note>. The *Spring and Autumn: Primordial Mandate of Fate* <note>The *Chunqiu Yuan Ming Bao*, a divination-focused commentary on the *Spring and Autumn Annals*.</note> states that the flowing energy of the Five Stars <note>The five visible planets of traditional Chinese astronomy: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> transformed to become the essence of Yanzhou. The name signifies "uprightness" and "trustworthiness."

### The Ninth Palace: Yangzhou

According to the "Tribute of Yu," Yangzhou consists of the lands between the Huai River and the sea. When the sage-emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures, this was one of them. The *Rites of Zhou* identifies the southeast as Yangzhou. <gloss>The regions of Huai and Wu</gloss>

The *Spring and Autumn: Primordial Bud* <note>The *Chunqiu Yuan Bao Bao*, another apocryphal commentary.</note> says that the flow from the "Heart of the Flower" <note>The "Hua" or central flowering of the cosmos.</note> became Yangzhou, which was later divided into the State of Yue. It is believed that the *Qi* <term>Qi</term> <note>The vital energy or breath of the universe.</note> of the Jiangnan region <note>The lands "South of the Yangtze River."</note> is impetuous and vigorous, yet its nature remains light.

It is further said that because the borders of this province contain many waters and the waves surge <note>The name "Yang" (揚) means to toss, surge, or wave.</note> across the land, it was given this name. In ancient times, it was a desolate and overgrown kingdom. During the Warring States period, it was a division of the State of Chu. When the First Emperor of Qin unified the world, he established the regional seat at Kuaiji. The area south of the Yangtze and Han rivers is called Huainan <note>South of the Huai River.</note>; it was here that the general Ying Bu was enfeoffed as the King of Huainan.

## The Crimson Palace: Jiaozhou

According to the "Tribute of Yu" <note>original: *Yu Gong*; a classic geographical work attributed to the legendary Great Yu.</note> <gloss>located in the state of Wu</gloss>, this was within the region of Yangzhou and is the land of the Southern Yue <note>original: *Nanyue*; an ancient kingdom covering parts of modern Guangdong, Guangxi, and northern Vietnam.</note>. When the sage-emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures <note>The legendary administrative divisions of ancient China.</note>, this was one of them. The First Emperor of Qin <note>Qin Shi Huang, the first unifier of China.</note> pacified the Yangzhou and Yue regions, dispatching garrison troops to guard the Five Ridges. Through military campaigns, he seized the "unruly lands" <note>original: *Luliang*; a term used in the Qin dynasty to describe the rugged, non-integrated tribal territories of the south.</note> and subsequently established the commanderies of Guilin, Nanhai, and Xiang in the Southern Yue territory.

Hepu <gloss>the name for</gloss> Jiaozhi <note>A major Han dynasty commandery in what is now northern Vietnam.</note>, Xinchang, Wuning, Jiuzhen, Jiude, Nanhai, and Xianglin <gloss>old names</gloss>.

## The Hall of Light: Yizhou

<gloss>In ancient times</gloss>, according to the "Tribute of Yu," this was the territory of Liangzhou. When the sage-emperor Shun established the Twelve Pastures, it was one of them. The Zhou dynasty merged Liang into <gloss>referred to as Yong</gloss> province. The *Spring and Autumn: Primordial Mandate of Fate* <note>original: *Chunqiu Yuan Ming Bao*; an apocryphal text that links terrestrial geography to celestial phenomena.</note> says: "The flow of the constellation Shen <note>original: *Can*; the constellation Orion, associated with the western quadrant of the sky.</note> became Yizhou." The word *Yi* <note>meaning "increase" or "benefit"</note> means "a strategic constriction." Its terrain is steep and rugged. It is also said that its borders and territory "increased greatly," hence the name. Among the names of the thirty-six commanderies established by the First Emperor of Qin, the name of Chengdu was changed to Wenshan.
